<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{al.t}
Let $u\in W^{1,\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\Omega)$ be a weakly spacelike local minimizer of \eqref{bi.en}, where $\Omega\subset \mathbb R^n$ is an open subset of $\mathbb R^n$. Assume that
\eqn{mmmmm}
$$
\begin{cases}
\displaystyle
\ \mu\in L_{\operatorname{loc}}(n-1,s)(\Omega), \ \ s<\infty\quad &\mbox{if} \ \ n\ge 3\vspace{1.5mm}\\ 
\displaystyle
\ \mu\in L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\Omega)\quad &\mbox{if} \ \ n=2.
\end{cases}
$$
If there is a segment $\overline{x_{1}x_{0}}\Subset \Omega$ such that
\eqn{a.0}
 $$
 u(x_{t})=u(x_{0})+t\lvert x_{1}-x_{0}\rvert\qquad \mbox{for all} \ \ t\in [0,1],
 $$
with $x_{t}:=x_{0}+t(x_{1}-x_{0})$, then \eqref{a.0} holds for all $t\in \mathbb{R}$ such that $x_{t}\in \Omega$ and $\overline{x_{t}x_{0}}\subset \Omega$.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{minimilocali}
Let \(\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n\) be an open subset and let
\(\mu\in L^1_{\operatorname{loc}}(\Omega)\).
A weakly spacelike function
\(u\in W^{1,\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\Omega)\)
is called a \emph{local minimizer} of \(\mathcal E_\mu\) if, for every bounded open set
\(U\Subset\Omega\) and every weakly spacelike
\(v\in W^{1,\infty}(U)\) such that
$
\,{\rm supp }(v-u)\Subset U,
$
one has
$
\mathcal E_\mu(u;U)
\le
\mathcal E_\mu(v;U).
$
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{light.t}
Let $n\ge2$, let $\mu\in\mathcal D^{1,2}(\mathbb R^n)^*$ satisfy\footnote{The space $L^1_0(\mathbb R^2)$ consists of  $L^1(\mathbb R^2)$-functions with zero global average, while $\mathcal D^{1,2}(\mathbb R^n)$ is the classical Deny-Lions space; see (\ref{zeromean}). See Section \ref{spazifunzionali} below for the details.}
\[
\begin{cases}
\mu\in L^1_0(\mathbb R^2),
& n=2,\\[1mm]
\mu\in L_{\operatorname{loc}}(n-1,s)(\mathbb R^n)
\quad\text{for some }0<s<\infty,
& n\ge3,
\end{cases}
\]
and let $u\in\mathbb X(\mathbb R^n)$\footnote{The space $\mathbb X(\mathbb R^n)$ is defined in (\ref{XXX}).} be the unique
minimizer of $\mathcal E_\mu$.
Then $u$ has no light segments, and, if $\mu\in L^2_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n)$, then
$u$ weakly solves \eqref{bi}.
In particular, this additional assumption is automatically
satisfied when $n\ge4$,  and  when $n=3$ with $s\le2$.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{ex.t}
Let $n\ge3$. There exists $\mu\in\mathcal D^{1,2}(\mathbb R^n)^*$ satisfying
\[
\mu\in L(n-1,\infty)(\mathbb R^n)
\setminus L(n-1,s)(\mathbb R^n)
\qquad\mbox{for every }0<s<\infty,
\]
such that the unique minimizer
$u\in\mathbb X(\mathbb R^n)$ of $\mathcal E_\mu$
admits a light segment.
Moreover, $u$ is a weak solution to \eqref{bi}
in $\mathbb R^n$.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{b.th}
Let $n\ge 2$ and let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a bounded
domain and assume \eqref{u0u0.bd00}. Assume in addition
that
\[
\mu\in L_{\mathrm{loc}}(n-1,s)(\Omega)
\qquad\text{for some }0<s<\infty
\]
when $n\geq 3$. 
Let $u\in\mathcal D_0(\Omega)$ be the unique minimizer
of the Dirichlet problem \eqref{pd2}.
Then $u$ has no light segments in $\Omega$. Moreover, if $\mu\in L^2_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)$,
then $u$ weakly solves \eqref{bi} in $\Omega$. In particular, this additional assumption is automatically
satisfied when $n\ge4$, or when $n=3$ and $s\le2$.
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor.t}
Conjecture 1 in {\normalfont\cite{bimm24}} holds true, while the answer
to Question 2 in {\normalfont\cite{bimm24}} is negative (but, at the same time, almost positive).
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{t1}
Let $n\ge2$, let $2\le q\le n$, and let
$u\in\mathbb X(\mathbb R^n)$ be the minimizer of
\eqref{bi.en}, with
$
\mu\in\mathbb Y(\mathbb R^n)\cap L^q(\mathbb R^n).
$
Then $u\in W^{2,2}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n)$, it weakly solves
\eqref{bi} and it holds that 
\eqn{unaw}
$$
\mathcal h_u\in L^q_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n),
\qquad
\mathcal h_u^{-1},\log\mathcal h_u
\in W^{1,2}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n),
$$
with
\eqn{duaw}
$$
u\in L^\infty(\mathbb R^n) \ \mbox{if}\  n\ge3 \qquad \mbox{and} \qquad  \dfrac{u}{\log(e+|\cdot|)}
\in L^\infty(\mathbb R^2) \ \mbox{if}\  n=2
$$
and 
\begin{itemize}
\item[(I)]
If $2<q<n$, then
$$
\mathcal h_u^{(q-2)/2}
\in W^{1,2}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n).
$$

\item[(II)]
If $q=n\ge3$, then
$$
\mathcal h_u^{p/2}
\in W^{1,2}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n)
\qquad\mbox{for every }1\le p<\infty.
$$
\item[(III)]
If $n=2$, then
$$
\mathcal h_u\in L^p_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^2)
\qquad\mbox{for every }1\le p<\infty.
$$
\end{itemize}
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{sm.t2}
Let $n\ge4$, let
$\mu\in
\mathcal D^{1,2}(\mathbb R^n)^*
\cap L^q(\mathbb R^n)$ and $2\le q<n-1$, 
and let $u\in\mathbb X(\mathbb R^n)$ be the unique minimizer
of $\mathcal E_\mu$.
Then $u$ weakly solves \eqref{bi} and
$
\operatorname{Cap}_2(\mathcal S_u)
=
\operatorname{Cap}_2(\mathcal I_u)
=0.
$
Consequently,
$\dim_{\mathcal H}\mathcal S_u\le n-2$, 
$\dim_{\mathcal H}\mathcal I_u\le n-2$.
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{brutto}
Conjecture 4 in {\normalfont\cite{bimm24}}, asserting that $\dim_{\mathcal H} \mathcal K_u\leq n-q$ when 
$\mu \in L^q$, with $2\le q\le n-1$, is false
for $n\ge4$ and $2\le q<n-1$. Specifically, there exists a compactly supported $ \mu \in L(n-1,\infty)$ 
while $|\mathcal K_u|>0$. Simultaneously, Question 5 in {\normalfont\cite{bimm24}}, asserting that $\mathcal H^{n-1}(\mathcal K_u)=0$, has a
negative answer for every $n\ge3$, even for absolutely
continuous data $\mu$.
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
\label{sm.c}
Under the assumptions of Theorem \ref{sm.t2}, suppose that
there exists $\ell_0>0$ such that
$
\operatorname{diam}\, (\ell)\ge\ell_0$
for every light ray $\ell$.
Then
$
\mathcal K_u=\mathcal S_u$ and 
$
\mathcal L_u=\varnothing$. Moreover, 
$
\operatorname{Cap}_2(\mathcal K_u)=0$
and $
\dim_{\mathcal H}\mathcal K_u\le n-2.
$
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
\label{intre}
Let
\(u\in W^{1,\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n)\) be weakly spacelike and let $\mathcal S_{u}$ be the union of all light segments as defined in \eqref{language1}.
Assume that
$
\mathcal h_u^q\in L^1_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n)
$
for some \(1\le q<\infty\). Then
\eqn{nicchia4}
$$
\begin{cases}
\displaystyle
\mathcal H^{n- q/2}
\ (\mathcal S_{u})=0
&\ \mbox{if}\quad  1\le q<n-1\\[2mm]
\displaystyle\mathcal S_{u}=\mathcal{K}_{u}=\varnothing
&\ \mbox{if}\quad  q\ge n-1.
\end{cases}
$$
In particular, when $n=2$  and  $\mathcal{h}_{u}\in L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})$, it follows that  $\mathcal{S}_{\texttt{i},u}=\mathcal{S}_{u}=\mathcal{K}_{u}=\varnothing$.
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- theorem-8: body_md -->
\label{t2}
Let \(u \in \mathbb{X}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\), \(n \geq 3\), be the minimizer
of \eqref{bi.en} with $\mu\in \mathbb{Y}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap L(n,1).$ 
Then \(u\) weakly solves \eqref{bi} and\footnote{See (\ref{normalo}) and (\ref{normalo2}) for the definition of the Lorentz norm $[\mu]_{n,1}.$}
\eqn{boost.boundi}
$$
\left\lVert \mathcal{h}_u \right\rVert_{L^\infty(\mathbb{R}^n)} \leq 
\exp\left\{c_n[\mu]_{n,1}\right\}.
$$
Furthermore, \(Du\) is continuous in \(\mathbb{R}^{n}\).
<!-- end theorem-8 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
\label{geore}
\emph{Let us outline a few geometric consequences of estimate (\ref{boost.boundi}). 
\begin{itemize}
\item The exponential type estimate in (\ref{boost.boundi}) -- as well as the later ones in (\ref{boost.2di}) and (\ref{boost.bound.dir})-(\ref{boost.2d.dir}) -- calls for a parallel with the classical gradient estimate of Bombieri, De Giorgi and Miranda for the minimal surfaces \cite{bdgm69}. Note that the Born--Infeld and minimal surface integrands are Legendre
dual, up to an additive constant. We refer to the recent paper \cite{df26} for more in this direction. 
\item The classical theorems of Calabi--Bernstein \cite{cal70} and Cheng--Yau \cite{cy76} assert that every entire
spacelike maximal graph in Lorentz--Minkowski space is a
hyperplane. 
As the function class we are working with implies, 
$Du\in L^2(\mathbb R^n)$, we are a priori excluding tilted hyperplanes.
Nevertheless, estimate \eqref{boost.boundi} provides a
quantitative stability statement for such results within the present 
finite-energy class. Indeed, estimate (\ref{boost.boundi}) readily implies
$$
\lVert Du \rVert_{L^\infty(\mathbb R^n)}^2
\le
1-\exp\left\{-2c_n[\mu]_{n,1;\mathbb R^n}\right\}.
$$
Geometrically, if $\theta_u$ denotes the hyperbolic angle
between the future-directed unit normal to the graph
and the vertical direction, then
$\mathcal h_u=\cosh\theta_u$, and therefore
$$
\lVert \theta_u \rVert_{L^\infty(\mathbb R^n)}
\le
\operatorname{arcosh}\left(
\exp\left\{c_n[\mu]_{n,1;\mathbb R^n}\right\}
\right).
$$
Consequently, for assigned mean curvatures $\mu$ satisfying the hypotheses of
Theorem \ref{t2}, convergence to zero in $L(n,1)$ forces
the normals to converge uniformly to the vertical normal. Ultimately, Theorem \ref{t2} reaches the sharp Lorentz
threshold of elliptic gradient regularity, connecting it to
quantitative stability in the geometric setting.
\item The exclusion of light segments is a qualitative conclusion:
it does not by itself provide a uniform separation of $|Du|$
from the lightlike threshold. A bound for the boost function
gives precisely this quantitative information, since
$
\|\mathcal h_u\|_{L^\infty}\le M$ implies 
$|Du|\le\sqrt{1-M^{-2}}<1$, that is, the surface does not ``go null'', quantitatively.
Equivalently, since the induced metric is
$
g=\mathds I-Du\otimes Du,
$
a uniform bound for $\mathcal h_u$ prevents the metric from degenerating and keeps the tangent spaces uniformly separated from the light cone. 
\end{itemize}}
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\label{plapre}
{\em The space $L(n,1)$ provides the natural setting for the boundedness of the boost function once this result is read as a stress tensor intrinsic regularity result. The best comparison is with the $p$-Laplacean equation for which $-\,{\rm div} (|Du|^{p-2}Du) \in L(n,1)$ implies the continuity of $Du$ with optimal local a priori estimates on the vector field $|Du|^{p-2}Du$; see \cite{km14b} for a survey of such facts. In the present setting Theorem \ref{t2} reads exactly in the same way: $-\mathcal M u=-\,{\rm div}(\mathcal h_uDu)\in L(n,1)$ implies the continuity of $Du$ and a bound on the stress $\mathcal h_uDu$ (recall $|Du|\leq 1$). Note that Theorem \ref{t2} is in a sense a geometric version of a classical theorem of Stein claiming that $\Delta u\in L(n,1)$ implies the continuity of $Du$. }
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- theorem-9: body_md -->
\label{t222}
Let $u\in \mathbb{X}(\mathbb{R}^{2})$ be the minimizer of \eqref{bi.en} with 
$\mu\in \mathbb{Y}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap L^2(\log L)^\alpha(\mathbb R^2)$ where  $\alpha>2.$ 
Then $u$ solves \eqref{bi} and\footnote{See (\ref{luxi}) and (\ref{lllog}) for the definition of $\lVert \mu \rVert_{L^2(\log L)^\alpha(\mathbb R^2)}$.}
\eqn{boost.2di}
$$
\lVert \mathcal h_u \rVert_{L^\infty(\mathbb R^2)}
\lesssim_n
\left(1+  \left\lVert \mu \right\rVert_
    {\mathcal{D}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{*}}^{2}\right)\exp\left\{
c_{\alpha}
\lVert \mu \rVert_{L^2(\log L)^\alpha(\mathbb R^2)}
\right\}.
$$
Furthermore, $Du$ is continuous in $\mathbb{R}^{2}$.
<!-- end theorem-9 -->
<!-- theorem-10: body_md -->
\label{dir.borderline}
Assume \eqref{assuntidir0}.
For every choice of the constants $\Lambda_0,F_0$ in \eqref{data2}, and of the boundary layer width $r_0>0$, there exist constants
$
\varepsilon_\partial\in(0,1)$ and $
\mathcal h_{\partial\Omega}\ge2$, 
depending only on
$
n,\ r_0,\ \sigma_0,\ \Lambda_0,\ F_0,\
\|\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0\|_{C^2(\overline\Omega)},\ \Omega,
$
with the following property.
Let $u$ be the minimizer of the Dirichlet problem
\eqref{pd2in}, with boundary datum
$u_0=\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0|_{\partial\Omega}$ and  $\mu$
satisfying \eqref{data1}--\eqref{data2}.
\begin{itemize}
\item If $n\ge3$, then
\begin{equation}\label{boost.bound.dir}
\|\mathcal h_u\|_{L^\infty(\Omega)}
\le
\mathcal h_{\partial\Omega}
\exp\left\{c_n[\mu]_{n,1;\Omega}\right\}.
\end{equation}

\item If $n=2$ and, additionally,
$\mu\in L^2(\log L)^\alpha(\Omega)$ for some $\alpha>2$,
then
\begin{equation}\label{boost.2d.dir}
\|\mathcal h_u\|_{L^\infty(\Omega)}
\le
\mathcal h_{\partial\Omega}
\exp\left\{
c_{\alpha,|\Omega|}
\|\mu\|_{L^2(\log L)^\alpha(\Omega)}
\right\}.
\end{equation}
\end{itemize}
Here $c_n$ depends only on $n$, and
$c_{\alpha,|\Omega|}$ depends only on $\alpha$ and $|\Omega|$.
In both cases, $u$ weakly solves \eqref{bi} in $\Omega$, and 
satisfies $\|Du\|_{L^\infty(\Omega)}<1$ with 
$
u\in C^1(\Omega)\cap W^{2,2}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\Omega).
$
<!-- end theorem-10 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{defiintrinsic.potentials}
Let $u:\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R$ be weakly spacelike, and write
$K_\rho^u(x_0)$ for the Lorentzian balls associated with its graph.
For $\sigma,\vartheta>0$ and $w\in L^1_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n)$,
we define the intrinsic Lorentzian potential
\begin{equation}\label{defi-PL}
\mathbf{PL}_{\sigma,u}^{\vartheta}(w;x_0,r)
:=
\int_0^r
\varrho^\sigma
\left(
\frac{1}{\omega_n\varrho^n}
\int_{K_\varrho^u(x_0)}|w|\,dx
\right)^\vartheta
\frac{d\varrho}{\varrho},
\qquad x_0\in\mathbb R^n,\quad r>0,
\end{equation}
with values in $[0,+\infty]$. The set $K_\varrho^u(x_0)$ is the projection onto
$\mathbb R^n$ of the Lorentzian ball of radius $\varrho$
on the graph of $u$, centered at $(x_0,u(x_0))$.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- theorem-11: body_md -->
\label{t3}
Let $n\ge2$ and let
$u\in\mathbb X(\mathbb R^n)$ be the minimizer of
\eqref{bi.en} with  $\mu\in\mathbb Y(\mathbb R^n)$. Moreover, assume that 
$\mu\in L(n,1)(\mathbb R^n)$ if $n\ge3$ and $\mu\in L^2(\log L)^\alpha(\mathbb R^2)$
for some $\alpha>2$ when $ n=2$.
Then the intrinsic potential estimate
\begin{align}\label{int.pot}
\mathcal h_u(x_0)^{-\gamma}
&\ge
\mathop{\int\hskip -1,05em -\, \!\!\!}\nolimits_{K_r^u(x_0)}
\mathcal h_u^{-(\gamma+1)}\,{\rm d}x
-2\mathbf{PL}_{2,u}^{1}(\mu^2;x_0,r)
-\mathbf{PL}_{1,u}^{1}(\mu;x_0,r)
\end{align}
holds for some $\gamma\in (0, 1/n)$\footnote{The constant $\gamma\equiv\gamma(n)$ originates in
Proposition \ref{p31}, and the same choice is used throughout
the paper. A possible choice is $\gamma=\frac1{4n}$.}, every $x_0\in\mathbb R^n$ and every $r>0$,
where the intrinsic Lorentzian potentials are defined
in \eqref{defi-PL}.
<!-- end theorem-11 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
{\em In the following we agree to set
\eqn{light}
$$
\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-|z|^2}}=\infty, \qquad \mbox{whenever $|z|=1$.}
$$
In particular, we set $\mathcal h_u(x)=\infty$ whenever $|Du(x)|=1$. Such case will be anyway very seldom considered.}
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
{\em We shall denote by $Dv=(D_i v)$ the usual gradient operator in $\mathbb R^n$, while, given a unit vector $\nu\in \mathbb R^n$, we denote by $\partial_\nu v$ the partial derivative of $v$ in direction $\nu$, so that, with  $\{\textnormal{\texttt{e}}_i\}$ being the standard basis of $\mathbb R^n$, $D_i v = \partial_{\textnormal{\texttt{e}}_i} v\equiv \partial_i v$. We shall sometimes depart from this notation using $\partial$ when considering an integrand as in (\ref{recalla})-(\ref{0.1}), so that, for instance, we have a notation like $D(H(Dv))= \partial H(Dv)D^2v$ allowing to better use the structural properties of the considered integrand when acting on gradients.}
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ be an open subset and let
$
\mu\in L^1_{\operatorname{loc}}(\Omega).
$
A weakly spacelike function
$
u\in W^{1,\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\Omega)
$
is called a distributional or a weak solution to \eqref{bi} in $\Omega$ if
$
\mathcal h_u  Du
\in L^1_{\operatorname{loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb R^n)
$
and\footnote{This implies, in particular, that $|Du(x)|<1$ for almost every $x\in \Omega$ in accordance with 
(\ref{light}).}
\eqn{weshall}
$$
 \int_\Omega
\langle\mathcal h_u  Du,D\varphi\rangle
\,{\rm d}x
=\int_\Omega \mu\varphi\,{\rm d}x
$$
for every
$
\varphi\in C^\infty_0(\Omega).
$
Equivalently,
$
-\mathcal Mu=\mu
$
in $\mathcal D'(\Omega)$.
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ be open and let
$\mu:\Omega\to\mathbb R$ be given. We say that
$
u\in C^2(\Omega)
$
is a \emph{classical solution} to \eqref{bi} if
$|Du|<1$
in $\Omega$ and
$
-\mathcal M u
=
\mu
$
holds pointwise in $\Omega$.
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- definition-5: body_md -->
\label{defidatobordo}
Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a bounded domain, let
$
u_0:\partial\Omega\to\mathbb R,
$ 
and let
$
u:\Omega\to\mathbb R
$
be weakly spacelike. We write
$
u=u_0
$
on $\partial\Omega$ if, for every
$
x_0\in\partial\Omega
$
and every open straight line segment
$
\ell\subset\Omega
$
having $x_0$ as an endpoint, one has
\eqn{fbi}
$$
\lim_{\substack{x\to x_0\\ x\in\ell}}u(x)
=
u_0(x_0).
$$
<!-- end definition-5 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
{\em
Assume that $\Omega$ is Lipschitz regular and
$
u\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega).
$
Then $u$ admits a Lipschitz continuous representative on
$
\bar\Omega.
$
Consequently, if
$
u_0\in C^0(\partial\Omega),
$
condition (\ref{fbi}) holds at every point
$
x_0\in\partial\Omega
$
if and only if
$
u_0
$
coincides with the Sobolev trace of $u$ on $\partial\Omega$.}
<!-- end remark-5 -->
<!-- remark-6: body_md -->
\label{concreto}
{\em
Let
$
\mu\in L^1_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n),
$
and, when $n=2$, assume in addition that
$
\mu\in L^1_0(\mathbb R^2).
$
We say that $\mu$ induces an element of $\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)^*$ if
\eqn{dual.function.norm}
$$
\|\mu\|_{\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)^*}
:=\sup_{\substack{
\varphi\in C^\infty_0(\mathbb R^n)\\
\|D\varphi\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^n)}\le1}}
\left|\int_{\mathbb R^n}
\mu\varphi\,{\rm d}x
\right|<\infty.
$$
When $n\ge3$, this is the usual concrete characterization of the
dual of
$
\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)=\mathcal D^{1,2}(\mathbb R^n),
$
since
$
C^\infty_0(\mathbb R^n)
$
is dense in $\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)$. 
When $n=2$, for every
$
\varphi\in C^\infty_0(\mathbb R^2),
$
set
$
P\varphi := \varphi-(\varphi)_{B_1(0)}.
$
Then
$
P\varphi\in\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^2)
$
and
$
D(P\varphi)=D\varphi.
$
Moreover, since
$
\mu\in L^1_0(\mathbb R^2),
$
one has
\eqn{dual.zero.mean}
$$
\int_{\mathbb R^2}\mu P\varphi\,{\rm d}x
=
\int_{\mathbb R^2}\mu\varphi\,{\rm d}x.
$$
Since
$
\{P\varphi:\varphi\in C^\infty_0(\mathbb R^2)\}
$
is dense in $\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^2)$, condition \eqref{dual.function.norm} is also
precisely the boundedness condition required for the distribution
induced by $\mu$ to extend continuously to the normalized space
$\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^2)$. Thus, in every dimension $n\ge2$, condition
\eqref{dual.function.norm} yields a unique continuous linear
functional on $\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)$, still denoted by $\mu$, satisfying
$$
|\langle\mu,w\rangle|
\le
\|\mu\|_{\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)^*}
\|w\|_{\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)}
\qquad
\mbox{for every }w\in\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n).
$$
The Riesz representation theorem
gives a unique
$
v_\mu\in\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)
$
such that
\eqn{dual.riesz}
$$
\begin{cases}
\displaystyle 
\langle\mu,w\rangle
=
\int_{\mathbb R^n}
\langle Dv_\mu,Dw\rangle\,{\rm d}x
\qquad
\mbox{for every }w\in\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)\\
\|\mu\|_{\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)^*}
=
\|Dv_\mu\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^n)}.
\end{cases}
$$
We call such a $v_{\mu}$ the Riesz representative of $\mu$. 
If $n\ge3$, this implies 
\eqn{dual.ide}
$$
\int_{\mathbb R^n}
\langle Dv_\mu,D\varphi\rangle\,{\rm d}x
=
\int_{\mathbb R^n}
\mu\varphi\,{\rm d}x, \qquad \mbox{for every $\varphi\in C^\infty_0(\mathbb R^n)$}. 
$$
If $n=2$, we instead take
$
w=P\varphi
$
and use \eqref{dual.zero.mean}, thereby 
obtaining the same identity. Consequently, in every dimension
$n\ge2$, it holds that  
$
-\Delta v_\mu=\mu
$
in $\mathcal D'(\mathbb R^n).
$
}
<!-- end remark-6 -->
<!-- definition-6: body_md -->
\label{minimiglobali}
Let $\mu\in\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)^*$.
A function $u\in\mathbb X(\mathbb R^n)$ is called a
\emph{global minimizer} of $\mathcal E_\mu$ if
$
\mathcal E_\mu(u)\le\mathcal E_\mu(v)
$
for every $v\in\mathbb X(\mathbb R^n)$.
<!-- end definition-6 -->
<!-- remark-7: body_md -->
\label{globale.locale}{\em 
Let
$\mu\in\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)^*\cap L^1_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n)$,
with the identification in Remark \ref{concreto}, and assume
in addition that $\mu\in L^1_0(\mathbb R^2)$ when $n=2$.
Let $u\in\mathbb X(\mathbb R^n)$ be a global minimizer
in the sense of Definition \ref{minimiglobali}.
For every bounded open subset $U\subset\mathbb R^n$, introduce
$$
\mathfrak X(u;U)
:=
\left\{
w\in W^{1,\infty}(U):
w-u\in W^{1,2}_0(U),
\quad
\|Dw\|_{L^\infty(U)}\le1
\right\}.
$$
Then $\mathcal E_\mu(u;U)\le\mathcal E_\mu(w;U)$ holds 
for every $w\in\mathfrak X(u;U)$.
In particular, $u$ is a local minimizer in the sense
of Definition \ref{minimilocali} with $\Omega =\mathbb R^n$. Indeed, given $w\in\mathfrak X(u;U)$, set
$$
w_u:=
\begin{cases}
w&\text{in }U\\
u&\text{in }\mathbb R^n\setminus U
\end{cases}
\qquad
z:=w_u-u.
$$
Define, as in Remark \ref{concreto}, 
$Pz:=z$ if $n\ge3$ and 
$Pz:=z-(z)_{B_1(0)}$ when $n=2$. 
Then $u+Pz\in\mathbb X(\mathbb R^n)$.
By Remark \ref{concreto}, approximating $z$ by smooth
compactly supported functions uniformly and in the
Dirichlet norm gives
$
\langle\mu,Pz\rangle
=
\int_U\mu(w-u)\,dx.
$
Global minimality therefore yields
$$
0
\le
\mathcal E_\mu(u+Pz)-\mathcal E_\mu(u)
=
\int_U
[H(Dw)-H(Du)-\mu(w-u)]\, \mathrm{d} x=
\mathcal E_\mu(w;U)-\mathcal E_\mu(u;U).
$$}
<!-- end remark-7 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{crit}
Let $n\ge2$ and $r\in(0,1)$.
If $\sigma,\vartheta>0$, $n\vartheta>\sigma$, and
$w\in L^1(\mathbb R^n)$, then
$$
\lVert {\bf P}_\sigma^\vartheta(w;\cdot,r) \rVert
_{L^\infty(\mathbb R^n)}
\lesssim_{n,\vartheta,\sigma}
[w]_{n\vartheta/\sigma,\vartheta;\mathbb R^n}^{\vartheta}.
$$
If $n=2$, $\alpha>2$, and
$w\in L^2(\log L)^\alpha(\mathbb R^2)$, then
$$
\lVert {\bf P}_1^{1/2}(\lvert w\rvert^2;\cdot,r) \rVert
_{L^\infty(\mathbb R^2)}
\lesssim_\alpha
\lVert w \rVert_{L^2(\log L)^\alpha(\mathbb R^2)}.
$$
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
The first estimate follows from
\cite[Lemma 4.1]{dm23}.
For completeness, we sketch the proof of the second estimate, a version of which already appears in \cite[Section 2]{bm20}.
Denote by $J$ the quantity in the left-hand side of (\ref{orlicz.rearr}). 
By \eqref{orlicz.rearr},
$
J\lesssim_\alpha
\lVert w \rVert_{L^2(\log L)^\alpha(\mathbb R^2)}^2.
$
The Hardy--Littlewood rearrangement inequality, 
the change of variables $s=\pi\varrho^2$ and the use of (\ref{orlicz.rearr}) give
$$
\begin{aligned}
{\bf P}_1^{1/2}(\lvert w\rvert^2;x,r)
&\le c\int_0^{\pi r^2}
\left(\int_0^s(w^*(t))^2\,{\rm d}t\right)^{1/2}
\frac{\,{\rm d}s}{s}\\
&\le c\int_0^{\pi r^2}\left[
\frac{1}{\log^\alpha(e+1/s)}\int_0^s(w^*(t))^2\log^\alpha\left(e+\frac1t\right)\,{\rm d}t\right]^{1/2}\frac{\,{\rm d}s}{s}
\\
&\le c \int_0^\pi \frac{1}{\log^{\alpha/2}(e+1/s)}\frac{\,{\rm d}s}{s}\lVert w \rVert_{L^2(\log L)^\alpha(\mathbb R^2)}
\le c_\alpha \lVert w \rVert_{L^2(\log L)^\alpha(\mathbb R^2)}. 
\end{aligned}
$$
Note that the last integral appearing in the display is finite because $\alpha>2$.
Taking the supremum over $x\in\mathbb R^2$
completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{trudinger.lemma}
Let $n\ge2$. There exist constants
$
\mathfrak c_2\equiv\mathfrak c_2(n)\ge1
$
and
$
\mathfrak c_3\equiv\mathfrak c_3(n)>0
$
such that, for every bounded open set
$
U\subset\mathbb R^n
$
and every
$w\in W^{1,n}_0(U)$
with
$\lVert Dw \rVert_{L^n(U)}>0,$
one has
\eqn{trudinger.scale}
$$
\int_U
\exp\left\{
\mathfrak c_3
\left(\frac{\lvert w\rvert}{\lVert Dw \rVert_{L^n(U)}}
\right)^{\frac{n}{n-1}}\right\}\,{\rm d}x
\le
\mathfrak c_2\lvert U\rvert.
$$
Moreover, for every ball $B\subset\mathbb R^n$ and every
$w\in W^{1,n}(B)$ with $\lVert Dw \rVert_{L^n(B)}>0$, one has
\eqn{trudinger.mean}
$$
\int_B
\exp\left\{\mathfrak c_4\left(\frac{\lvert w-(w)_B\rvert}{\lVert Dw \rVert_{L^n(B)}}\right)^{\frac{n}{n-1}}\right\}
\,{\rm d}x\le
\mathfrak c_5\lvert B\rvert,
$$
for constants $\mathfrak c_4, \mathfrak c_5$ again depending on $n$.
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{boun.p}
Let $u\in \mathbb{X}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ be the minimizer of \eqref{bi.en} with $\mu\in \mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)^{*}$. Then
\eqn{linf}
$$
\begin{cases}
\displaystyle
\ \lVert u \rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\le c\mathfrak{m}_\mu\quad &\mbox{if} \ \ n\ge 3\vspace{1.5mm}\\
\displaystyle
\ \left\|\frac{u}{\log(e+\lvert \ \cdot \ \rvert)}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\le c\mathfrak{m}_\mu\quad &\mbox{if} \ \ n=2,
\end{cases}
$$
where $c\equiv c(n)$, and $\mathfrak{m}_\mu$ is defined as 
\eqn{mmi}
$$
\mathfrak{m}_\mu
:=
\begin{cases}
\displaystyle
\lVert \mu \rVert_{\mathcal D^{1,2}(\mathbb R^n)^*}^{3/n}+\lVert \mu \rVert_{\mathcal D^{1,2}(\mathbb R^n)^*}^{1/n}
&\mbox{if }n\ge3
\\[3mm]
\displaystyle
\lVert \mu \rVert_{\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)^*}+1
&\mbox{if }n=2.
\end{cases}
$$
Moreover, 
\eqn{coerciva}
$$
\lVert Du \rVert_{L^2(\mathbb R^n)}=\lVert u \rVert_{\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)}\leq 2 \|\mu\|_{\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)^*}\,.
$$
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lemmino}
Let $w\in\mathcal D^{1,2}(\mathbb R^n)$ be such that 
$\|Dw\|_{L^\infty(\mathbb R^n)}\le1$. The following holds. 
\begin{itemize}
    \item If $n\ge 3$, then $w\in L^{2^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ with $\lim_{\lvert x\rvert\to \infty}w(x)=0$, and 
    \eqn{infsup}
    $$
    \lVert w \rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\le c \lVert Dw \rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{\frac{2(n+\mathcal{s})}{n\mathcal{s}}}+c\lVert Dw \rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{\frac{2}{\mathcal{s}}}
    $$
    for all $n<\mathcal{s}<\infty$, where $c\equiv c (n, \mathcal{s})$.
\item If $n=2$, let $w$ denote a locally Lipschitz
representative of its class in \eqref{24.3}$_2$. Then
    \eqn{logsup}
    $$
\sup_{x\in \mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{\lvert w(x)\rvert}{\log(e+\lvert x\rvert)} + \sup_{x\in \mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{\lvert w(x)\rvert}{1+\lvert x\rvert} \le c\lVert Dw \rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})} + c\lvert (w)_{B_{1}(0)}\rvert+c,
    $$
    where $c\equiv c(n)$. Moreover, for any $\varepsilon>0$ there exists $r_{\varepsilon}\equiv r_{\varepsilon}(w,\varepsilon)>0$ such that
    \eqn{weightsmall}
    $$
    \sup_{x\in \mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{r_{\varepsilon}}(0)}\frac{\lvert w(x)\rvert}{1+\lvert x\rvert}<\varepsilon.
    $$
\end{itemize}
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
For the content of the first bullet see \cite[Lemma 5.2]{bi23}. Let us focus on \eqref{logsup}-\eqref{weightsmall}, which are probably well-known under different assumptions, but we couldn't locate them in the literature. So we report the proof for completeness. 
In the following the denoted constant $c\equiv c_{n}$ depends only on the dimension $n$. Let 
$x\in\mathbb R^{2}$ and $r>0$, then we have
\begin{align*}
\lvert (w)_{B_r(x)}-(w)_{B_{2r}(x)}\rvert &\leq \mathop{\int\hskip -1,05em -\, \!\!\!}\nolimits_{B_r(x)}
   \lvert w-(w)_{B_{2r}(x)}\rvert\,{\rm d}y \leq  c
   \left(\mathop{\int\hskip -1,05em -\, \!\!\!}\nolimits_{B_{2r}(x)}
   \lvert w-(w)_{B_{2r}(x)}\rvert^2\,{\rm d}y\right)^{1/2}
\end{align*}
and here we have used Jensen and  H\"older's inequalities. By further using Poincaré's inequality and exploiting that $n=2$ we conclude with 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dyadic-means}
\lvert (w)_{B_r(x)}-(w)_{B_{2r}(x)}\rvert
\leq c\lVert Dw \rVert_{L^{2}(B_{2r}(x))}.
\end{equation}
Choose $k\in\mathbb N\cup\{0\}$ such that
$
2^{k-1}<1+\lvert x\rvert\leq 2^{k}. 
$ With $R:=2^{k}$ we have 
$k+1\leq c\log(e+\lvert x\rvert)$
and $
B_R(x)\subset B_{2R}(0).
$
Iterating \eqref{eq:dyadic-means} yields 
\eqn{eq:chain-x}
$$
\begin{cases}
\lvert (w)_{B_1(x)}-(w)_{B_R(x)}\rvert
\leq ck\lVert Dw \rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb R^{2})}
\\[4pt]
\lvert (w)_{B_{2R}(0)}-(w)_{B_1(0)}\rvert
\leq
c(k+1)\lVert Dw \rVert_{L^2(\mathbb R^2)}.
\end{cases}
$$
Moreover, since $B_R(x)\subset B_{2R}(0)$, arguing as for (\ref{eq:dyadic-means}) we find 
\eqn{eq:overlapping-balls}
$$
\lvert (w)_{B_R(x)}-(w)_{B_{2R}(0)}\rvert
 \leq c\lVert Dw \rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb R^{2})}.
$$
Combining \eqref{eq:chain-x} and \eqref{eq:overlapping-balls}, we obtain
\eqn{eq:mean-log-growth}
$$
\lvert (w)_{B_1(x)}-(w)_{B_1(0)}\rvert
\leq
c\log(e+\lvert x\rvert)\lVert Dw \rVert_{L^2(\mathbb R^2)}.
$$
On the other hand, since \(w\) is \(1\)-Lipschitz,
$
\lvert w(x)-(w)_{B_1(x)}\rvert\leq1.
$
This and \eqref{eq:mean-log-growth} imply that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pointwise-log-growth}
\lvert w(x)\rvert
\leq
1+\lvert (w)_{B_1(0)}\rvert
+c\log(e+\lvert x\rvert)\lVert Dw \rVert_{L^2(\mathbb R^2)}.
\end{equation}
Since \(\log(e+\lvert x\rvert)\geq\log2\), dividing
\eqref{eq:pointwise-log-growth} by \(\log(e+\lvert x\rvert)\), and recalling
that \(x\in\mathbb R^2\) is arbitrary, proves the estimate for the
first quantity in \eqref{logsup}. We next estimate the second, which follows by observing that the Lipschitz continuity 
of $w$ gives 
$\lvert w(x)\rvert
\leq \lvert w(0)\rvert+\lvert x\rvert$ and $\lvert w(0)\rvert
\leq
\lvert (w)_{B_1(0)}\rvert+1
$. The property in (\ref{weightsmall}) now easily follows from (\ref{eq:pointwise-log-growth}).
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
By \eqref{infsup}, with the choice $\mathcal s=2n$, and by
\eqref{logsup}, recalling that $(u)_{B_1(0)}=0$ when $n=2$, it is
enough to prove \eqref{coerciva}.
Using that
$ \mathcal E_{\mu}(u)\le\mathcal E_{\mu}(0)=0$, we find
$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac12 \|Du\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^n)}^2\le
\int_{\mathbb R^n}H(Du)\,{\rm d}x
&\le \langle\mu,u\rangle\\&\le
\|\mu\|_{\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)^*}\|u\|_{\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)}=\|\mu\|_{\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)^*}\|Du\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^n)},
\end{aligned}
$$
from which (\ref{coerciva}) follows immediately.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- definition-7: body_md -->
A \(C^1\) hypersurface \(\mathfrak M\subset\mathbb L^{n+1}\) is said to be
spacelike if, for every \(z\in\mathfrak M\), the restriction of the
Lorentzian metric \(\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb L^{n+1}}\) to
\(\textnormal T_z\mathfrak M\) is positive definite.
<!-- end definition-7 -->
<!-- definition-8: body_md -->
If $u\in C^1(\Omega)$, then its vertical graph is a spacelike
hypersurface if and only if $u$ is strictly spacelike in the sense
introduced in Section \ref{notazioni}.
<!-- end definition-8 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{ibp}
Let \(u\in C^2(\Omega)\) be strictly spacelike and let \(\mathfrak M\)
be its vertical graph. Let \(v\in C_{0}^1(\mathfrak M)\) and
\(w\in C^1(\mathfrak M)\). Let $\textnormal{\texttt{H}}_u\in C(\Omega)$ be defined by
 $$
 \textnormal{\texttt{H}}_{u}:=-\mathcal{h}_{u}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}g^{ij}D_{ij}u.
 $$
 It holds
 $$
 \begin{cases}
     \displaystyle
     \ \int_{\mathfrak{M}}v\delta_{n+1}w\,{\rm d} A=\int_{\mathfrak{M}}\mathcal{h}_{u}vw \textnormal{\texttt{H}}_{u}\,{\rm d} A-\int_{\mathfrak{M}}w\delta_{n+1}v\,{\rm d} A\vspace{1.5mm}\\
     \displaystyle
     \ \int_{\mathfrak{M}}v\delta_{i}w\,{\rm d} A=\int_{\mathfrak{M}}\mathcal{v}_{i}vw \textnormal{\texttt{H}}_{u}\,{\rm d} A-\int_{\mathfrak{M}}w\delta_{i}v\,{\rm d} A,
 \end{cases}
 $$
 for all $i\in \{1,\cdots,n\}$.
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{coa}
Let $u\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ be a strictly spacelike function,\footnote{For strictly spacelike functions, $K_{s}(x_{0})$ is always bounded for all $s\in (0,\infty)$, cf. Lemma \ref{balls} \textnormal{(h$_1$)}.} $\mathfrak{M}:=\textnormal{graph}(u)$, $x_{0}\in \mathbb{R}^{n}$, $w\in C^1(\mathfrak{M})$, 
$$
f(t):= \int_{L_{t}^u(x_{0})}w\,{\rm d} A \quad \mbox{for every $t>0$.}
$$
Then
$$
\frac{\textnormal{d}}{\textnormal{d}s} f(s)=\int_{\partial L_{s}^u(x_{0})}\frac{w}{\lvert \delta \ell_{x_0,u}\rvert_{\mathbb{L}^{n+1}}}\,{\rm d}\sigma
$$
holds whenever $s>0$, where $\,{\rm d}\sigma$ denotes the surface measure induced by $g$
on $\partial L_s^u(x_0)$.
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop.mon}
Let $u\in C^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ be a classical solution to \eqref{bi} (and therefore strictly spacelike) with a smooth charge $\mu\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ with compact support, $r\in (0,\infty)$ a number, and assume that $(x_{0},u(x_{0}))=(0,0)$. With $\gamma, s>0$ we define the quantities 
$$
\mathbb A(s) :=\frac{1}{s^{n}}\int_{L_{s}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\,{\rm d} A , \qquad \mathbb B(s):= \int_{L_{s}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\ell^{-n-2}\lvert \langle\Phi,\mathcal{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{n+1}}\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d} A, 
$$
$$
\mathbb E(s):= \frac{1}{s^{n+1}}\int_{L_{s}}\frac{1}{2}(s^{2}-\ell^{2})\mathcal{h}_{u}^{2-\gamma}\left[\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}(D_{ij}u)^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{v}_{i}D_{ij}u\right)^{2}\right]\,{\rm d} A,
$$
$$
\mathbb E_\mu(s):=\frac{1}{s^{n+1}}\int_{L_{s}}\frac{1}{2}(s^{2}-\ell^{2})\left(\frac{1}{4}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\mu^{2}+\gamma\delta_{n+1}(\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-(\gamma+1)}\mu)\right)\,{\rm d} A
$$
and 
$$
\mathbb F_\mu(s):=
\frac{1}{s^{n+1}}\int_{L_{s}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\mu\langle\Phi,\mathcal{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{n+1}}\,{\rm d} A, 
$$
where $\ell(\Phi(x)):=\ell_u(x,0)$ and $L_{s}:=\{(x,u(x))\in \mathfrak{M}\colon \ell(x,0)<s\}$. 
There exists $\gamma\equiv \gamma(n)\in (0,1/n)$ such that
\eqn{monotonia}
$$
\frac{\textnormal{d}}{\textnormal{d}s} [\mathbb  A(s)+\mathbb  B(s)] \leq - c_{\star}\mathbb  E(s) + \mathbb  E_\mu(s) - \mathbb  F_\mu(s)
$$holds for all $s\in (0,r)$, with $c_{\star}\equiv c_{\star}(n)>0$. In particular, when $\mu\equiv 0$ the function $s \mapsto \mathbb  A(s)+\mathbb  B(s)$ is non-increasing.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- remark-8: body_md -->
\label{rem0}
    \emph{If $u\in C^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ is a classical solution to \eqref{bi} with charge $\mu\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ and $x_{0}\in \mathbb{R}^{n}$ is a point, the translated map $u_{0}(x):=u(x_{0}+x)-u(x_{0})$ is still a solution to \eqref{bi} with datum $\mu_{0}(x):=\mu(x_{0}+x)$, so there is no loss of generality in assuming that $x_{0}=0$ and $u(0)=0$. In Proposition \ref{prop.mon} note that 
    $$
    \langle\Phi,\mathcal{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{n+1}}= - \mathcal h_u (u-\langle Du, x\rangle).
    $$}
<!-- end remark-8 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{balls} Let $u\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ be strictly spacelike, $s\in (0,\infty)$ be a number and $x_{0}\in \mathbb{R}^{n}$ be any point. Then the following holds.
\begin{itemize}
    \item[(\textnormal{h}$_1$)] For all balls $B_{s}(x_{0})\subset \mathbb{R}^{n}$, it is $B_{s}(x_{0})\subset K_{s}(x_{0})$. Moreover there exists $r_{0}\equiv r_{0}(x_{0},u,s)>0$ such that $K_{s}(x_{0})\subset B_{r_{0}}(x_{0})$. If in addition $s\in (0,1]$, then $K_{s}(x_{0})\subset B_{c_{0}s}(x_{0})$ for some positive constant $c_{0}\equiv c_{0}(x_{0},u)$. Finally, if 
    \eqn{h-}
    $$\mathcal{h}_{-}:=\inf_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-1}=\inf_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\sqrt{1-|Du(x)|^2}>0,$$ then $K_{s}(x_{0})\subset B_{s/\mathcal{h}_{-}}(x_{0})$.
    \item[(\textnormal{h}$_2$)] If $u\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$, then $K_{s}(x_{0})\subset B_{\tilde{s}}(x_{0})$ with $\tilde{s}:=(s^{2}+4\lVert u \rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2})^{1/2}$.
    \item[(\textnormal{h}$_3$)] In dimension $n=2$, if $u\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap \mathbb{X}$ and $\Omega\subset \mathbb{R}^{2}$ is a compact set and $s\in (0,\infty)$ a number, there exists a radius $r_{*}\equiv r_{*}(u,\Omega,s)>1$ such that $$\Omega\Subset B_{r_{*}}(0) \quad \mbox{and}\quad \,{\rm dist}_{L}(\Omega, \mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{r_{*}}(0))\ge 10\max\{s,1\}\,.$$ In other terms, for any compact set $\Omega\subset \mathbb{R}^{2}$, radius $s\in (0,\infty)$, we can find a ball $B_{r_{*}}(0)\subset \mathbb{R}^{2}$ with radius $r_{*}\equiv r_{*}(\Omega,u,s)>1$ such that $\Omega\cap K_{s}(y)=\varnothing$ for all $y\in \mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{r_{*}}(0)$. 
\end{itemize}
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
We start with the proof of (\textnormal{h}$_1$). Inclusion $B_{s}(x_{0})\subset K_{s}(x_{0})$ is trivial by the very definition of Lorentz distance. Since $|Du(x_0)|<1$ and $Du$ is continuous, there exist
$\delta_0\in(0,1)$ and $\varrho_0>0$, depending on
$1-|Du(x_0)|$ and on the local modulus of continuity of $Du$,
such that
$\|Du\|_{L^\infty(B_{\varrho_0}(x_0))}\le\delta_0$. We then bound
\begin{eqnarray*}
\lvert x-x_{0}\rvert^{2}&\le&\ell(x,x_{0})^{2}+\lvert u(x)-u(x_{0})\rvert^{2}\nonumber \\
&\stackrel{x\in K_{s}(x_{0})}{\le}& s^{2} +\lvert x-x_{0}\rvert^{2}\int_{0}^{\min\left\{1,\frac{\varrho_{0}}{\lvert x-x_{0}\rvert}\right\}}\lvert Du(x_{0}+t(x-x_{0}))\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d}t\nonumber \\
&&+\lvert x-x_{0}\rvert^{2}\int^{1}_{\min\left\{1,\frac{\varrho_{0}}{\lvert x-x_{0}\rvert}\right\}}\lvert Du(x_{0}+t(x-x_{0}))\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d}t\nonumber\\
&\le&s^{2}+\lvert x-x_{0}\rvert^{2}\left(\delta_{0}^{2}\min\left\{1,\frac{\varrho_{0}}{\lvert x-x_{0}\rvert}\right\}+1-\min\left\{1,\frac{\varrho_{0}}{\lvert x-x_{0}\rvert}\right\}\right),
\end{eqnarray*}
so, reabsorbing terms, we obtain that 
$$\lvert x-x_{0}\rvert^{2}(1-\delta_{0}^{2})\min \left\{1,\frac{\varrho_{0}}{\lvert x-x_{0}\rvert}\right\}\le s^{2}.$$ We set 
$$
r_{0}:=\max\left\{\frac{s}{\sqrt{1-\delta_{0}^{2}}},\frac{s^{2}}{\varrho_{0}(1-\delta_{0}^{2})}\right\},
$$
and deduce from the above displays that $K_{s}(x_{0})\subset B_{r_{0}}(x_{0})$. In particular, if $s\in (0,1]$ by definition we can further estimate 
$$r_0\le
s\max\left\{
(1-\delta_0^2)^{-1/2},
\frac{1}{\varrho_0(1-\delta_0^2)}
\right\}
=:c_0(x_0,u)s.$$
Concerning the final claim in (\textnormal{h}$_1$), we notice that $\mathcal{h}_{-}>0$ implies that $\lVert Du \rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}\le 1-\mathcal{h}_{-}^{2}$, thus 
\begin{flalign*}
\lvert x-x_{0}\rvert^{2}&\le \ell(x,x_{0})^{2}+\lvert u(x)-u(x_{0})\rvert^{2}\nonumber \\
&\le s^{2}+\lVert Du \rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}\lvert x-x_{0}\rvert^{2}\le s^{2}+(1-\mathcal{h}_{-}^{2})\lvert x-x_{0}\rvert^{2},
\end{flalign*}
and $\lvert x-x_{0}\rvert\le s/\mathcal{h}_{-}$.
For assertion \textnormal{(h$_2$)}, see
\cite[Lemma 2.8(i)]{bi23}. We now turn to the proof of
\textnormal{(h$_3$)}. The idea is that Lorentz balls
centered far away from a given compact set do not
``grow'' fast enough to reach it. Being $\Omega$ compact, there exists $\bar{r}\equiv \bar{r}(\Omega)$ such that $\Omega\Subset B_{\bar{r}}(0)$. Moreover by \eqref{weightsmall} applied with $\varepsilon=1/2^{4}$, we can find a radius $r_{2^{-4}}>0$ such that
\eqn{24.6}
$$
\sup_{x\in \mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{r_{2^{-4}}}(0)}\frac{\lvert u(x)\rvert}{1+\lvert x\rvert}<\frac{1}{2^{4}}.
$$
Now set
\eqn{24.7}
$$
r_{*}:=2^{10}\max\{s,1\}\max\left\{\bar{r}, r_{2^{-4}},2\max\{\bar{r},1\}\left\|\frac{u}{1+\lvert \ \cdot \ \rvert}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},1\right\}.
$$
For $x\in\Omega$ and
$y\in\mathbb R^2\setminus B_{r_*}(0)$, we have $$
\lvert x-y\rvert\ge \lvert y\rvert-\lvert x\rvert\ge 2^{-1}\lvert y\rvert+2^{-1}r_{*}(1-2^{-9}\max\{s,1\}^{-1})\ge 2^{-1}\lvert y\rvert+2^{-2}r_{*},
$$
and
\begin{eqnarray*}
\lvert u(x)-u(y)\rvert&\le&  \left\|\frac{u}{1+\lvert \ \cdot \ \rvert}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}(1+\lvert x\rvert)+\left(\sup_{x\in \mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{r_{*}}(0)}\frac{\lvert u(x)\rvert}{1+\lvert x\rvert}\right)(1+\lvert y\rvert)\nonumber \\
&\stackrel{\eqref{24.6}}{\le}&2\max\{\bar{r},1\}\left\|\frac{u}{1+\lvert \ \cdot \ \rvert}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+2^{-3}\lvert y\rvert\stackrel{\eqref{24.7}}{\le}\frac{r_{*}}{2^{10}}+2^{-3}\lvert y\rvert.
\end{eqnarray*}
We then control
\begin{flalign*}
\ell(x,y)^{2}&\ge\lvert y\rvert^{2}(2^{-2}-2^{-6})+r_{*}^{2}(2^{-4}-2^{-20})+\lvert y\rvert r_{*}(2^{-2}-2^{-12})\nonumber \\
&\ge\frac{\lvert y\rvert^{2}}{5}+\frac{r_{*}^{2}}{20}+\frac{\lvert y\rvert r_{*}}{5}\ge \frac{2r_{*}^{2}}{5}\stackrel{\eqref{24.7}}{>}100\max\{s,1\}^{2},
\end{flalign*}
so $\,{\rm dist}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{r_{*}}(0))\ge \,{\rm dist}_{L}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{r_{*}}(0))\ge 10\max\{s,1\}$ and $\Omega\cap K_{s}(y)=\varnothing$ for all $y\in \mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{r_{*}}(0)$.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{limlem}
    Let $u\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ be strictly spacelike, $f\in L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ be a function such that $\lvert f\rvert^{p}\in L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ for some $p>0$, and $x_{0}$ be a Lebesgue point for $\lvert f\rvert^{p}$. Then
    \eqn{0.8}
    $$
    \lim_{s\to 0}\frac{1}{s^{n}}\int_{L_{s}(x_{0})}\lvert f(x)\rvert^{p}\,{\rm d} A=\omega_{n}\lvert f(x_{0})\rvert^{p}.
    $$
    In particular, if $f\equiv 1$, it is 
    $$\lim_{s\to 0}\frac{A(L_{s}(x_{0}))}{\lvert B_{s}(x_{0})\rvert}=1.$$
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- definition-9: body_md -->
\label{defilight}
Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ be open and let
$u:\Omega\to\mathbb R$ be weakly spacelike.
\begin{itemize}

\item A nondegenerate segment
$\overline{xy}\subset\Omega$, $x\neq y$, is called a
\emph{light segment} if
$
|u(x)-u(y)|=|x-y|.
$

\item A \emph{light ray} is a maximally extended light segment.
More precisely, given a light segment $\overline{xy}$, let
$\mathsf r_{x,y}$ denote the line through $x$ and $y$. The light ray
containing $\overline{xy}$ is the set
\[
\ell_{xy}
:=
\bigcup
\left\{
\overline{zw}\subset\Omega:
\begin{array}{l}
\overline{zw}\text{ is a light segment},\\
\overline{xy}\subset\overline{zw}\subset\mathsf r_{x,y}
\end{array}
\right\}.
\]
\end{itemize}
<!-- end definition-9 -->
<!-- remark-9: body_md -->
\label{raggire}
{\em
Let $u:\Omega\to\mathbb R$ be weakly spacelike and let
$
\overline{xy}\subset\Omega
$
with $x\neq y$. If
$
|u(x)-u(y)|=|x-y|,
$
then it is easy to see that $u$ is affine on $\overline{xy}$. More precisely, assuming for
definiteness that $u(y)>u(x)$ and setting
$
z_t:=(1-t)x+ty
$
for $t\in[0,1]$, one has
$
u(z_t)
=
u(x)+t|y-x|
=
(1-t)u(x)+tu(y)
$
for every $t\in[0,1]$. Moreover, if
$
u\in\mathbb X(\mathbb R^n)
$
and $n\ge3$, all light segments, and therefore all light rays, have
uniformly bounded length. Indeed, by \eqref{infsup},
\eqn{aff.0.1}
$$
\sup
\left\{
|x-y|:
\overline{xy}\subset\mathbb R^n
\mbox{ is a light segment}
\right\}
\le
2\|u\|_{L^\infty(\mathbb R^n)}.
$$
In dimension $n=2$, \eqref{logsup} rules out unbounded light rays.}
<!-- end remark-9 -->
<!-- remark-10: body_md -->
{\em We clarify a few conventions we shall adopt for the rest of the paper. 
\begin{itemize}
\item Here, as in all the rest of the paper we identify $u$ with its locally Lipschitz continuous representative. This allows for no ambiguity occurring in the definitions of the light segments and in (\ref{defilight}) and in (\ref{language1}) and (\ref{language3}). \item The situation is slightly different for (\ref{language2}). In the definition of $\mathcal I_u$ in (\ref{language2}),
the notation $|Du(z)|$ refers to the precise representative
of the scalar function $|Du|$, defined by 
\eqn{esilimite}
$$
|Du(z)|:=  
\begin{cases} 
\lim_{r\to 0} \, (|Du|)_{B_r(z)} & \mbox{if this limit exists}\\
0 & \mbox{otherwise}. 
\end{cases}
$$
In this sense the precise representative is defined everywhere and the definition of $\mathcal{I}_{u}$ automatically discards the points where the limit in (\ref{esilimite})$_1$ does not exist. Later on, we shall assume higher regularity properties on $Du$ that will allow us to better clarify the structure of $\mathcal{I}_{u}$.
\item If $z$ is a Lebesgue point of the weak gradient $Du$,
we denote its precise value by $Du(z)$. Then $z$ is
also a Lebesgue point of $|Du|$, and
\[
\lim_{r\to 0}(|Du|)_{B_r(z)}
=
|\lim_{r\to 0}(Du)_{B_r(z)}|.
\]
Thus the scalar representative in (\ref{esilimite})
agrees with the modulus of the vector representative
at such points. The converse implication between
the two Lebesgue-point properties is generally false.
 \item Moreover, we shall denote by $D_{\mathrm{cl}}u(z)$ the classical, pointwise differential of $u$ at $z$, whenever it exists. We recall that whenever $z$ is both a Lebesgue point for $Du$ and a point of classical differentiability, then the precise representative of $Du$ at $z$ and the classical differential do coincide, i.e., $Du(z)=D_{\mathrm{cl}}u(z)$. 
 \end{itemize}}
<!-- end remark-10 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lemmaraggi}
Let \(\overline{xy}\) be a light segment, oriented so that
\(u(x)>u(y)\). Then every point
$
z\in\overline{xy}\setminus\{x,y\}
$
is both a classical differentiability point of \(u\) and a Lebesgue
point of the weak gradient \(Du\), and moreover 
$$
Du(z)=D_{\mathrm{cl}}u(z)=\frac{x-y}{|x-y|}.
$$
If either endpoint \(x\) or \(y\) is a Lebesgue point of \(Du\), then
\(u\) is classically differentiable at that endpoint and the classical
differential agrees with the precise representative of \(Du\).
In particular,
\eqn{aff.3}
$$
\begin{cases}
\displaystyle |Du(z)|=1
&\mbox{for every } z\in\overline{xy}\setminus\{x,y\}
\\[1.5mm]
\displaystyle |Du(z)|=1
&\mbox{if \(z \in \{x,y\}\) is a Lebesgue point of \(Du\).}
\end{cases}
$$
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
All balls appearing below are taken sufficiently small
to be compactly contained in $\Omega$. By the argument in \cite[Lemma 10]{fm02}, $u$ is
classically differentiable at every interior point $z$
of $\overline{xy}$ and, since $u(x)>u(y)$,
\eqn{valore}
$$
D_{\mathrm{cl}}u(z)=\frac{x-y}{|x-y|}.
$$
Moreover, we have 
\eqn{aff.0}
$$
u(x+t_2(y-x))-u(x+t_1(y-x))=-|x-y|(t_2-t_1),\qquad 0\le t_1\le t_2\le1.
$$
We next show that every such \(z\) is also a Lebesgue point of the
weak gradient \(Du\) (that we identify via its precise representative) and that $Du(z)=D_{\mathrm{cl}}u(z)$. Fix
$
z\in\overline{xy}\setminus\{x,y\}
$
and set
$
q:=D_{\mathrm{cl}}u(z)$ so that $ |q|=1$,
and consider the affine map 
$
\ell(w):=\langle q,w-z\rangle+u(z)
$
for every $w \in \mathbb R^n$. 
Integration by parts gives 
\begin{flalign*}
|(Du-q)_{B_r(z)}| & =\frac{1}{|B_r(z)|} \left|\int_{\partial B_r(z)}
(u-\ell)\nu_r\,\textnormal{d}\mathcal H^{n-1} \right|\le\frac{\mathcal H^{n-1}(\partial B_r(z))}
{|B_r(z)|}\sup_{\partial B_r(z)}|u-\ell | =\frac{o(r)}{r},
\end{flalign*}
where $\nu_r(w):=(w-z)/r$ is the outward Euclidean unit
normal to $\partial B_r(z)$, and therefore
$
(Du)_{B_r(z)}\to q
$
as $r\to 0$. 
Using $\lvert Du\rvert\le1$ and $\lvert q\rvert=1$, we obtain
$$
\mathop{\int\hskip -1,05em -\, \!\!\!}\nolimits_{B_r(z)}|Du-q|^2\,\textnormal{d}w=\mathop{\int\hskip -1,05em -\, \!\!\!}\nolimits_{B_r(z)}\left(
|Du|^2+|q|^2-2\langle Du,q\rangle\right)\,dw\leq 2\left(1-\langle (Du)_{B_r(z)},q\rangle\right)\to 0.
$$
Thus \(z\) is a Lebesgue point of \(Du\), and its precise value is the one displayed in (\ref{valore}). 
To prove (\ref{aff.3})$_2$ and differentiability at the endpoints, we now consider an endpoint, say $x$, which is a Lebesgue
point of $Du$. Since $u$ is locally Lipschitz, the argument
in \cite[proof of Theorem 6.2]{eg15} yields
\eqn{contr}
$$
\lim_{\varrho \to 0} \frac{1}{\varrho}\mathop{\int\hskip -1,05em -\, \!\!\!}\nolimits_{B_{\varrho}(x)}\lvert u(z)-u(x)-\langle Du(x),z-x\rangle\rvert\,{\rm d}z=0.
$$
In our setting this implies classical differentiability at $x$. By contradiction, we assume that there is $\delta\in(0,1)$ and a sequence of vectors $\{h_{k}\}_{k\in \mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}$ with $\varrho_{k}:=\lvert h_{k}\rvert\to 0$ such that
\eqn{aff.1}
$$
\lvert u(x+h_{k})-u(x)-\langle Du(x),h_{k}\rangle\rvert>\delta\lvert h_{k}\rvert\qquad \mbox{for all} \ \ k\in \mathbb{N}.
$$
Set $x_{k}:=x+h_{k}$. For any $z\in B_{\delta\varrho_{k}/4}(x_{k})$ we bound
\begin{eqnarray}\label{aff.2}
\lvert u(z)-u(x)-\langle Du(x),z-x\rangle\rvert&\ge&\lvert u(x_{k})-u(x)-\langle Du(x),x_{k}-x\rangle\rvert\nonumber \\
&&-\lvert u(z)-u(x_{k})\rvert-\lvert \langle Du(x),x_{k}-z\rangle\rvert\nonumber \\
&\stackrel{\eqref{aff.1}}{\ge}&\delta\lvert h_{k}\rvert-2\lvert x_{k}-z\rvert\ge  \delta\varrho_{k}/2.
\end{eqnarray}
As for all $k\in \mathbb{N}$ it is $B_{\delta\varrho_{k}/4}(x_{k})\subset B_{2\varrho_{k}}(x)$, we find
\begin{flalign*}
&\frac{1}{2\varrho_{k}}\mathop{\int\hskip -1,05em -\, \!\!\!}\nolimits_{B_{2\varrho_{k}}(x)}\lvert u(z)-u(x)-\langle Du(x),z-x\rangle\rvert\,{\rm d}z\nonumber \\
&\qquad \quad \ge\frac{1}{\varrho_{k}}\left(\frac{\delta}{2^{4}}\right)^{n}\mathop{\int\hskip -1,05em -\, \!\!\!}\nolimits_{B_{\frac{\delta\varrho_{k}}{4}}(x_{k})}\lvert u(z)-u(x)-\langle Du(x),z-x\rangle\rvert\,{\rm d}z\stackrel{\eqref{aff.2}}{\ge}\frac{\delta^{n+1}}{2^{5n}}>0,
\end{flalign*}
which contradicts (\ref{contr}). Hence $u$ is classically differentiable at every Lebesgue point of $Du$, and the classical differential agrees there with the precise representative of $Du$. We look back at \eqref{aff.0}, and notice that
$$
\frac{u(x+t(y-x))-u(x)}{t\lvert x-y\rvert}=-1\qquad \mbox{for all} \ \ t\in (0,1),
$$
which, combined with the classical differentiability of $u$ at $x$, implies that $(\langle Du(x),(y-x)\rangle)/\lvert x-y\rvert=-1$. Similarly, if also $y$ is a Lebesgue point of $Du$, we gain that $(\langle Du(y),(y-x)\rangle)/\lvert x-y\rvert=-1$. Recalling that $\|Du\|_{L^\infty(\Omega)}\le1$ we deduce that (\ref{aff.3})$_2$ holds, and the proof is complete.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- remark-11: body_md -->
{\em 
 For later use, observe that the vector field
$$
\partial H(D\mathcal w_\varepsilon^-)=\frac{D\mathcal w_\varepsilon^-}{\sqrt{1-|D\mathcal w_\varepsilon^-|^2}}
$$
is smooth in $\textnormal{\texttt{B}}_{0}\setminus\{0\}$; hence \eqref{a.8} also holds
distributionally on every open set $U\Subset \textnormal{\texttt{B}}_{0}\setminus\{0\}$.}
<!-- end remark-11 -->
<!-- remark-12: body_md -->
\emph{In \eqref{mmmmm}, the Lorentz condition is in force only in dimension $n\ge 3$, while for $n=2$ we assume the (slightly stronger) $L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\Omega)\equiv L_{\operatorname{loc}}(1,1)(\Omega)$. This is due to the fact that for $s>1$, and any fixed ball $B\Subset \Omega$, the H\"older-type bound $\lVert fg \rVert_{L^{1}(B)}\lesssim_{n,s}\lVert f \rVert_{L(1,s)(B)}\lVert g \rVert_{L^{\infty}(B)}$ fails already for $f\in L(1,s)(B)$ and $g=1$. }
<!-- end remark-12 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
If \(\mathcal S_{\texttt{i},u}=\varnothing\), there is nothing to
prove. We therefore fix 
$z\in\mathcal S_{\texttt{i},u}.$



{\em Step 1: A close-to-lightness estimate}.
We may choose a light segment
$\overline{y_zx_z}$
such that \(z\) is its midpoint, and label its endpoints so that
$u(y_z)>u(x_z)$.
Set
$\textnormal{\texttt{l}}_z:=|x_z-y_z|>0$,
$\textnormal{\texttt{e}}_z:=(y_z-x_z)/\textnormal{\texttt{l}}_z$,
so that
$y_z=z+\textnormal{\texttt{l}}_z\textnormal{\texttt{e}}_z/2$,
$x_z=z-\textnormal{\texttt{l}}_z\textnormal{\texttt{e}}_z/2$,
and
$u(y_z)=u(x_z)+\textnormal{\texttt{l}}_z$.
Fix
$
0<\varrho<\textnormal{\texttt{l}}_z/2^{3}.
$
We now decompose
$$
x=z+t\textnormal{\texttt{e}}_z+x_\perp, \qquad |t|<\varrho, \qquad \langle x_\perp,\textnormal{\texttt{e}}_z\rangle=0, \qquad |x_\perp|<\varrho,
$$
and define
$$
\begin{cases}
B_\varrho^{n-1}:=
\{x_\perp\in\textnormal{\texttt{e}}_z^\perp:|x_\perp|<\varrho\}\\[1mm]
\mathcal C_\varrho(z):=\left\{z+t\textnormal{\texttt{e}}_z+x_\perp:|t|<\varrho,\ x_\perp\in B_\varrho^{n-1}\right\}
\Subset B_{2\varrho}(z).
\end{cases}
$$
The notation here is obvious: $\textnormal{\texttt{e}}_z^\perp$ denotes the space orthogonal to $\textnormal{\texttt{e}}_z$. 
The \(1\)-Lipschitz regularity of \(u\), together with the elementary
inequality
$\sqrt{a^2+b^2}\le a+b^2/(2a)$ valid whenever \(a>0\),
gives
\eqn{cc.0}
$$\left|u(x)-\left(u(x_z)+\frac{\textnormal{\texttt{l}}_z}{2}+t\right)\right|
\le\frac{4|x_\perp|^2}{3\textnormal{\texttt{l}}_z}
\qquad
\mbox{for every }x\in\mathcal C_\varrho(z).
$$
Indeed,
\begin{flalign*}
u(x)
&\le
u(x_z)+|x-x_z|=u(x_z)+
\sqrt{\left(t+\frac{\textnormal{\texttt{l}}_z}{2}\right)^2
+|x_\perp|^2}
\\
&\le
u(x_z)+t+\frac{\textnormal{\texttt{l}}_z}{2}+\frac{|x_\perp|^2}{2t+\textnormal{\texttt{l}}_z}\le
u(x_z)+t+\frac{\textnormal{\texttt{l}}_z}{2}+\frac{4|x_\perp|^2}{3\textnormal{\texttt{l}}_z},
\end{flalign*}
and, similarly,
\begin{flalign*}
u(x)&\ge u(y_z)-|y_z-x|=u(x_z)+\textnormal{\texttt{l}}_z-
\sqrt{\left(\frac{\textnormal{\texttt{l}}_z}{2}-t\right)^2+
|x_\perp|^2}
\\
&\ge
u(x_z)+t+\frac{\textnormal{\texttt{l}}_z}{2}-
\frac{|x_\perp|^2}{\textnormal{\texttt{l}}_z-2t}
\ge u(x_z)+t+\frac{\textnormal{\texttt{l}}_z}{2}-\frac{4|x_\perp|^2}{3\textnormal{\texttt{l}}_z}.
\end{flalign*}
Merging the inequalities in the last two displays leads to
\eqref{cc.0}. Next, for every \(x_\perp\in B_\varrho^{n-1}\), define
$
\mathcal f_{x_\perp}(t)
:=
u(z+t\textnormal{\texttt{e}}_z+x_\perp) $ for 
$t\in[-\varrho,\varrho].$
Since \(u\) is \(1\)-Lipschitz, \(\mathcal f_{x_\perp}\) is
\(1\)-Lipschitz, and hence absolutely continuous, for every
\(x_\perp\in B_\varrho^{n-1}\). Moreover, by the slicing properties of
Sobolev functions, for a.e. \(x_\perp\in B_\varrho^{n-1}\),
$
\mathcal f_{x_\perp}'(t)=\left\langle Du(z+t\textnormal{\texttt{e}}_z+x_\perp),\textnormal{\texttt{e}}_z \right\rangle
$
for a.e. \(t\in(-\varrho,\varrho)\). By \eqref{cc.0},
$$
\int_{-\varrho}^{\varrho}\left(1-\mathcal f_{x_\perp}'(t)\right)\,{\rm d}t
=
2\varrho-\left(\mathcal f_{x_\perp}(\varrho)
-
\mathcal f_{x_\perp}(-\varrho)\right)\le\frac{8|x_\perp|^2}{3\textnormal{\texttt{l}}_z}.
$$
Since $1-|Du|^2\le2(1-|Du|)\le2(1-\mathcal f_{x_\perp}'),$
we obtain the following {\em close-to-lightness} estimate:
\eqn{cc.2}
$$
\int_{-\varrho}^{\varrho}
\left(1-|Du(z+t\textnormal{\texttt{e}}_z+x_\perp)|^2\right)\,{\rm d}t
\le\frac{16|x_\perp|^2}{3\textnormal{\texttt{l}}_z}.
$$
\medskip

{\em Step 2: Consequences of higher integrability}.
Define
\eqn{choice}
$$
\mathcal h_{u;\varrho}^{q}(x_\perp)
:=\mathop{\int\hskip -1,05em -\, \!\!\!}\nolimits_{-\varrho}^{\ \ \varrho}\mathcal h_u(z+t\textnormal{\texttt{e}}_z+x_\perp)^q\,{\rm d}t.
$$
Since
$
s\longmapsto s^{-q/2}
$
is convex on \((0,\infty)\), Jensen's inequality and \eqref{cc.2}
give
\eqn{higher.light}
$$
\mathcal h_{u;\varrho}^{q}(x_\perp)
\ge
\left(\mathop{\int\hskip -1,05em -\, \!\!\!}\nolimits_{-\varrho}^{\ \ \varrho}\left(1-|Du(z+t\textnormal{\texttt{e}}_z+x_\perp)|^2\right)\,{\rm d}t\right)^{-q/2}
\ge \left(\frac{3\varrho\textnormal{\texttt{l}}_z}{8|x_\perp|^2}\right)^{q/2}
$$
for a.e.
\(x_\perp\in B_\varrho^{n-1}\setminus\{0\}\). Consequently, for every \(0<\delta<\varrho\), Fubini's theorem and
the inclusion $\mathcal C_\varrho(z)\Subset B_{2\varrho}(z)$ yield
\begin{align}\label{higher.int.lower}
\int_{B_{2\varrho}(z)}\mathcal h_u^q\,{\rm d}x
&\ge
\int_{\mathcal C_\varrho(z)}\mathcal h_u^q\,{\rm d}x
\nonumber =2\varrho\int_{B_\varrho^{n-1}}\mathcal h_{u;\varrho}^{q}(x_\perp)\,{\rm d}x_\perp
\nonumber\\
&\ge \frac{\textnormal{\texttt{l}}_z^{q/2}\varrho^{1+q/2}}{c}\int_{\{\delta<|x_\perp|<\varrho\}}\frac{dx_\perp}{|x_\perp|^q}=c(n,q)\textnormal{\texttt{l}}_z^{q/2}\varrho^{1+q/2}\int_\delta^\varrho s^{n-2-q}\,\mathrm{d} s.
\end{align}
Assume first that
$
q\ge n-1.
$
Letting \(\delta\downarrow0\) in \eqref{higher.int.lower}, we obtain
$$
\int_{B_{2\varrho}(z)}\mathcal h_u^q\,{\rm d}x=\infty,
$$
which is a contradiction. 
Thus
$
\mathcal S_{\texttt{i},u}=\varnothing
$ and 
hence
$
\mathcal S_{u}=\varnothing=\mathcal{K}_{u}=\overline{\mathcal S_{u}}=\varnothing. 
$ This proves the second conclusion in (\ref{nicchia4}). 
Assume now that
$
1\le q<n-1.
$
Letting \(\delta\downarrow0\) in \eqref{higher.int.lower}, we find
\eqn{findy}
$$
\int_{B_{2\varrho}(z)}\mathcal h_u^q\,{\rm d}x \ge
\frac{\textnormal{\texttt{l}}_z^{q/2}\varrho^{1+q/2}}{c(n,q)}\int_0^\varrho s^{n-2-q}\,\mathrm{d} s=\frac{\textnormal{\texttt{l}}_z^{q/2}\varrho^{n-q/2}}{c(n,q)}.
$$
Define
$$
E_q
:=\left\{
x\in\mathbb R^n: \limsup_{r\to0}\, r^{q/2-n}\int_{B_r(x)}\mathcal h_u^q\,{\rm d}x>0\right\}.
$$
Estimate (\ref{findy}) shows that
$
\mathcal S_{\texttt{i},u}\subset E_q.
$
Since \(\mathcal h_u^q\in L^1_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n)\) we have 
$
\mathcal H^{n-q/2}(E_q)=0 
$ by \cite[Theorem 2.10]{eg15}, 
and therefore 
$
\mathcal H^{n-q/2}(\mathcal S_{\texttt{i},u})=0.
$ This implies, via Lemma \ref{endpoints} below, that $
\mathcal H^{n-q/2}
(\mathcal S_{u})
=0
$ and the proof is complete.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{endpoints}
Let $U\subset\mathbb R^n$ be open and let
$u\in W^{1,\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(U)$ be weakly spacelike.
For every $s>0$,
$
\mathcal H^s(\mathcal S_u)=0$ if and only if 
$\mathcal H^s(\mathcal S_{\texttt{i},u})=0$.
In particular,
$
\dim_{\mathcal H}\mathcal S_u=\dim_{\mathcal H}\mathcal S_{\texttt{i},u}.
$
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
Since $\mathcal S_{\texttt{i},u}\subset\mathcal S_u$,
it is sufficient to prove that
$\mathcal H^s(\mathcal S_{\texttt{i},u})=0$ implies
$
\mathcal H^s(\mathcal S_u)=0$, and therefore we assume that 
\eqn{walter}
$$\mathcal H^s(\mathcal S_{\texttt{i},u})=0.$$
The idea of the proof is that every midpoint of a light segment
determines its two endpoints once the length is fixed.
Since the directions depend Lipschitz continuously on the midpoints,
this association has Lipschitz character too.
This implies that the endpoints do not spread too much,
so that the information in (\ref{walter}) extends to the endpoints too.
Choose a countable covering
$
U=\bigcup_{j\ge1}B_j
$
by balls $B_j\Subset U$.
For $\delta>0$, let $M_{\delta,j}$ be the set of midpoints
of light segments of length $2\delta$ contained in $B_j$. 
Clearly,
$
M_{\delta,j}\subset\mathcal S_{\texttt{i},u}.
$
For $y\in M_{\delta,j}$, choose the corresponding increasing
unit direction $\textnormal{\texttt{e}}_y$, i.e.,
$
u(y\pm\delta \textnormal{\texttt{e}}_y)=u(y)\pm\delta$.
This direction is unique by Lemma \ref{lemmaraggi}.
Next, consider the endpoint maps
$
F_{\delta,j}^\pm:M_{\delta,j}\to\mathcal S_u$
defined by
$
F_{\delta,j}^\pm(y):=y\pm\delta \textnormal{\texttt{e}}_y.
$
Upon extending the restriction $u|_{B_j}$ to 
$1$-Lipschitz extension to $\mathbb R^n$, we can use 
 \cite[Lemma 16, Remark 17 and the proof of Lemma 22]{cfm02}, to deduce that the dependence 
of $\textnormal{\texttt{e}}_y$ 
on $y\in M_{\delta,j}$ is Lipschitz continuously
on $y$, for every fixed $\delta>0$. It follows that the maps $F_{\delta,j}^\pm$ are Lipschitz too. Now, every point $p\in \mathcal S_u$ is an endpoint of a
nondegenerate light subsegment $\ell$ contained in some $B_j$ and, up to shorten $\ell$, we can assume $\,{\rm diam}(\ell)=2/k$ for some integer $k$. The midpoint of $\ell$ belongs to $M_{1/k,j}$.
As $p$ was generic, we conclude with 
$$
\mathcal S_u
\subset
\bigcup_{j,k\ge1}
\left(
F_{1/k,j}^+(M_{1/k,j})
\cup
F_{1/k,j}^-(M_{1/k,j})
\right).
$$
By (\ref{walter}), since $M_{1/k,j}\subset\mathcal S_{\texttt{i},u}$,
the Lipschitz continuity of the maps $F_{1/k,j}^{\pm}$ yields
$$
\mathcal H^s\left(F_{1/k,j}^\pm(M_{1/k,j})\right)=0
\qquad
\mbox{for every }j,k\ge1.
$$
Countable subadditivity of Hausdorff measure then yields
$
\mathcal H^s(\mathcal S_u)=0.
$
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- theorem-12: body_md -->
\label{sobre1}
Let \(u\in W^{1,\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n)\) be weakly spacelike. Let $p\geq 1$  and $m>0$ such that $
\mathcal h_u^m\in W^{1,p}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n)
$ and 
\eqn{notache}
$$
\frac{n-1}{1+m}\le p<n-1.
$$
Then
$
\mathcal S_{\texttt{i},u}=\mathcal S_{u}=\mathcal{K}_{u}=\varnothing.
$
<!-- end theorem-12 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
Note that assumption (\ref{notache}) automatically implies $n\geq 3$. 
Assume by contradiction that
\(z\in\mathcal S_{\texttt{i},u}\). Using the notation and the
close-to-lightness estimate from the proof of Theorem
\ref{intre}, restart from (\ref{higher.light}) (there we replace $q$ by $m$ and observe that the whole argument remains unchanged). 
Then $|x_\perp|^{-m} \lesssim \mathcal h_{u;\varrho}^{m}$
for a.e.
\(x_\perp\in B_\varrho^{n-1}\setminus\{0\}\); integrating then yields
\eqn{lefty}
$$
\int_{B_\varrho^{n-1}}
(\mathcal h_{u;\varrho}^{m})^{p^*}\,{\rm d}x_\perp
\ge
\frac1c
\int_0^\varrho s^{n-2-mp^*}\,\mathrm{d} s,
\qquad
p^*:=\frac{(n-1)p}{n-1-p}.
$$
By the assumption on \(\mathcal h_u^m\) and Fubini's theorem,
$
\mathcal h_{u;\varrho}^{m}\in W^{1,p}(B_\varrho^{n-1}).
$
Sobolev embedding in dimension \(n-1\) gives
$
\mathcal h_{u;\varrho}^{m}\in L^{p^*}(B_\varrho^{n-1}),
$ so that the last integral in (\ref{lefty}) is finite. 
On the other hand, the assumption
$
(1+m)p\ge n-1
$
is equivalent to
$
mp^*\ge n-1,
$
and hence the last integral diverges, a contradiction. Therefore
$
\mathcal S_{\texttt{i},u}=\varnothing,
$
and consequently
$
\mathcal S_{u}=\mathcal{K}_{u}=\varnothing.
$
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- theorem-13: body_md -->
\label{sobre2}
Let
\(u\in W^{1,\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n)\) be weakly spacelike.
Assume that
$
\log\mathcal h_u\in W^{1,p}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n)$ for some 
$p\ge n-1.$
Then
$
\mathcal S_{\texttt{i},u}=\mathcal S_{u}=
\mathcal{K}_{u}=\varnothing.
$
<!-- end theorem-13 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
Assume by contradiction there exists 
$
z\in\mathcal S_{\texttt{i},u}.
$
This time it is natural to define
$$
\ell\mathcal h_{u;\varrho}(x_\perp)
:=\mathop{\int\hskip -1,05em -\, \!\!\!}\nolimits_{-\varrho}^{\ \ \varrho}\log\mathcal h_u(z+t\textnormal{\texttt{e}}_z+x_\perp)\,{\rm d}t,
$$
which replaces the choice in (\ref{choice}). 
Again Fubini's theorem yields $
\ell\mathcal h_{u;\varrho}
\in W^{1,p}(B_\varrho^{n-1}).$
Moreover, Jensen's inequality and \eqref{cc.2} give
\eqn{zz.0}
$$
\ell\mathcal h_{u;\varrho}(x_\perp)
\ge
\log\left(
\frac{1}{|x_\perp|}
\sqrt{\frac{3\varrho\textnormal{\texttt{l}}_z}{8}}
\right)
$$
for a.e. \(x_\perp\in B_\varrho^{n-1}\setminus\{0\}\). When  \(p>n-1\), Morrey's embedding theorem gives a locally bounded
continuous representative of
\(\ell\mathcal h_{u;\varrho}\), contradicting \eqref{zz.0} as
\(x_\perp\to0\). Therefore it remains to consider the borderline case \(p=n-1\). 
If \(n=2\), then \(p=1\), and $
\ell\mathcal h_{u;\varrho}\in W^{1,1}(-\varrho,\varrho) $
has an absolutely continuous, bounded representative.
This again contradicts \eqref{zz.0}. We may therefore assume that \(n\ge3\), so that \(p=n-1>1\). If
$
\left\|
\partial_{x_\perp}\ell\mathcal h_{u;\varrho}
\right\|_{L^p(B_\varrho^{n-1})}=0,
$
then $\ell\mathcal h_{u;\varrho}$ is constant almost everywhere,
contradicting \eqref{zz.0}.
Otherwise we apply 
\eqref{trudinger.mean} of Lemma \ref{trudinger.lemma},
in dimension $n-1=p$, to obtain
\eqn{zz.1}
$$
\int_{B_\varrho^{n-1}}\exp\left\{c_*\left|\ell\mathcal h_{u;\varrho}-(\ell\mathcal h_{u;\varrho})_{B_\varrho^{n-1}}
\right|^{\frac{p}{p-1}}
\right\}\,{\rm d}x_\perp
<\infty,
$$
where 
$c_*>0$ depends on $
n,p,
\left\|
\partial_{x_\perp}
\ell\mathcal h_{u;\varrho}
\right\|_{L^p(B_\varrho^{n-1})}$. 
Choose \(\varrho_\perp\in(0,\varrho)\) so small that
\[
\frac12
\log\left(
\frac1{\varrho_\perp}
\sqrt{\frac{3\varrho\textnormal{\texttt{l}}_z}{8}}
\right)
\ge
(\ell\mathcal h_{u;\varrho})_{B_\varrho^{n-1}}.
\]
Then (\ref{zz.0}) implies that
$$
\left|\ell\mathcal h_{u;\varrho}(x_\perp)-(\ell\mathcal h_{u;\varrho})_{B_\varrho^{n-1}}\right|
\ge
\frac12\log\left(\frac1{|x_\perp|}\sqrt{\frac{3\varrho\textnormal{\texttt{l}}_z}{8}}\right)
$$
holds for a.e. \(x_\perp\in B_{\varrho_\perp}^{n-1}\). 
Set
$ c_1:=c_*2^{-p/(p-1)}$ and $c_2:=\sqrt{3\varrho\textnormal{\texttt{l}}_z/8}.$
Using polar coordinates and then changing variables via 
$
t=\log (c_2/s),
$
we obtain
\begin{flalign*}
\int_{B_\varrho^{n-1}}\exp\left\{c_*\left|\ell\mathcal h_{u;\varrho}-(\ell\mathcal h_{u;\varrho})_{B_\varrho^{n-1}}\right|^{\frac{p}{p-1}}
\right\}\,{\rm d}x_\perp
&\ge
\frac1c
\int_0^{\varrho_\perp}
s^{n-2}
\exp\left\{
c_1
\left[
\log\left(\frac{c_2}{s}\right)
\right]^{\frac{p}{p-1}}
\right\}\,\mathrm{d} s
\\
&=
\frac{c_2^{n-1}}{c}
\int_{\log(c_2/\varrho_\perp)}^\infty
\exp\left\{
c_1t^{\frac{p}{p-1}}-(n-1)t
\right\}\,\textnormal{d}t.
\end{flalign*}
Since $
p/(p-1)>1,$
the last integral is infinite, contradicting \eqref{zz.1}.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- theorem-14: body_md -->
\label{cap.t.1}
 Let $u\in W^{1,\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ be a weakly spacelike function such that $Du\in W^{1,p}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n;\mathbb R^n)$, with $1\le p<n$. It holds that
 \begin{itemize}
 \item  $\mathcal{K}_{u}
=
\mathcal S_{u}\,\dot\cup\,\mathcal {L}_{u}.$
 \item Up to zero $p$-capacity sets, every point of $\mathcal {L}_{u}$ with $\lvert Du\rvert<1$ is a discontinuity point of the precise representative of $\lvert Du\rvert$.
\item If either 
$
\mathcal h_{u}^{m}\in W^{1,p}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n)
$ for some $m >0$, 
or
$
\log \mathcal h_u\in W^{1,p}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n) 
$ holds,  then 
\eqn{zerocap}
$$
\begin{cases}
\displaystyle
\textnormal{Cap}_{p}(\mathcal I_{u})=0 \quad  \mbox{and}  \quad  \dim_{\mathcal H}(\mathcal I_{u})\leq n-p
\\[1mm]
\displaystyle
\textnormal{Cap}_{p}(\mathcal S_{u})=0 \quad \mbox{and}  \quad  \dim_{\mathcal H}(\mathcal S_{u})\leq n-p. 
\end{cases}
$$
If, in addition,
\eqn{ndeg}
$$
\inf\left\{\,{\rm diam}(\ell):\ell\mbox{ is a light ray}\right\}>0,
$$
then
\eqn{nicchia5}
$$
\mathcal{K}_{u}=\mathcal S_{u},
\qquad
\mathcal {L}_{u}=\varnothing,
\qquad
\textnormal{Cap}_{p}(\mathcal{K}_{u})=0,
\qquad
\dim_{\mathcal H} \mathcal{K}_{u}\le n-p.
$$
\end{itemize}
<!-- end theorem-14 -->
<!-- remark-13: body_md -->
\label{loc.rem}
\emph{In the following we shall extensively make use of the usual $p$-Capacity in $\mathbb R^n$ as described in \cite[Section 4]{eg15}, and therefore with $1\leq p<n$. The basic relevant property \cite[Theorem 4.19]{eg15} is of course that if a function $v\in W^{1,p}(A)$, where $A\subset \mathbb R^n$ is any open subset, then there exists an exceptional Borel set $E\equiv E(v)\subset A$ such that $\textnormal{Cap}_{p}(E)=0$ and $z$ is a Lebesgue point for $v$ whenever $z\not \in E$. When proving that a certain set $\mathcal U$ has zero $p$-Capacity by standard properties of the $p$-Capacity \cite[Theorem 4.15 (viii)]{eg15} it will be sufficient to prove that $\textnormal{Cap}_{p}(\mathcal U\cap B)=0$ for every ball $B\subset \mathbb R^n$. We shall furthermore denote $\mathcal {U}^B:= \mathcal U \cap B$ and $\mathcal {U}^{\bar B}:= \mathcal U \cap \bar B$. }
<!-- end remark-13 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{blow-up}
Let $u\in W^{1,\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n)$ be weakly
spacelike and assume that
$\log\mathcal h_u\in L^1_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n)$.
For every $z\in\mathbb R^n$, one has
\eqn{unalog}
$$
\lim_{r\to0}(\mathcal h_u^{-1})_{B_r(z)}=0
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\lim_{r\to0}(\log\mathcal h_u)_{B_r(z)}=\infty.
$$
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
The integrability assumption implies that
$0<\mathcal h_u^{-1}\le1$ almost everywhere.
By Jensen's inequality applied to the convex function
$t\mapsto-\log t$,
\[
(\log\mathcal h_u)_{B_r(z)}
=
(-\log\mathcal h_u^{-1})_{B_r(z)}
\ge
-\log\bigl((\mathcal h_u^{-1})_{B_r(z)}\bigr).
\]
The right-hand side tends to $+\infty$ as $r\to0$,
which proves the assertion.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{cap.t}
Let $1\le p<n$ and let
$u\in W^{1,\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n)$ be weakly spacelike.
Assume that
$
Du\in W^{1,p}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n;\mathbb R^n)$ and $
\log\mathcal h_u\in W^{1,p}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n).
$
Then \eqref{zerocap} holds.
In particular, the conclusion holds if the assumption on
$\log\mathcal h_u$ is replaced by
$\mathcal h_u^m\in W^{1,p}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n)$
for some $m>0$.
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
It suffices to prove the assertions about capacity.
By Remark \ref{loc.rem}, fix an arbitrary ball
$B\subset\mathbb R^n$ and prove that
$
\textnormal{Cap}_{p}(\mathcal I_u^B)=\textnormal{Cap}_{p}(\mathcal S_u^B)=0.
$ Let $E_0:=E_0(Du)$ and $E_1:=E_1(\log\mathcal h_u)$
be the exceptional sets of non-Lebesgue points from
Remark \ref{loc.rem}. Then
$
\textnormal{Cap}_{p}(E_0^B)=\textnormal{Cap}_{p}(E_1^B)=0.
$
If $z\in\mathcal I_u^B\setminus E_0^B$, then $z$ is
a Lebesgue point of $Du$, hence also of $|Du|$, and
$|Du(z)|=1$.
Since $t\mapsto\sqrt{1-t^2}$ is H\"older continuous
on $[0,1]$, it follows that
\[
\lim_{r\to0}(\mathcal h_u^{-1})_{B_r(z)}
=\sqrt{1-|Du(z)|^2}=0.
\]
By \eqref{unalog} we infer 
$
\lim_{r\to0}(\log\mathcal h_u)_{B_r(z)}=\infty,
$
so $z\in E_1^B$. Therefore, $
\mathcal I_u^B\subset E_0^B\cup E_1^B.
$
By \eqref{aff.3}$_{1,2}$, every point of a light segment
which is a Lebesgue point of $Du$ satisfies $|Du|=1$,
including the endpoints. Hence
$
\mathcal S_u^B\setminus E_0^B\subset\mathcal I_u^B,
$
and consequently
$
\mathcal I_u^B\cup\mathcal S_u^B
\subset E_0^B\cup E_1^B.
$
Monotonicity and subadditivity of the $p$-capacity give
the desired conclusion on $B$.
Since $B$ is arbitrary, Remark \ref{loc.rem}
yields \eqref{zerocap}. Finally, if
$\mathcal h_u^m\in W^{1,p}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n)$
for some $m>0$, then $\mathcal h_u^m\ge1$ almost everywhere.
Composition with the Lipschitz function
$t\mapsto m^{-1}\log\max\{1,t\}$ gives
$\log\mathcal h_u\in W^{1,p}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n)$,
so the preceding argument applies.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:structure-Kmu}
Let
$
u\in W^{1,\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n)
$
be weakly spacelike. Then
\eqn{structure.K}
$$
\mathcal K_u
=
\mathcal S_u\,\dot\cup\,\mathcal L_u.
$$
In particular, if $1\le p<n$ and
$
\textnormal{Cap}_{p}(\mathcal S_u)=0,
$
then
$
\textnormal{Cap}_{p}\left(
\mathcal K_u\mathbin{\triangle}\mathcal L_u
\right)
=0.
$
Assume, in addition, that
$
Du\in W^{1,p}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n;\mathbb R^n),
$
and let $E_0=E_0(Du)$ be the exceptional set from
Remark \ref{loc.rem}. Every point
$
z\in\mathcal L_u\setminus E_0
$
such that
$
|Du(z)|<1
$
is a discontinuity point of the precise representative of $|Du|$.
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
Since $\mathcal S_u\subset\mathcal K_u$ by definition and
$\mathcal S_u\cap\mathcal L_u=\varnothing$ by \eqref{vuoto},
it remains to prove the inclusions
$
\mathcal L_u\subset\mathcal K_u$ and 
$\mathcal K_u\setminus\mathcal S_u\subset\mathcal L_u.$ Let first $z\in\mathcal L_u$. There exist light rays $\ell_k$ such that
$
\,{\rm dist}(z,\ell_k)\to0$
and 
$\,{\rm diam}(\ell_k)\to0.
$
Choose $z_k\in\ell_k$ such that
$
|z-z_k|
\le
\,{\rm dist}(z,\ell_k)+1/k.
$
It follows
that $z_k\in\mathcal S_u$. Hence
$
z\in\overline{\mathcal S_u}=\mathcal K_u.
$
Conversely, let
$
z\in\mathcal K_u\setminus\mathcal S_u.
$
Choose $z_k\in\mathcal S_u$ such that $z_k\to z$, and let $\ell_k$ be
the light ray containing a light segment through $z_k$. Then
$\,{\rm dist}(z,\ell_k)\le|z-z_k|\to0.$
We claim that
$\,{\rm diam}(\ell_k)\to0.$
Otherwise, after passing to a subsequence, there exists $\rho>0$ such
that
$
\,{\rm diam}(\ell_k)\ge4\rho
$
for every $k$. Since $\ell_k$ is an interval of a straight line and
$z_k\in\ell_k$, we may choose $w_k\in\ell_k$ such that
$|w_k-z_k|=\rho.$
The segment $\overline{z_kw_k}$ is obviously a light segment. After passing to a
further subsequence,
$\rho^{-1}(w_k-z_k)\to \textnormal{\texttt{e}}$
for some $\textnormal{\texttt{e}}\in \mathbb R^n$, $|\textnormal{\texttt{e}}|=1$, and therefore
$w_k\to w:=z+\rho \textnormal{\texttt{e}}.$
By continuity of $u$,
$|u(w)-u(z)|
=|w-z|=\rho.$
Thus $\overline{zw}$ is a nondegenerate light segment, contradicting
$
z\notin\mathcal S_u.
$
Consequently,
$
\,{\rm diam}(\ell_k)\to0,
$
and hence $z\in\mathcal L_u$. This proves \eqref{structure.K}. Since
$
\mathcal K_u\mathbin{\triangle}\mathcal L_u=\mathcal S_u,
$
the capacity assertion follows immediately. Finally, let
$
z\in\mathcal L_u\setminus E_0
$
with $|Du(z)|<1$, and let $\ell_k$ be as in
\eqref{language3}. We can choose
$
\tilde z_k\in\mathcal S_{\texttt{i},u}\cap\ell_k
$
such that
$
|\tilde z_k-z|
\le
\,{\rm dist}(z,\ell_k)+\,{\rm diam}(\ell_k)+1/k.
$
Then $\tilde z_k\to z$, and Lemma \ref{lemmaraggi} gives
$
|Du(\tilde z_k)|=1.
$
Since $z\notin E_0$ and $|Du(z)|<1$, the precise representative of
$|Du|$ is discontinuous at $z$.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:nondegenerate-Kmu}
Let
$
u\in W^{1,\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n)
$
be weakly spacelike and assume that \eqref{ndeg} holds. Then
$
\mathcal K_u=\mathcal S_u
$ and $
\mathcal L_u=\varnothing.
$
In particular, if $1\le p<n$ and
$
\textnormal{Cap}_{p}(\mathcal S_u)=0,
$
then
$
\textnormal{Cap}_{p}(\mathcal K_u)=0
$ and 
$\dim_{\mathcal H}\mathcal K_u\le n-p.$
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Condition \eqref{ndeg} and the definition in (\ref{language3}) immediately
give
$\mathcal L_u=\varnothing.$
The identity
$\mathcal K_u=\mathcal S_u$
then follows from Proposition \ref{lem:structure-Kmu}. The remaining
assertions are immediate.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
The proof follows by combining the above propositions. The first two assertions follow from Proposition
\ref{lem:structure-Kmu}, which gives the disjoint decomposition
$
\mathcal{K}_{u}
=
\mathcal S_{u}\,\dot\cup\,\mathcal {L}_{u}
$
and the stated discontinuity property outside an exceptional set of
zero \(p\)-capacity. Finally, Propositions \ref{cap.t} and \ref{lem:nondegenerate-Kmu} imply the third point.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- proposition-6: body_md -->
\label{p31}
Let $u\in \mathbb{X}(\mathbb R^n)\cap C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ be a classical solution of \eqref{bi} with $\mu\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$. There exists $\gamma\equiv \gamma(n)\in (0,1/n)$ such that the intrinsic potential estimate
\begin{flalign}\label{0.10}
\mathcal h_u(x_0)^{-\gamma}
&\ge \mathop{\int\hskip -1,05em -\, \!\!\!}\nolimits_{K_r(x_0)}\mathcal h_u^{-(\gamma+1)}\,{\rm d}x
+\frac{c_n}{r^{n-2}}\int_{K_{r/2}(x_0)}\lvert D^2u\rvert^2\,{\rm d}x
\nonumber\\
&\quad-2\mathbf{PL}_{2,u}^{1}(\mu^2;x_0,r)-\mathbf{PL}_{1,u}^{1}(\mu;x_0,r)
\end{flalign}
holds for all $x_0\in\mathbb R^n$ and every $r>0$,
with a constant $c_n>0$ depending only on $n$.
Moreover, the following choice is possible:
\eqn{sceltagamma}
$$
\gamma = \frac{1}{4n}\,.
$$
<!-- end proposition-6 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
As pointed out in Remark \ref{rem0}, we may assume that $x_0=0$ and
$u(0)=0$, namely $\Phi(0)=(0,u(0))=0$. Throughout the proof, we write
$L_s:=L_s^u(0)$, $K_s:=K_s^u(0)$, and
$\ell(\Phi(x))=\ell_u(x,0)$. Proposition \ref{prop.mon} gives
\eqn{diffy}
$$
\frac{\textnormal{d}}{\textnormal{d}s} \mathbb  A(s) \leq - \frac{\textnormal{d}}{\textnormal{d}s}\mathbb  B(s) - c_{\star}\mathbb  E(s) + \mathbb  E_\mu(s) - \mathbb  F_\mu(s)
$$
for all $s\in(0,r)$, with $c_\star=c_\star(n)>0$. We leave the term $c_{\star}\mathbb  E(s)$ as it is for the moment. In the following we shall use the basic relations
 \eqn{basic}
$$
\begin{cases}
\displaystyle
\ \lvert \delta \ell\rvert_{\mathbb{L}^{n+1}}^{2}=1+\ell^{-2}\langle\mathcal{v},\Phi\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{n+1}}^{2}\vspace{1.5mm}\\ \displaystyle
\ \lvert \mathcal{h}_{u}^{-1}\delta_{n+1}\ell\rvert\le \lvert \delta \ell\rvert_{\mathbb{L}^{n+1}},
\end{cases}
$$
for which we refer to \cite[(2.9) and (3.14)]{bi23}. Set \(\varphi_s:=(s^2-\ell^2)/2\). By Lemma \ref{ibp}\footnote{Strictly speaking, we apply Lemma \ref{ibp} to a smooth approximation of
\(\varphi_s\mathds 1_{L_s}\); no boundary term appears since
\(\varphi_s=0\) on \(\partial L_s\).}, with
\(w=\mathcal h_u^{-(\gamma+1)}\mu\), and since \(\textnormal{\texttt{H}}_u=\mu\), 
$$
\int_{L_s}\varphi_s\,\delta_{n+1}w\,dA
=
\int_{L_s}\varphi_s\mathcal h_u^{-\gamma}\mu^2\,dA
+
\int_{L_s}\ell\,\mathcal h_u^{-(\gamma+1)}\mu\,\delta_{n+1}\ell\,dA.
$$
Hence, using \(0\le \varphi_s\le s^2/2\) on \(L_s\),
$$
\mathbb E_\mu(s)
\le
\frac{1+4\gamma}{8s^{n-1}}
\int_{L_s}\mathcal h_u^{-\gamma}\mu^2\,dA
+
\frac{\gamma}{s^{n+1}}
\int_{L_s}
\ell\mathcal h_u^{-\gamma}|\mu|\,
|\mathcal h_u^{-1}\delta_{n+1}\ell|\,dA .
$$
Again using (\ref{basic}) we gain
$$
\mathbb E_\mu(s)
\le\frac{1+4\gamma}{8s^{n-1}}\int_{L_{s}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\mu^{2}\,{\rm d} A+\frac{\gamma}{s^{n}}\int_{L_{s}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\lvert \mu\rvert \,{\rm d} A  +\frac{\gamma}{s^{n}}\int_{L_{s}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\lvert \mu\rvert\ell^{-1}\lvert \langle \mathcal{v},\Phi\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{n+1}}\rvert\,{\rm d} A. 
$$
By Young's inequality we continue to bound
\begin{flalign}
\notag \mathbb E_\mu(s)
&\le\frac{1+4\gamma}{8s^{n-1}}\int_{L_{s}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\mu^{2}\,{\rm d} A+\frac{\gamma}{s^{n}}\int_{L_{s}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\lvert \mu\rvert\,{\rm d} A\nonumber \\
\notag&\quad +\frac{\gamma}{2s^{n}}\int_{L_{s}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\mu^{2}\ell \,{\rm d} A+\frac{\gamma}{2s^{n}}\int_{L_{s}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\ell^{-3}\lvert \langle\Phi,\mathcal{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{n+1}}\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d} A\nonumber \\
&\le\frac{1+8\gamma}{8s^{n-1}}\int_{L_{s}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\mu^{2}\,{\rm d} A+\frac{\gamma}{s^{n}}\int_{L_{s}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\lvert \mu\rvert\,{\rm d} A+\frac{\gamma}{2s^{n}}\int_{L_{s}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\ell^{-3}\lvert \langle\Phi,\mathcal{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{n+1}}\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d} A. \label{you1}
\end{flalign}
Similarly, again by Young's inequality, we find
\eqn{you2}
$$
\lvert \mathbb F_\mu(s)\rvert\le\frac{1}{2s^{n-1}}\int_{L_{s}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\mu^{2}\,{\rm d} A+\frac{1}{2s^{n+1}}\int_{L_{s}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\ell^{-2}\lvert \langle\Phi,\mathcal{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{n+1}}\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d} A.
$$
The last two integrals appearing in (\ref{you1})-(\ref{you2}) can be estimated using the coarea formula (Lemma \ref{coa}) as follows:
\begin{flalign*}
\frac{1}{s^{n}}\int_{L_{s}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\ell^{-3}\lvert \langle\Phi,\mathcal{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{n+1}}\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d} A&=-\frac{1}{n-1}\frac{\textnormal{d}}{\textnormal{d}s}\left(s^{1-n}\int_{L_{s}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\ell^{-3}\lvert \langle\Phi,\mathcal{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{n+1}}\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d} A\right)\nonumber \\
&\quad +\frac{1}{n-1}\int_{\partial L_{s}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\ell^{-2-n}\lvert \langle\Phi,\mathcal{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{n+1}}\rvert^{2}\lvert \delta \ell\rvert_{\mathbb{L}^{n+1}}^{-1}\,{\rm d}\sigma\nonumber \\
&=-\frac{1}{n-1}\frac{\textnormal{d}}{\textnormal{d}s}\left(s^{1-n}\int_{L_{s}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\ell^{-3}\lvert \langle\Phi,\mathcal{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{n+1}}\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d} A\right)\nonumber \\
&\quad +\frac{1}{n-1}\frac{\textnormal{d}}{\textnormal{d}s}\left(\int_{L_{s}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\ell^{-2-n}\lvert \langle\Phi,\mathcal{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{n+1}}\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d} A\right),
\end{flalign*}
and 
\begin{flalign*}
\frac{1}{s^{n+1}}\int_{L_{s}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\ell^{-2}\lvert \langle\Phi,\mathcal{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{n+1}}\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d} A&= -\frac{1}{n}\frac{\textnormal{d}}{\textnormal{d}s}\left(s^{-n}\int_{L_{s}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\ell^{-2}\lvert \langle\Phi,\mathcal{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{n+1}}\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d} A\right)\nonumber \\
&\quad +\frac{1}{n}\int_{\partial L_{s}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\ell^{-2-n}\lvert \langle\Phi,\mathcal{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{n+1}}\rvert^{2}\lvert \delta \ell\rvert_{\mathbb{L}^{n+1}}^{-1}\,{\rm d}\sigma\nonumber \\
&=-\frac{1}{n}\frac{\textnormal{d}}{\textnormal{d}s}\left(s^{-n}\int_{L_{s}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\ell^{-2}\lvert \langle\Phi,\mathcal{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{n+1}}\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d} A\right)\nonumber \\
