&\quad +\frac{1}{n}\frac{\textnormal{d}}{\textnormal{d}s}\left(\int_{ L_{s}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\ell^{-2-n}\lvert \langle\Phi,\mathcal{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{n+1}}\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d} A\right).
\end{flalign*}
Combining the last four displays with (\ref{diffy}) we obtain
\begin{flalign}\label{0.4}
\frac{\textnormal{d}}{\textnormal{d}s} \mathbb  A(s)&\le - c_{\star} \mathbb  E(s)-\left(1-\frac{\gamma}{2(n-1)}-\frac{1}{2n}\right)\frac{\textnormal{d}}{\textnormal{d}s} \mathbb  B(s)\nonumber \\
&\quad -\frac{\textnormal{d}}{\textnormal{d}s}\left(\int_{L_{s}}\left(\frac{\gamma \mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\ell^{-3}}{2(n-1)s^{n-1}}+\frac{ \mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\ell^{-2}}{2ns^{n}}\right)\lvert \langle\Phi,\mathcal{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{n+1}}\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d} A\right)\nonumber \\
&\quad +\left(\frac{5+8\gamma}{8s^{n-1}}\right)\int_{L_{s}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\mu^{2}\,{\rm d} A+\frac{\gamma}{s^{n}}\int_{L_{s}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\lvert \mu\rvert\,{\rm d} A.
\end{flalign}
Next, denote
\eqn{recapp}
$$
\mathcal{A}(s):=\int_{L_{s}}\left(\frac{\gamma \ell^{-1}}{2(n-1)s^{n-1}}+\frac{1}{2ns^{n}}+\left(1-\frac{n(\gamma+1)-1}{2n(n-1)}\right)\ell^{-n}\right)\frac{\lvert \langle\Phi,\mathcal{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{n+1}}\rvert^{2}}{\ell^{2}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{\gamma}}\,{\rm d} A \geq 0.
$$
With this notation, \eqref{0.4} implies
\eqn{0.9}
$$
 \frac{\textnormal{d}}{\textnormal{d}s} \mathbb A(s)\le -c_{\star} \mathbb  E(s) -\frac{\textnormal{d}}{\textnormal{d}s} \mathcal{A}(s)
+\frac{2\lVert \mathcal{h}_{u}^{-1} \rVert_{L^{\infty}(L_{s})}^{\gamma}}{s^{n-1}}\int_{L_{s}}\mu^{2}\,{\rm d} A+\frac{\lVert \mathcal{h}_{u}^{-1} \rVert_{L^{\infty}(L_{s})}^{\gamma}}{s^{n}}\int_{L_{s}}\lvert \mu\rvert\,{\rm d} A.
$$
Since $u\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb R^n)$ is strictly spacelike, by Lemma \ref{limlem} we find 
\eqn{lim1}
$$\lim_{s\to0} \mathbb A(s)= \omega_{n}\mathcal h_u(0)^{-\gamma}$$
and, moreover, 
 $$\langle \Phi,\mathcal{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{n+1}}=-\mathcal{h}_{u}\left(u(x)-\langle x,Du(x)\rangle\right)=O(\lvert x\rvert^{2}), \quad \ell(x)\approx \lvert x\rvert$$
 for $|x|$ small enough. 
 It follows that 
 $$
 \lim_{s\to 0}\frac{1}{s^n}\int_{L_{s}}\lvert \langle\Phi,\mathcal{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{n+1}}\rvert^{2}\ell^{-2}\,{\rm d} A=0
 $$
and 
$$
\lim_{s\to 0}\int_{L_{s}}\lvert \langle\Phi,\mathcal{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{n+1}}\rvert^{2}\ell^{-n-2}\,{\rm d} A=\lim_{s\to 0}\int_{L_{s}}\lvert \langle\Phi,\mathcal{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{n+1}}\rvert^{2}s^{-n+1}\ell^{-3}\,{\rm d} A=0.
$$
Recalling (\ref{recapp}) we conclude with 
\eqn{lim2}
$$
\lim_{s\to 0} \mathcal A(s)=0\,.
$$
Using (\ref{lim1}) and (\ref{lim2}), and recalling that
$\mathcal h_u^{-1}\le1$, we integrate \eqref{0.9} over
$(\varepsilon,r)$ and let $\varepsilon\downarrow0$ to obtain
\begin{flalign*}
\mathbb A(r) &\le \omega_{n}\mathcal h_u(0)^{-\gamma}-\mathcal{A}(r) - c_{\star} \int_0^r \mathbb E(s) \,{\rm d}s \nonumber \\
&\quad +2\int_{0}^{r}s^{2-n}\int_{L_{s}}\mu^{2}\,{\rm d} A\frac{\,{\rm d} s}{s}+\int_{0}^{r}s^{1-n}\int_{L_{s}}\lvert \mu\rvert\,{\rm d} A\frac{\,{\rm d}s}{s}.
\end{flalign*}
We now proceed as in \cite[p.~140]{bs82}. Note that for any function $w\in L^{1}(\mathfrak{M})$ it holds that
$$
\int_{L_{s}}w\,{\rm d} A=\int_{\mathfrak{M}}\mathds{1}_{[0,\infty)}(s-\ell)w\,{\rm d} A,\qquad \quad \mathds{1}_{[0,\infty)}(t):=\begin{cases}
\ 1\quad &\mbox{if} \ \ t\ge 0\vspace{1.5mm}\\
\displaystyle
\ 0\quad &\mbox{if} \ \ t<0.
\end{cases}
$$
Therefore, also using Fubini's theorem, we have 
\begin{flalign*}
 \int_0^r \mathbb E(s) \,{\rm d}s 
& =\int_{\mathfrak{M}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{2-\gamma}\left[\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}(D_{ij}u)^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{v}_{i}D_{ij}u\right)^{2}\right]\left(\int_{0}^{r}\frac{\mathds{1}_{[0,\infty)}(s-\ell)(s^{2}-\ell^{2})}{2s^{n+1}}\,{\rm d}s\right)\,{\rm d} A\nonumber \\
& \ge\int_{\mathfrak{M}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{2-\gamma}\left(\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}(D_{ij}u)^{2}\right)\mathcal{J}(\ell,r)\,{\rm d} A \ge\int_{L_{r}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{2-\gamma}\left(\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}(D_{ij}u)^{2}\right)\mathcal{J}(\ell,r)\,{\rm d} A \\
& \ge c_{n}r^{2-n}\int_{L_{r/2}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{2-\gamma}\lvert D^{2}u\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d} A,
\end{flalign*}
where
$$
\mathcal{J}(\ell,r):=
\begin{cases}
\displaystyle
\frac{\ell^{2-n}}{n(n-2)}
-\frac{r^{2-n}}{2(n-2)}
+\frac{\ell^{2}r^{-n}}{2n}
&\mbox{if } n\ge3,\quad 0<\ell<r
\\[2mm]
\displaystyle
\frac12\log\left(\frac r\ell\right)
-\frac14\left(1-\frac{\ell^2}{r^2}\right)
&\mbox{if } n=2,\quad 0<\ell<r
\\[2mm]
0
&\mbox{if } \ell\ge r.
\end{cases}
$$
We also used that $\mathcal{J}(\ell,r)\ge c_{n}r^{2-n}$ whenever $\ell\in (0,r/2]$. Recalling that $\mathcal{A}(r)\ge 0$, combining the preceding estimates gives
\begin{flalign*}
\omega_{n}\mathcal h_u(0)^{-\gamma}&\ge \frac{1}{r^{n}}\int_{L_{r}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\,{\rm d} A+\frac{c_n}{r^{n-2}}\int_{L_{r/2}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{2-\gamma}\lvert D^{2}u\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d} A\nonumber \\
&\quad -2\int_{0}^{r}s^{2-n} \int_{L_{s}}\mu^{2}\,{\rm d} A \frac{\,{\rm d} s}{s}-\int_{0}^{r}s^{1-n} \int_{L_{s}}\lvert \mu\rvert\,{\rm d} A \frac{\,{\rm d}s}{s}.
\end{flalign*}
Translating the previous estimate back to $(x_0,u(x_0))$, we obtain 
\begin{flalign*}
\omega_{n}\mathcal{h}_u(x_0)^{-\gamma}&\ge \frac{1}{r^{n}}\int_{L_{r}(x_{0})}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-\gamma}\,{\rm d} A+\frac{c_n}{r^{n-2}}\int_{L_{r/2}(x_{0})}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{2-\gamma}\lvert D^{2}u\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d} A\nonumber \\
&\quad -2\int_{0}^{r}s^{2-n} \int_{L_{s}(x_{0})}\mu^{2}\,{\rm d} A \frac{\,{\rm d} s}{s}-\int_{0}^{r}s^{1-n} \int_{L_{s}(x_{0})}\lvert \mu\rvert\,{\rm d} A \frac{\,{\rm d}s}{s}.
\end{flalign*}
Recalling that $B_{r}(x_{0})\subset K_{r}(x_{0})$ and that $\gamma\in (0,1)$, we obtain 
\begin{flalign}
\mathcal{h}_u(x_0)^{-\gamma}&\ge\mathop{\int\hskip -1,05em -\, \!\!\!}\nolimits_{K_{r}(x_{0})}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{-(\gamma+1)}\,{\rm d} x+\frac{c_{n}}{r^{n-2}}\int_{K_{r/2}(x_{0})}\lvert D^{2}u\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d}x\nonumber \\
&\quad -\frac{2}{\omega_{n}}\int_{0}^{r}s^{2-n} \int_{K_{s}(x_{0})}\mu^{2}\,{\rm d} x \frac{\,{\rm d} s}{s}-\frac{1}{\omega_{n}}\int_{0}^{r}s^{1-n} \int_{K_{s}(x_{0})}\lvert \mu\rvert\,{\rm d}x \frac{\,{\rm d}s}{s},
\end{flalign}
which proves \eqref{0.10}. Finally, the choice in \eqref{sceltagamma} is admissible in the computation leading to
\cite[(3.8)]{bi23}: applying the trace inequality and
Young's inequality with parameter $1/4$ in
\cite[(3.6)]{bi23} yields $c_\star=1/(8n)$ and a coefficient
at most $1/4$ for the term $\mathcal h_u^{-\gamma}\mu^2$.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- corollary-4: body_md -->
\label{cor.pot}
Within the same setting as Proposition \ref{p31},
for every $x_0\in\mathbb R^n$ there exists
$c_0=c_0(x_0,u)\ge1$ such that
\begin{flalign}\label{0.10.1}
\mathcal h_u(x_0)^{-\gamma}
&\ge
\mathop{\int\hskip -1,05em -\, \!\!\!}\nolimits_{K_r(x_0)}\mathcal h_u^{-(\gamma+1)}\,{\rm d}x
+
\frac{c_n}{r^{n-2}}
\int_{K_{r/2}(x_0)}\lvert D^2u\rvert^2\,{\rm d}x
\nonumber\\
&\quad
-2c_0^{n-2}\mathbf P_2^1(\mu^2;x_0,c_0r)
-c_0^{n-1}\mathbf P_1^1(\mu;x_0,c_0r)
\end{flalign}
for every $r\in(0,1]$.
If additionally $\mathcal h_->0$, see \eqref{h-},
then \eqref{0.10.1} holds uniformly in $x_0\in\mathbb R^n$
and for every $r>0$, with $\mathcal h_-^{-1}$
replacing $c_0$.
<!-- end corollary-4 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
Apply the comparisons in (\ref{javier}) with
$(\sigma,\vartheta,w)=(2,1,\mu^2)$ and $(1,1,\mu)$ 
to the two potential terms in \eqref{0.10}.
This gives \eqref{0.10.1}.
When $\mathcal h_->0$, the uniform comparison applies
with $c_0=\mathcal h_-^{-1}$ for every centre and radius,
proving the final assertion.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- remark-14: body_md -->
\label{rem.intrinsic.potentials}
{\em Intrinsic potentials of a different type also occur in the nonlinear potential theory of degenerate parabolic equations of the type
\eqn{appearpar}
$$
u_t-\,{\rm div}\, (|Du|^{p-2}Du)=\mu\,, \qquad p> 2-\frac{1}{n+1}
$$
in cylinders of the type $\Omega \times \mathbb R$, $\Omega \subset \mathbb R^n$, as pioneered in  \cite{KM13,km14}.
There, the intrinsic cylinders
\[
Q_\varrho^\lambda(x_0,t_0)
:=
B_\varrho(x_0)\times
(t_0-\lambda^{2-p}\varrho^2,t_0),
\qquad p\ge2,
\]
give rise to the potentials
$$
I_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mu}(x_0,t_0;r):=
\int_0^r
\frac{|\mu|(Q_\varrho^\lambda(x_0,t_0))}
{\varrho^{n+2-\beta}}
\frac{d\varrho}{\varrho},
\qquad 0<\beta\le n+2,
$$
where $\mu$ is the measure datum appearing in (\ref{appearpar}).
The parameter $\lambda$ is linked to the solution by the
condition in \cite[Theorem 1.1]{km14}. More precisely, for $p\ge2$, \cite[Theorem 1.1]{km14}
gives the implication
\[
cI_{1,\lambda}^{\mu}(x_0,t_0;r)
+
c\left(
\mathop{\int\hskip -1,05em -\, \!\!\!}\nolimits_{Q_r^\lambda(x_0,t_0)}
|Du|^{p-1}\,dx\,\mathrm{d} t
\right)^{1/(p-1)}
\le\lambda
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
|Du(x_0,t_0)|\le\lambda,
\]
where $c=c(n,p)>1$, provided that
$Q_r^\lambda(x_0,t_0)$ is contained in the domain
and $(x_0,t_0)$ is a Lebesgue point of $Du$.
Thus $\lambda$ determines the integration cylinder
and simultaneously controls the potential and the
gradient average computed on it, tying the geometry
to the solution. Such estimates yield sharp regularity
criteria and demonstrate the intrinsic geometry approach
going back to DiBenedetto \cite{dib93}.
In the present setting, the corresponding dependence is
encoded directly in the sets $K_\varrho^u(x_0)$. In both constructions, the potential is therefore evaluated
on a geometry tied to the function being estimated.}
<!-- end remark-14 -->
<!-- proposition-7: body_md -->
\label{com.sup}
Let
$
u\in\mathbb X(\mathbb R^n)\cap C^\infty(\mathbb R^n)
$
be the minimizer of \eqref{bi.en} with charge
$
\mu\in C^\infty_0(\mathbb R^n).
$
If $n=2$, assume in addition that $\mu\in L^1_0(\mathbb R^2)$ (so that $u$ is a classical solution to \eqref{bi} by Proposition \ref{exex}).
Then
\eqn{boost.at.infinity}
$$
\lim_{|x|\to\infty}\mathcal h_u(x)=1.
$$
Moreover, there exist a radius
$
\textnormal{\texttt{r}}_\mu
\equiv
\textnormal{\texttt{r}}_\mu(n,\mu)
\in(0,\infty)
$
and dimensional constants
$c_0(n)\ge4$
and
$\mathfrak s_n>0$
such that, setting
\eqn{soglie}
$$
\mathcal h_\infty(\mu)
:=
c_0(n)
\left(1+\lVert \mu \rVert_{\mathcal D^{1,2}(\mathbb R^n)^*}^2\right)
\geq 4,
$$
one has
\eqn{0.17}
$$
\mathcal h_u
\le
\frac{\mathcal h_\infty(\mu)}{2}
\qquad
\mbox{in }
\mathbb R^n\setminus B_{\textnormal{\texttt{r}}_\mu}(0)
$$
and
\eqn{stimamis}
$$
\left| \left\{x\in\mathbb R^n:\mathcal h_u(x)>\mathcal h_\infty(\mu)\right\}\right|
\le\mathfrak s_n.
$$
In particular,
\eqn{external.compact.support}
$$
\,{\rm supp } \left(\mathcal h_u-\mathcal h_\infty(\mu) \right)_+\Subset B_{\textnormal{\texttt{r}}_\mu}(0).
$$
<!-- end proposition-7 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
We strongly rely on \eqref{0.10}. For this, we first recall that
by Proposition \ref{exex}, $u$ is strictly spacelike
and solves \eqref{bi} in the classical sense.
Let $\gamma$ be the exponent in Proposition \ref{p31}.
Fix a dimensional constant $c_0(n)\ge4$.
We first prove \eqref{boost.at.infinity} using only the equation
and the fact that $Du\in L^2(\mathbb R^n)$.
Fix $\tau>1$ and choose $R>0$ such that
\eqn{barry}
$$
\,{\rm supp }\,\mu\Subset B_R(0),
\qquad
\int_{\mathbb R^n\setminus B_R(0)}|Du|^2\,{\rm d}x
\le\omega_n(1-\tau^{-\gamma}).
$$
By Lemma \ref{lemmino} and Lemma \ref{balls}
\textnormal{(h$_2$)} when $n\ge3$, and by Lemma
\ref{balls} \textnormal{(h$_3$)} when $n=2$,
there exists $R_1\equiv R_1(\tau)>R$ such that
$
K_1(x_0)\cap\overline{B_R(0)}=\varnothing
$
whenever $|x_0|\ge R_1$.
Indeed, in dimensions $n\ge3$ the function $u$ is bounded,
so the Euclidean radii containing $K_1(x_0)$ can be
chosen independently of $x_0$.
In dimension two, apply \textnormal{(h$_3$)} with
the compact set $\overline{B_R(0)}$ and $s=1$.
Since $K_s(x_0)\subset K_1(x_0)$ for $0<s\le1$,
both potentials involving $\mu$ in \eqref{0.10}
vanish at such centers when $r=1$.
Further discarding the nonnegative Hessian term, we obtain
$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal h_u(x_0)^{-\gamma}
&\ge
\mathop{\int\hskip -1,05em -\, \!\!\!}\nolimits_{K_1(x_0)} (1-|Du|^2)^{(\gamma+1)/2}\,{\rm d}x
\ge 1-\mathop{\int\hskip -1,05em -\, \!\!\!}\nolimits_{K_1(x_0)}|Du|^2\,{\rm d}x\\
&\ge 1-\frac1{\omega_n} \int_{\mathbb R^n\setminus B_R(0)}|Du|^2\,{\rm d}x \stackrel{\eqref{barry}}{\ge}
\tau^{-\gamma}.
\end{aligned}
$$
Here we used $0<(\gamma+1)/2<1$ and
$B_1(x_0)\subset K_1(x_0)$.
We deduce that
$
\mathcal h_u(x_0)\le\tau
$
whenever $|x_0|\ge R_1(\tau)$.
Since $\tau>1$ is arbitrary and $\mathcal h_u\ge1$,
this proves \eqref{boost.at.infinity}.
Taking $\tau=2$, we obtain
$
\mathcal h_u(x_0)\le2 \le\mathcal h_\infty(\mu)/2
$
whenever $|x_0|\ge R_1(2)$.
This proves \eqref{0.17} with
$\textnormal{\texttt{r}}_\mu:=2R_1(2)$.
Note that since $u$ is uniquely determined by $\mu$,
this radius depends only on $n$ and $\mu$.
Moreover,
$
\,{\rm supp }\,(\mathcal h_u-\mathcal h_\infty(\mu))_+
\subset\overline{B_{R_1(2)}(0)}
\Subset B_{\textnormal{\texttt{r}}_\mu}(0),
$
which proves \eqref{external.compact.support}.
It remains to establish \eqref{stimamis}. 
By \eqref{l1l1} we have 
\[
\|Du\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^n)}^2
\le
\int_{\mathbb R^n}\mathcal h_u|Du|^2\,{\rm d}x
\le
\langle\mu,u\rangle
\le
\|\mu\|_{\mathcal D^{1,2}(\mathbb R^n)^*}
\|Du\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^n)}.
\]
Thus
$
\|Du\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^n)}
\le
\|\mu\|_{\mathcal D^{1,2}(\mathbb R^n)^*},
$
and, finally
\eqn{jenny}
$$
\int_{\mathbb R^n}\mathcal h_u|Du|^2\,{\rm d}x
\le
\|\mu\|_{\mathcal D^{1,2}(\mathbb R^n)^*}^2.
$$
Set
$
E_0:=\{x\in\mathbb R^n:
\mathcal h_u(x)>\mathcal h_\infty(\mu)\}.
$
Since $\mathcal h_\infty(\mu)\ge4$, on $E_0$ we have
$|Du|^2=1-\mathcal h_u^{-2}\ge1/2$.
Therefore,
$$
\frac{\mathcal h_\infty(\mu)}2\,|E_0|
\le
\int_{E_0}\mathcal h_u|Du|^2\,{\rm d}x
\stackrel{(\ref{jenny})}{ \leq}
\|\mu\|_{\mathcal D^{1,2}(\mathbb R^n)^*}^2.
$$
Using \eqref{soglie}, we conclude
$$
|E_0|
\le
\frac{2}{c_0(n)}
\frac{\|\mu\|_{\mathcal D^{1,2}(\mathbb R^n)^*}^2}{1+\|\mu\|_{\mathcal D^{1,2}(\mathbb R^n)^*}^2}
\le\frac{2}{c_0(n)}
=:\mathfrak s_n.
$$
This proves \eqref{stimamis}.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- proposition-8: body_md -->
\label{connessione}
Let
$u\in\mathbb X(\mathbb R^n)\cap C^\infty(\mathbb R^n)$
be a classical solution to \eqref{bi}, with
$\mu\in C^\infty_0(\mathbb R^n)$.
Then $\mu\in\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)^*$ and $u$ minimizes $\mathcal E_\mu$
over $\mathbb X(\mathbb R^n)$.
Moreover, if $n=2$, then $
\mu\in L^1_0(\mathbb R^2).
$
<!-- end proposition-8 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
The exterior bound established in the first part of the proof of Proposition \ref{com.sup} uses only the classical equation and $u\in\mathbb X(\mathbb R^n)$.
It therefore applies here and gives $\mathcal h_u\le2$ outside a sufficiently large ball.
Together with the continuity of $Du$ and strict spacelikeness on compact sets, this implies
$\mathcal h_u\in L^\infty(\mathbb R^n)$.
Since $Du\in L^2(\mathbb R^n)$, it follows that
$
F:=\mathcal h_uDu
\in L^2(\mathbb R^n;\mathbb R^n).
$ If $n=2$, choose $R>0$ such that $\,{\rm supp }\, \mu\Subset B_R(0)$.
To prove $\mu\in L^1_0(\mathbb R^2)$,
fix a nonincreasing function 
$\vartheta\in C^\infty(\mathbb R)$ such that
$0\le\vartheta\le1$, $\vartheta=1$ on $(-\infty,1]$,
and $\vartheta=0$ on $[2,\infty)$.
For $T>\max\{e^2,R+e\}$, define
\eqn{logatau}
$$
\eta_T(x)
:=\vartheta\left(\frac{\log(e+|x|)}{\log T}\right).
$$
Then $\eta_T\in C^\infty_0(\mathbb R^2)$,
$\eta_T=1$ in $B_{T-e}(0)$, and
$\eta_T=0$ outside $B_{T^2-e}(0)$.
In particular, $\eta_T=1$ on $\,{\rm supp }\, \mu$.
Moreover,
$$
|D\eta_T(x)|\le\frac{\|\vartheta'\|_{L^\infty}}{(e+|x|)\log T}\mathds1_{\{T-e<|x|<T^2-e\}}(x),
$$
for every $x\in \mathbb R^2$, so that
$$
\|D\eta_T\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^2)}^2\le
\frac{2\pi\|\vartheta'\|_{L^\infty}^2}{(\log T)^2}
\int_{T-e}^{T^2-e}\frac{\rho}{(e+\rho)^2}\,d\rho\le
\frac{2\pi\|\vartheta'\|_{L^\infty}^2}{\log T}.
$$
Testing $-\,{\rm div} F=\mu$ directly with $\eta_T$, we obtain
$$
\left|\int_{\mathbb R^2}\mu\,dx\right|
=\left|\int_{\mathbb R^2}\langle F, D\eta_T\rangle\,dx\right|
\le
\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}\|\vartheta'\|_{L^\infty}}{\sqrt{\log T}}
\|F\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^2)}
\stackrel{T\to \infty}{\longrightarrow}0,
$$
which establishes $
\mu\in L^1_0(\mathbb R^2).
$ In every dimension, the equation gives
$$
\left|\int_{\mathbb R^n}\mu\varphi\,dx\right|
=\left|\int_{\mathbb R^n}\langle F, D\varphi\rangle \,dx\right|
\le
\|F\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^n)}
\|D\varphi\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^n)}
$$
for all $\varphi\in C^\infty_0(\mathbb R^n)$.
Hence $\mu\in\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)^*$, and a standard density argument, together with
Remark \ref{concreto}, gives
$
\langle\mu,w\rangle
=
\int_{\mathbb R^n}\langle F, Dw\rangle\,dx$
for every $w\in\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)$. 
Finally, another standard argument: the convexity of
$H(\xi)=1-\sqrt{1-|\xi|^2}$ implies that, for every
$v\in\mathbb X(\mathbb R^n)$,
$$
\mathcal E_\mu(v)-\mathcal E_\mu(u)\ge
\int_{\mathbb R^n}\langle F, D(v-u)\rangle\,dx
-\langle\mu,v-u\rangle =0.
$$
This proves the minimality of $u$.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{ww.lem}
Under assumptions \eqref{assuntidir}, 
let
$
\Lambda_0>0$, 
$
M>0$, 
$\textnormal{\texttt{l}}>0.
$
Then there exists
$
\sigma
=
\sigma(n,\textnormal{\texttt{l}},\Lambda_0,\sigma_0,M,\Omega)
\in(0,1)
$
with the following property. Let
$
u\in C^2(\Omega)\cap C^0(\overline\Omega)
$
satisfy
$$
\lvert Du(x)\rvert<1 \mbox{ for every $x\in\Omega$  \quad $[-\mathcal Mu]_{n,1;\Omega}\le\Lambda_0$ \quad and $ u=\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0$  
 on $\partial\Omega$}
 $$
and assume 
$
\lVert \textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0 \rVert_{C^2(\overline\Omega)}
\le M$.
Then, for every
$
x_0,x_1\in\overline\Omega
$
such that
$
\overline{x_1x_0}\subset\overline\Omega$ and 
$\textnormal{\texttt{l}}/4
\le
\lvert x_1-x_0\rvert
\le
\textnormal{\texttt{l}},
$
it holds that 
\eqn{proved}
$$
\lvert u(x_1)-u(x_0)\rvert
\le
(1-\sigma)\lvert x_1-x_0\rvert.
$$
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
In the following we shall repeatedly pass
to subsequences without relabeling them. We first observe that every function satisfying the assumptions of the
lemma is weakly spacelike along every segment contained in
$\overline\Omega$, namely
\eqn{equi}
$$
\lvert u(x)-u(y)\rvert
\le
\lvert x-y\rvert
\qquad
\mbox{whenever }
\overline{xy}\subset\overline\Omega.
$$
Indeed, set
$
\gamma(t):=x+t(y-x)
$
for $t\in[0,1]$. If
$
\gamma((0,1))\subset\Omega,
$
the claim follows from $\lvert Du\rvert<1$ and continuity up to the
endpoints. Otherwise, setting
$
a:=\min\{t\in[0,1]:\gamma(t)\in\partial\Omega\},
$, $
b:=\max\{t\in[0,1]:\gamma(t)\in\partial\Omega\},
$
we have, by $\lvert Du\rvert<1$ in $\Omega$ and continuity,
$
\lvert u(x)-u(\gamma(a))\rvert
\le
\lvert x-\gamma(a)\rvert$, $
\lvert u(\gamma(b))-u(y)\rvert
\le
\lvert \gamma(b)-y\rvert.
$
Moreover, since $u=\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0$ on $\partial\Omega$ and
$\lVert D\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0 \rVert_{C^0(\overline\Omega)}\le1-\sigma_0$,
$
\lvert u(\gamma(a))-u(\gamma(b))\rvert
\le
(1-\sigma_0)\lvert \gamma(a)-\gamma(b)\rvert.
$
Since the points are collinear and ordered along the segment, the
triangle inequality gives \eqref{equi}.

We argue by contradiction. Then there exist sequences of functions 
$
\{u_i\}\subset  C^2(\Omega)\cap C^0(\overline\Omega)$, $
\{\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_{0;i}\}\subset  C^2(\overline\Omega),
$
and points
$
\{x_{0;i} \},\{x_{1;i}\}\subset \overline\Omega
$
such that
$
\lvert Du_i\rvert<1
$
 in $\Omega$, 
$
[-\mathcal Mu_i]_{n,1;\Omega}\le\Lambda_0$, 
$
u_i=\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_{0;i}
$ 
on $\partial\Omega,
$
with
\eqn{ww.unif.bd}
$$
\lVert \textnormal{\texttt{u}}_{0;i} \rVert_{C^2(\overline\Omega)}
\le M,
\qquad
\lVert D\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_{0;i} \rVert_{C^0(\overline\Omega)}
\le1-\sigma_0,
$$
and
$
\overline{x_{1;i}x_{0;i}}\subset\overline\Omega$, 
 $ \textnormal{\texttt{l}}/4 \le\lvert x_{1;i}-x_{0;i}\rvert\le \textnormal{\texttt{l}},
$
while
\eqn{pp.0}
$$
\lvert x_{1;i}-x_{0;i}\rvert
\ge
\lvert u_i(x_{1;i})-u_i(x_{0;i})\rvert
\ge
(1-1/i)\lvert x_{1;i}-x_{0;i}\rvert.
$$

By compactness of $\overline\Omega$, we may assume that
$
x_{0;i}\to x_0,
$ and 
$x_{1;i}\to x_1.$
It follows that
$
\textnormal{\texttt{l}}/4
\le
\lvert x_1-x_0\rvert
\le
\textnormal{\texttt{l}},
$
and hence $x_0\ne x_1$. Moreover, since
$
\overline{x_{1;i}x_{0;i}}\subset\overline\Omega
$
for every $i$ and $\overline\Omega$ is closed, we have
$
\overline{x_1x_0}\subset\overline\Omega.
$ The uniform $C^2$-bound in \eqref{ww.unif.bd} and the compactness of
$\overline\Omega$ allow us, after passing to a further subsequence, to
find
$
\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0\in C^1(\overline\Omega)
$
such that
\eqn{ww.bd.conv}
$$
\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_{0;i}\to\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0
\qquad
\mbox{in }C^1(\overline\Omega).
$$
In particular,
$
\lVert D\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0 \rVert_{C^0(\overline\Omega)}\le1-\sigma_0.
$
By defining 
$
u_0:=\left.\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0\right|_{\partial\Omega},
$
we also have
$
\left.\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_{0;i}\right|_{\partial\Omega}
\to u_0$
uniformly in $\partial\Omega$. Furthermore, whenever
$y_0,y_1\in\partial\Omega$ and
$\overline{y_1y_0}\subset\overline\Omega$,
we have 
\eqn{ww.bd.strict}
$$
\lvert u_0(y_1)-u_0(y_0)\rvert
\le
(1-\sigma_0)\lvert y_1-y_0\rvert. 
$$
We next establish compactness of $\{u_i\}$ in
$C^0(\overline\Omega)$. For every $x\in\Omega$, choose
$y_x\in\partial\Omega$
such that
$\lvert x-y_x\rvert=\,{\rm dist}(x,\partial\Omega).
$
The open segment joining $x$ to $y_x$ is contained in $\Omega$, and
therefore \eqref{equi} gives
$
\lvert u_i(x)-\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_{0;i}(y_x)\rvert
\le
\,{\rm dist}(x,\partial\Omega).
$
Hence
$
\sup_i\lVert u_i \rVert_{L^\infty(\Omega)}
\le
M+\,{\rm diam}(\Omega).
$
Moreover, the functions $u_i$ are locally uniformly $1$-Lipschitz in
$\Omega$. By the Ascoli-Arzel\`a theorem and a diagonal argument,
they converge, after passing to a subsequence, locally uniformly in
$\Omega$. The previous boundary estimate, together with
\eqref{ww.bd.conv}, upgrades the convergence to
\eqn{ww.unif.conv}
$$
u_i\to u
\qquad
\mbox{uniformly on }\overline\Omega
$$
for some
$
u\in C^0(\overline\Omega)
$
satisfying
$
u=u_0
$ on $\partial\Omega.
$
Passing to the limit in \eqref{equi}, we obtain
\eqn{ww.limit.sp}
$$
\lvert u(x)-u(y)\rvert
\le
\lvert x-y\rvert
\qquad
\mbox{whenever }
\overline{xy}\subset\overline\Omega.
$$
In particular,
$
u\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega),
$ with $
\lvert Du\rvert\le1$
a.e. in $\Omega.$ 
Set
$
\mu_i:=-\mathcal Mu_i.
$
Since
$
[\mu_i]_{n,1;\Omega}\le\Lambda_0
$
and
$
L(n,1)(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^n(\Omega)
$,
the sequence $\{\mu_i\}$ is uniformly bounded in $L^n(\Omega)$.
Since $n\ge2$, after passing to a subsequence we may assume that
\eqn{ww.weak.mu}
$$
\mu_i\rightharpoonup\mu
\qquad
\mbox{weakly in }L^n(\Omega)
$$
for some $\mu\in L^n(\Omega)$. By the weak lower semicontinuity
properties of the Lorentz norm,
$
\mu\in L(n,1)(\Omega)$, 
with $
[\mu]_{n,1;\Omega}\le\Lambda_0.
$ We can now adapt the convergence argument of
\cite[Lemma 1.3]{bs82}\footnote{Indeed, each $u_i$ minimizes
$\mathcal E_{\mu_i}(\,\cdot\,;\Omega)$ with its own boundary datum.
To justify this without any control of the boost near the boundary,
let $v\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)$ be weakly spacelike with
$v=u_i$ on $\partial\Omega$, and set
$
v_\delta
:=u_i+(v-u_i-\delta)_+-(u_i-v-\delta)_+$, 
$ \delta>0.
$
Then $v_\delta$ is weakly spacelike and
$\,{\rm supp }(v_\delta-u_i)\Subset\Omega$.
Convexity of $H$ and the equation for $u_i$, tested with
$v_\delta-u_i$, give
$\mathcal E_{\mu_i}(u_i;\Omega)
\le\mathcal E_{\mu_i}(v_\delta;\Omega)$.
Since $v_\delta\to v$ uniformly and $Dv_\delta\to Dv$ a.e.,
letting $\delta\downarrow0$ yields the desired minimality
by dominated convergence, using $0\le H\le1$ on the unit ball
and $\mu_i\in L^1(\Omega)$. See also the argument at the end of the proof of Proposition \ref{connessione}.}. The uniform $L(n,1)$-bound gives uniform
$L^n$- and $L^1$-bounds for the charges, while
\eqref{ww.unif.conv} and \eqref{ww.weak.mu} allow passage to the limit
in the linear terms. In particular, the moving-set argument is
unchanged: whenever
$
E_i\to E
$
in measure and $v$ is bounded,
$$
\left|
\int_{E_i\triangle E}\mu_i v\,{\rm d}x
\right|
\le
c\lVert v \rVert_{L^\infty(\Omega)}
\lVert \mu_i \rVert_{L^n(\Omega)}
\lvert E_i\triangle E\rvert^{1-1/n}
\to0.
$$
It follows that $u$ is the variational minimizer of
$
\mathcal E_\mu(\,\cdot\,;\Omega)
$
with boundary datum $u_0$.
By \eqref{ww.unif.conv}, letting \(i\to\infty\) in \eqref{pp.0}
gives
\eqn{ww.light.end}
$$
\lvert u(x_1)-u(x_0)\rvert
=
\lvert x_1-x_0\rvert.
$$
Set  
\(\gamma(t):=x_0+t(x_1-x_0)\), \(t\in[0,1]\) and  we may assume that
\(u(x_1)-u(x_0)=\lvert x_1-x_0\rvert\). Then \eqref{ww.limit.sp} and \eqref{ww.light.end} imply
\eqn{ombra}
$$
u(\gamma(t))
=
u(x_0)+t\lvert x_1-x_0\rvert
\qquad
\mbox{for every }t\in[0,1].
$$
If \(\overline{x_1x_0}\cap\Omega=\varnothing\), then
\(\overline{x_1x_0}\subset\partial\Omega\), and
\eqref{ww.light.end}, together with \(u=u_0\) on
\(\partial\Omega\), gives
\(\lvert u_0(x_1)-u_0(x_0)\rvert=\lvert x_1-x_0\rvert\), contradicting
\eqref{ww.bd.strict}. Assume therefore that
\(\overline{x_1x_0}\cap\Omega\ne\varnothing\). Then
\(I:=\{t\in(0,1):\gamma(t)\in\Omega\}\) is a nonempty open subset
of \((0,1)\), and hence contains a nonempty open interval. By
\eqref{ombra}, the graph of \(u\) contains a nontrivial light
segment entirely contained in \(\Omega\). Since \(u\) is the
variational minimizer with charge \(\mu\in L(n,1)(\Omega)\), the
results of Section \ref{dirisec} allow us to apply
Theorem \ref{al.t}. The light segment therefore extends along the
whole maximal connected component of the line through
\(x_0,x_1\) contained in \(\Omega\). Let \(y_0,y_1\in\partial\Omega\) be the endpoints of this maximal
component. By the definition of the component,
\(\overline{y_1y_0}\subset\overline\Omega\). By continuity, the light-ray identity extends to the endpoints and gives
$
\lvert u(y_1)-u(y_0)\rvert
=
\lvert y_1-y_0\rvert.
$
Since $u=u_0$ on $\partial\Omega$, we obtain
$
\lvert u_0(y_1)-u_0(y_0)\rvert
=
\lvert y_1-y_0\rvert,
$
which contradicts \eqref{ww.bd.strict}.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{correctors}
Let $\mathcal U\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a $C^2$-regular, bounded domain, 
let $\mathcal w\in C^2(\overline{\mathcal U})$, and assume that
\eqn{ww.background.f}
$$
-\mathcal M \mathcal w=\mathfrak f
\qquad
\mbox{in }\mathcal U
$$
for some $\mathfrak f\in L^\infty(\mathcal U)$. Suppose moreover that
\eqn{ww.}
$$
\lVert D\mathcal w \rVert_{C^0(\overline{\mathcal U})}
\leq1-\mathcal s
\qquad
\mbox{for some }\mathcal s\in(0,1).
$$
Let $\mu_*\in L(n,1)(\mathcal U)$. There exists a constant
$
\tilde{c}_* =\tilde{c}_* (n,\mathcal s,\lVert D\mathcal w \rVert_{C^1(\overline{\mathcal U})},\mathcal U)
\geq1
$
such that, if
\eqn{ww.17}
$$
\tilde{c}_*[\mu_*]_{n,1;\mathcal U}\leq1,
$$
then there exists
$
\mathcal b \in
W^{2;n,1}(\mathcal U) \cap W^{1;n,1}_0(\mathcal U)
$
solving
\eqn{pd.ww}
$$
\begin{cases}
-\mathcal M(\mathcal w+\mathcal b)
=
\mathfrak f+\mu_*
&
\mbox{in }\mathcal U,
\\[1mm]
\mathcal b=0
&
\mbox{on }\partial\mathcal U.
\end{cases}
$$
Moreover,
\eqn{ww.15}
$$
\lVert \mathcal b \rVert_{W^{2;n,1}(\mathcal U)}
\leq
c[\mu_*]_{n,1;\mathcal U},
\qquad
\lVert D\mathcal b \rVert_{L^\infty(\mathcal U)}
\leq
\frac{\mathcal s}{4},
$$
where
$c$ depends only on 
$
n,\mathcal s,
\lVert D\mathcal w \rVert_{C^1(\overline{\mathcal U})},
\mathcal U.
$
The solution is unique among the solutions satisfying the gradient
bound in \eqref{ww.15}. Finally,
\eqn{ww.18}
$$
\mu_*\geq0
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\mathcal b\geq0,
\qquad
\mu_*\leq0
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\mathcal b\leq0.
$$
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
The proof splits into three steps. 

{\em Step 1: Linearized operator and nonlinear remainder}.
Set
\(
\textnormal{\texttt{H}}(x):=\partial^2H(D\mathcal w(x)).
\)
By \eqref{ww.}, the matrix $\textnormal{\texttt{H}}(\cdot)$ is symmetric and uniformly
elliptic. More precisely, there exists
$c_{\mathcal s}=c_{\mathcal s}(n,\mathcal s)\geq1$ such that
\eqn{ww.2}
$$
|\xi|^2
\leq
\langle\textnormal{\texttt{H}}(x)\xi,\xi\rangle
\leq
c_{\mathcal s}|\xi|^2
\qquad
\mbox{for every }
x\in\overline{\mathcal U},
\quad
\xi\in\mathbb R^n.
$$
Moreover,
\eqn{ww.1}
$$
\lVert \textnormal{\texttt{H}} \rVert_{C^0(\overline{\mathcal U})}
\leq c,
\qquad
\lVert D\textnormal{\texttt{H}} \rVert_{C^0(\overline{\mathcal U})}
\leq
c(n,\mathcal s)
\lVert D^2\mathcal w \rVert_{C^0(\overline{\mathcal U})},
$$
where $c=c(n,\mathcal s)$. Expanding
$-\,{\rm div}(\textnormal{\texttt{H}}(x)D\varphi)$, define
\begin{flalign*}
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{w}}\varphi&:=-\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\partial^{2}_{ij}H(D\mathcal{w})D_{ij}\varphi-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{i,k=1}^{n}\partial^{3}_{ij,k}H(D\mathcal{w})D_{ik}\mathcal{w}\right)D_{j}\varphi\nonumber \\
&=:-\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\textnormal{\texttt{H}}_{ij}(x)D_{ij}\varphi-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\textnormal{\texttt{J}}_{j}(x)D_{j}\varphi.                                                                              
\end{flalign*}
Note that 
$
\lVert \textnormal{\texttt{J}} \rVert_{C^0(\mathcal{\overline U})}\le c(n,\mathcal{s})\lVert D^{2}\mathcal{w} \rVert_{C^0(\mathcal{\overline U})}.
$
Using \eqref{ww.2}, \eqref{ww.1}, and the above bound
on $\textnormal{\texttt{J}}$, we may apply
\cite[Theorem 9.15 and Lemma 9.17, pp.~241--242]{gt01}.
Thus, 
for every \(p\in(1,\infty)\) and every \(\textnormal{\texttt{f}}\in L^p(\mathcal U)\), we can uniquely solve $\mathcal {L}_{\mathcal w}v=\textnormal{\texttt{f}}$ in the Dirichlet class $W^{1,p}_0(\mathcal U)$ with  
\eqn{dirip}
$$
\|v\|_{W^{2,p}(\mathcal U)}\le c\|\textnormal{\texttt{f}}\|_{L^p(\mathcal U)},
$$
where $c$ depends only on $n,p,\lVert D\mathcal w \rVert_{C^1(\mathcal{\overline U})}, \mathcal s, \mathcal U$. 
With abuse of notation we denote by $\mathcal {L}_{\mathcal w}^{-1}$ the linear operator such that 
$\mathcal {L}_{\mathcal w}^{-1}\textnormal{\texttt{f}} =v$ (the abuse is linked to the fact that, in principle, such an operator should depend on the exponent $p$, which is in fact not the case). By \eqref{dirip} and standard real interpolation,
we obtain
\eqn{ww.4}
$$
\lVert \mathcal {L}_{\mathcal w}^{-1}\textnormal{\texttt{f}} \rVert_{W^{2;n,1}(\mathcal U)}
\le
c_{\rm inv}[\textnormal{\texttt{f}}]_{n,1;\mathcal U}
\quad
\mbox{for every }\textnormal{\texttt{f}}\in L(n,1)(\mathcal U),
$$
where $c_{\rm inv}\geq 1$ depends on $n,\lVert D\mathcal w \rVert_{C^1(\mathcal{\overline U})}, \mathcal s, \mathcal U$ (see (\ref{normalo}) for the definition of the right-hand side). 
We then linearize around
\eqn{ww.5}
$$
v\in W^{2;n,1}(\mathcal{U})\cap W^{1;n,1}_{0}(\mathcal{U}) \ \ \mbox{such that} \ \ \lVert Dv \rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{U})}\le \mathcal{s}/4.
$$
By \eqref{ww.} and \eqref{ww.5},
\(
|D\mathcal{w}+tDv|
\le 1-3\mathcal{s}/4
\)
for every \(t\in[0,1]\). Hence all the derivatives of \(H\) up to
order four are uniformly bounded on the corresponding compact
subset of the unit ball. 
Since $-\mathcal M \mathcal w=\mathfrak f$, we can write
\begin{eqnarray}\label{ww.8}
-\mathcal M(\mathcal w+v)-\mathfrak f
&=&
-\mathcal M(\mathcal w+v)+\mathcal M \mathcal w
=
\mathcal L_{\mathcal w}v
+
\left(
-\mathcal M(\mathcal w+v)
+\mathcal M \mathcal w
-\mathcal L_{\mathcal w}v
\right)
\nonumber\\
&=:&
\mathcal L_{\mathcal w}v
+
\mathcal R_{\mathcal w}(v).
\end{eqnarray}
Recalling that $\partial^{3}_{ij,k}H$ is symmetric in all indices, we split the nonlinear remainder $\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{w}}(v)$ as 
\begin{flalign}\label{ww.7}
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{w}}(v)&=-\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\big(\partial^{2}_{ij}H(D\mathcal{w}+Dv)-\partial^{2}_{ij}H(D\mathcal{w})\big)D_{ij}v\nonumber \\
&\quad -\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\big(\partial^{2}_{ij}H(D\mathcal{w}+Dv)-\partial^{2}_{ij}H(D\mathcal{w})- \sum_{k=1}^{n}\partial^{3}_{ij,k}H(D\mathcal{w})D_{k}v\big)D_{ij}\mathcal{w}\nonumber \\
&=:\mbox{(I)}+\mbox{(II)}.
\end{flalign}
For the first term, the fundamental theorem of calculus gives
\[
\partial^2_{ij}H(D\mathcal{w}+Dv)
-
\partial^2_{ij}H(D\mathcal{w})
=
\sum_{k=1}^{n}
\int_0^1
\partial^3_{ij,k}H(D\mathcal{w}+tDv)
D_kv\,{\rm d}t.
\]
We conclude with 
\eqn{del1}
$$
|\mathrm{(I)}|
\le
c(n,\mathcal{s})|Dv||D^2v|.
$$
For the second term, Taylor's formula with integral remainder yields
\[
\begin{aligned}
&
\partial^2_{ij}H(D\mathcal{w}+Dv)
-
\partial^2_{ij}H(D\mathcal{w})
-
\sum_{k=1}^{n}
\partial^3_{ij,k}H(D\mathcal{w})D_kv
\\
&\qquad=
\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{n}
\int_0^1
(1-t)\,
\partial^4_{ij,k\ell}H(D\mathcal{w}+tDv)
D_kv\,D_\ell v\,{\rm d}t.
\end{aligned}
\]
Therefore,
\eqn{del2}
$$
|\mathrm{(II)}| \le c(n,\mathcal{s})|Dv|^2|D^2\mathcal{w}|.
$$
Combining the estimates in \eqref{del1} and \eqref{del2} with (\ref{ww.7}), we obtain
\eqn{rem}
$$
|\mathcal R_{\mathcal w}(v)|
\le
c|Dv||D^2v|
+
c|Dv|^2|D^2\mathcal w|
$$
with $c\equiv c(n,\mathcal{s})$. By the endpoint Sobolev--Lorentz embedding \cite{cp98}, we have
\eqn{immersione}
$$
\lVert Dv \rVert_{L^\infty(\mathcal U)}
\le
c_{\rm emb}\lVert v \rVert_{W^{2;n,1}(\mathcal U)}, \quad c_{\rm emb} \equiv c_{\rm emb}(n,\mathcal U)\geq 1\,.
$$
Using Young's inequality in \eqref{rem}, we get
\begin{flalign*}
[\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{w}}(v)]_{n,1;\mathcal{U}}&\le c\lVert Dv \rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{U})}[D^{2}v]_{n,1;\mathcal{U}}+c\lVert Dv \rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{U})}^{2}\lVert D^{2}\mathcal{w} \rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{U})}[\mathds 1_{\mathcal U}]_{n,1;\mathcal U} \nonumber \\
&\le c \lVert Dv \rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{U})}^{2}+c[D^{2}v]_{n,1;\mathcal{U}}^{2} 
\end{flalign*}
and therefore, by means of (\ref{immersione}), we conclude with 
\eqn{ww.10}
$$
[\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{w}}(v)]_{n,1;\mathcal{U}}\leq c_{\rm rem} \lVert v \rVert_{W^{2;n,1}(\mathcal{U})}^{2},
$$
where  $c_{\rm rem}\geq 1$ depends on $n,\mathcal{s},\,{\rm diam} (\mathcal U)\lVert D^2\mathcal{w} \rVert_{C^{0}(\bar{\mathcal{U}})},c_{\rm emb}$\footnote{For such a constant dependence note that
$
[\mathds 1_{\mathcal U}]_{n,1;\mathcal U}
\leq c(n)|\mathcal U|^{1/n}
\leq c(n)\,{\rm diam}(\mathcal U).
$}. A similar argument then yields
\eqn{ww.13}
$$
[\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{w}}(v_{1})-\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{w}}(v_{2})]_{n,1;\mathcal{U}}\le c_{\rm diff}\left(\lVert v_{1} \rVert_{W^{2;n,1}(\mathcal{U})}+\lVert v_{2} \rVert_{W^{2;n,1}(\mathcal{U})}\right)\lVert v_{1}-v_{2} \rVert_{W^{2;n,1}(\mathcal{U})},
$$
which holds for all $v_{1},v_{2}\in W^{2;n,1}(\mathcal{U})\cap W^{1;n,1}_{0}(\mathcal{U})$ such that $\lVert Dv_{i} \rVert_{L^{\infty}(\bar{\mathcal{U}})}\le \mathcal{s}/4,$ $i\in \{1,2\}$, where $c_{\rm diff}\geq 1$ again depends on $n,\mathcal{s},\,{\rm diam} (\mathcal U)\lVert D^2\mathcal{w} \rVert_{C^{0}(\bar{\mathcal{U}})}$ and $c_{\rm emb}$.
\medskip

{\em Step 2: Fixed-point argument}. 
Recall the meaning of the constants $c_{\rm inv}, c_{\rm emb}, c_{\rm rem}, c_{\rm diff}$; these have been defined in (\ref{ww.4}), (\ref{immersione}), (\ref{ww.10}) and (\ref{ww.13}), respectively, and altogether ultimately depend on $n,\mathcal{s},\lVert D\mathcal{w} \rVert_{C^1(\mathcal{\overline U})}$ and $\mathcal{U}$.  
Set
\(
X:=
W^{2;n,1}(\mathcal U)\cap W^{1;n,1}_0(\mathcal U)
\)
and take a number $\rho$ such that 
\eqn{ww.rho}
$$
0<\rho
\le
\frac18
\min
\left\{
\frac{\mathcal s}{c_{\rm emb}},
\frac1{c_{\rm inv}c_{\rm rem}},
\frac1{c_{\rm inv}c_{\rm diff}}
\right\}=:\rho_{\rm uni}. 
$$
Consider the closed ball
$
\mathbb B_{\rho}^X
:=\{v\in X:\lVert v \rVert_{W^{2;n,1}(\mathcal U)}\le\rho\}.
$
It is a complete metric  space  and, by \eqref{ww.rho},
$$
\lVert Dv \rVert_{L^\infty(\mathcal U)}
\le
c_{\rm emb}\rho
\le
\mathcal s/4
\qquad
\mbox{for every }v\in\mathbb B_{\rho}^X.
$$
For \(v\in\mathbb B_{\rho}^X\), define
$$
\mathcal G_{\mathcal w}(v;\mu_{*})
:=
\mathcal {L}_{\mathcal w}^{-1}
(\mu_{*}-\mathcal R_{\mathcal w}(v)).
$$
We choose the constant $\widetilde c_*$ appearing
in \eqref{ww.17} as
$
\widetilde c_*:=2c_{\rm inv}/\rho.
$
With this choice, condition \eqref{ww.17} becomes
$
c_{\rm inv}[\mu_*]_{n,1;\mathcal U}\le \rho/2.
$
Then, using \eqref{ww.4}, \eqref{ww.10}, and \eqref{ww.rho}, we obtain
\eqn{ww.14}
$$
\begin{aligned}
\lVert \mathcal G_{\mathcal w}(v;\mu_{*}) \rVert
_{W^{2;n,1}(\mathcal U)}
&\le
c_{\rm inv}
[\mu_{*}]_{n,1;\mathcal U}
+c_{\rm inv}
[\mathcal R_{\mathcal w}(v)]_{n,1;\mathcal U}
\le
\frac{\rho}{2}
+
c_{\rm inv}c_{\rm rem}\rho^2
\le
\frac{5\rho}{8}
<
\rho.
\end{aligned}
$$
Thus \(\mathcal G_{\mathcal w}\) maps
\(\mathbb B_{\rho}^X\) into itself. Similarly, by \eqref{ww.4}, \eqref{ww.13}, and
\eqref{ww.rho},
$$
\begin{aligned}
&
\lVert 
\mathcal G_{\mathcal w}(v_1;\mu_{*})
-
\mathcal G_{\mathcal w}(v_2;\mu_{*})
 \rVert_{W^{2;n,1}(\mathcal U)}
\le
2c_{\rm inv}c_{\rm diff}\rho
\lVert v_1-v_2 \rVert_{W^{2;n,1}(\mathcal U)}
\le
\frac14
\lVert v_1-v_2 \rVert_{W^{2;n,1}(\mathcal U)}
\end{aligned}
$$
holds whenever \(v_1,v_2\in\mathbb B_{\rho}^X\). 
Hence \(\mathcal G_{\mathcal w}\) is a contraction on
\(\mathbb B_{\rho}^X\). The Banach--Caccioppoli fixed-point theorem gives a unique
\(\mathcal b\in\mathbb B_{\rho}^X\) such that
\(
\mathcal b=\mathcal G_{\mathcal w}(\mathcal b;\mu_{*}).
\)
By \eqref{ww.8}, this is equivalent to (\ref{pd.ww}). Proceeding as for (\ref{ww.14}), the fixed-point property  and \eqref{ww.10} also give
$$
\lVert \mathcal b \rVert_{W^{2;n,1}(\mathcal U)} = \lVert \mathcal G_{\mathcal w}(\mathcal b;\mu_{*}) \rVert
_{W^{2;n,1}(\mathcal U)}
\le
c_{\rm inv}[\mu_{*}]_{n,1;\mathcal U}
+
c_{\rm inv}c_{\rm rem}\lVert \mathcal b \rVert_{W^{2;n,1}(\mathcal U)}^2.
$$
Since
\(
\lVert \mathcal b \rVert_{W^{2;n,1}(\mathcal U)}\le\rho
\)
and \(c_{\rm inv}c_{\rm rem}\rho\le1/8\), the last term can be absorbed, yielding
(\ref{ww.15}). The solution is unique in the class determined by the last
gradient bound. Indeed, if \(\mathcal b_1,\mathcal b_2\) are two
such solutions, subtracting their weak formulations and testing
with \(\mathcal b_1-\mathcal b_2\) gives
$$
\begin{aligned}
0
={}&
\int_{\mathcal U}
\left\langle
\partial H(D\mathcal w+D\mathcal b_1)
-
\partial H(D\mathcal w+D\mathcal b_2),
D\mathcal b_1-D\mathcal b_2
\right\rangle\,{\rm d}x.
\end{aligned}
$$
The strict monotonicity of \(\partial H\) implies
\(D\mathcal b_1=D\mathcal b_2\) a.e. in \(\mathcal U\), and the
zero boundary trace gives \(\mathcal b_1=\mathcal b_2\).

\medskip


{\em Step 3: Sign of the corrector}.
Finally, \eqref{ww.18} follows from the weak comparison principle.
Indeed, by \eqref{ww.} and \eqref{ww.15},
$
\lVert D\mathcal w+D\mathcal b \rVert_{L^\infty(\mathcal U)}
\le
1-3\mathcal s/4,
$
and, since
$
\lVert D\mathcal w \rVert_{L^\infty(\mathcal U)}\le1-\mathcal s,
$
we have
$
\lvert D\mathcal w+tD\mathcal b\rvert
\le
1-3\mathcal s/4
$
for every $t\in[0,1]$.
Hence, setting
$$
\textnormal{\texttt{H}}_{\mathcal b}(x)
:=
\int_0^1
\partial^2H(D\mathcal w(x)+tD\mathcal b(x))\,{\rm d}t,
$$
the matrix \(\textnormal{\texttt{H}}_{\mathcal b}\) is uniformly elliptic in
\(\mathcal U\). Subtracting
\(-\mathcal M \mathcal w =\mathfrak f\) from
\(-\mathcal M(\mathcal w+\mathcal b)=\mathfrak f+\mu_*\), we obtain
$
-\,{\rm div} \, (\textnormal{\texttt{H}}_{\mathcal b}D\mathcal b)
=
\mu_*$
in $\mathcal U$
with 
$\mathcal b=0$
on $\partial \mathcal U$, and (\ref{ww.18}) 
follows by the weak comparison principle.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- corollary-5: body_md -->
\label{correctors.uniform}
Let \(\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n\) be a $C^2$-regular, bounded domain.
There exists \(r_\Omega>0\) with the following property. For every
\(r\in(0,r_\Omega]\), one can choose, for each
\(x_0\in\partial\Omega\), a \(C^2\)-regular boundary cap
\(\mathcal U_{x_0}\) such that
$$
\Omega\cap B_{r/2}(x_0)
\subset
\mathcal U_{x_0}
\subset
\Omega\cap B_r(x_0),
\qquad
\partial\mathcal U_{x_0}\cap B_{r/2}(x_0)
=
\partial\Omega\cap B_{r/2}(x_0),
$$
and such that the family
\(\{\mathcal U_{x_0}\}_{x_0\in\partial\Omega}\) has uniformly controlled
\(C^2\)-geometry. More precisely, the caps may be constructed from a
fixed reference cap in boundary coordinates, so that, after
translation, rotation, and dilation by \(1/r\), their \(C^2\)
boundary charts have uniform bounds only depending on \(r\) and
\(\Omega\).
Let \(\mathcal s\in(0,1)\) and \(K>0\). For every
\(x_0\in\partial\Omega\), let
\(\mathcal w_{x_0}\in C^2(\overline{\mathcal U}_{x_0})\) satisfy
\eqn{ww.uniform.background}
$$
-\mathcal M \mathcal w_{x_0}=\mathfrak f_{x_0}
\quad\mbox{in }\mathcal U_{x_0},
\qquad
\lVert D\mathcal w_{x_0} \rVert_{L^\infty(\mathcal U_{x_0})}
\leq1-\mathcal s,
\qquad
r\lVert D^2\mathcal w_{x_0} \rVert_{L^\infty(\mathcal U_{x_0})}
\leq K,
$$
where \(\mathfrak f_{x_0}\in L^\infty(\mathcal U_{x_0})\). Then there exist constants $
\varepsilon_{\rm \partial} \in (0,1)$ and $c_{\rm uni}\geq 1$, 
only depending on $n,\mathcal s,K,r,\Omega$, 
such that the following holds. If
\(\mu_{x_0}\in L(n,1)(\mathcal U_{x_0})\) satisfies
$$
[\mu_{x_0}]_{n,1;\mathcal U_{x_0}}
\leq
\varepsilon_{\rm \partial},
$$
then there exists
$
\mathcal b_{x_0}
\in
W^{2;n,1}(\mathcal U_{x_0})
\cap
W^{1;n,1}_0(\mathcal U_{x_0})
$
solving \eqref{pd.ww} with $\mathcal b\equiv  \mathcal b_{x_0}$, $\mathfrak f\equiv  \mathfrak f_{x_0}$ and $\mu_*\equiv  \mu_{x_0}$, 
and satisfying \eqref{ww.15}, with $c_{\rm uni}$ in place of $c$. Moreover, the sign conclusion \eqref{ww.18} holds.
<!-- end corollary-5 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
Since \(\partial\Omega\) is compact and the caps
\(\mathcal U_{x_0}\) are constructed at the fixed scale \(r\), their
quantitative \(C^2\)-geometry is controlled uniformly with respect to
\(x_0\in\partial\Omega\). Consequently, the constant $c_{\rm emb}$ in the endpoint Sobolev-Lorentz
embedding (\ref{immersione}) can be chosen independently of \(x_0\in \partial \Omega\).
On the other hand, by \eqref{ww.uniform.background}, 
it follows that the coefficient matrices
$
\partial^2H(D\mathcal w_{x_0})
$
are uniformly elliptic, with ellipticity constants only depending on
\(n\) and \(\mathcal s\), and that their \(W^{1,\infty}\)-norms are bounded
uniformly in \(x_0\), in terms of \(n,\mathcal s,K/r\). Similarly, the
coefficients of the linearized operators
$
\mathcal L_{\mathcal w_{x_0}}
$
satisfy common ellipticity and regularity bounds. Therefore, the global \(W^{2,p}\)-estimates used in the proof of
Lemma \ref{correctors}, at two fixed exponents
\(1<p_0<n<p_1<\infty\), give a common inverse constant
\(c_{\rm inv}\) via real interpolation. The estimates for the nonlinear
remainder and its difference give common constants
\(c_{\rm rem}\) and \(c_{\rm diff}\). Summarizing, the constants
\(c_{\rm inv}\), \(c_{\rm rem}\), and \(c_{\rm diff}\) can be chosen
uniformly with respect to \(x_0\in\partial\Omega\), only depending on
\(n,\mathcal s,K/r,r,\Omega\), while \(c_{\rm emb}\) depends only on
\(n,r,\Omega\). Here the dependence on \(r\) and \(\Omega\) accounts
for the common quantitative geometry of the caps. With such a uniformized choice, define $\rho_{\rm uni}$ as in (\ref{ww.rho}), with the present uniform meaning, and finally define
$
\varepsilon_{\rm \partial}
:=
\rho_{\rm uni}/(2c_{\rm inv}).
$
The fixed-point argument in Lemma \ref{correctors} can then be
performed on every \(\mathcal U_{x_0}\) with the same radius
\(\rho_{\rm uni}\). It yields (\ref{ww.15}) with $c_{\rm uni}:=2c_{\rm inv}$ as in the statement of the Corollary.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- proposition-9: body_md -->
\label{corr}
Under assumptions \eqref{assuntidir}, 
fix \(\Lambda_0, F_0>0\). For every $r_0>0$ there exist numbers
\eqn{piccole}
$$
\varepsilon_\partial, \sigma_*\equiv \varepsilon_\partial, \sigma_*\left(n,r_0,\sigma_0,\Lambda_0,F_0,
\lVert \textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0 \rVert_{C^2(\overline\Omega)},\Omega\right)\in (0,1)
$$
with the following property. Let $f\in L^\infty(\Omega)$ satisfy
\(\lVert f \rVert_{L^\infty(\Omega)}\leq F_0\). Let
$\mu\in L^\infty(\Omega)$, and assume 
\eqn{ww.41}
$$
[\mu]_{n,1;\Omega}\leq\Lambda_0.
$$
Assume that
\(u\in C^2(\Omega)\cap C^1(\overline\Omega)\)
is a strictly spacelike solution of
\eqn{ww.background.perturbed.problem}
$$
\begin{cases}
-\mathcal Mu=f+\mu
&
\mbox{in }\Omega,
\\[1mm]
u=\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0
&
\mbox{on }\partial\Omega.
\end{cases}
$$
Set
\(
\Omega_{r_0}
:=
\{x\in\Omega:\,{\rm dist}(x,\partial\Omega)<r_0\}.
\)
If
\eqn{ww.31}
$$
[\mu]_{n,1;\Omega_{r_0}}
\leq
\varepsilon_\partial,
$$
then
\eqn{ww.boundary.gap}
$$
\lVert Du \rVert_{C^0(\partial\Omega)}
\leq
1-\sigma_*.
$$
<!-- end proposition-9 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
Again, the proof goes in two steps.

{\em Step 1: Constructing uniform caps}.
Fix a bounded \(C^2\)-extension operator to a tubular neighbourhood
\(N_{\rho_{\rm ext}}(\overline\Omega)\) of size ${\rho_{\rm ext}}$, and continue to denote the
extension of \(\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0\) by \(\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0\). Put
\(M_2:=
\lVert D^2\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0 \rVert_{L^\infty
(N_{\rho_{\rm ext}}(\overline\Omega))}\).
The extension may be fixed so that
\(M_2
\leq
C_{\rm ext}(\Omega)
\lVert \textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0 \rVert_{C^2(\overline\Omega)}\). Let \(r_\Omega>0\) be the radius supplied by Corollary
\ref{correctors.uniform}, and set
\[
r_*
:=
\min\left\{
1,r_\Omega,\rho_{\rm ext},
\frac{\sigma_0}{2\max\{M_2,1\}}
\right\},
\qquad
\rho_0:=\min\{r_0,r_*\}.
\]
In particular, \(0<\rho_0\leq r_0\) and
\(\rho_0\leq r_\Omega\). Applying the construction in Corollary \ref{correctors.uniform} at
the fixed scale \(r=\rho_0\), we choose, for every
\(x_0\in\partial\Omega\), a \(C^2\)-regular boundary cap
\(\mathcal U_{x_0}\) such that
\eqn{ww.21}
$$
\Omega\cap B_{\rho_0/2}(x_0)
\subset
\mathcal U_{x_0}
\subset
\Omega\cap B_{\rho_0}(x_0),
\qquad
\partial\mathcal U_{x_0}\cap B_{\rho_0/2}(x_0)
=
\partial\Omega\cap B_{\rho_0/2}(x_0).
$$
By the same corollary, the family
\(\{\mathcal U_{x_0}\}_{x_0\in\partial\Omega}\)
has uniformly controlled \(C^2\)-geometry at the scale \(\rho_0\).
In particular, the caps are fixed solely in terms of
\(r_0,\sigma_0,
\lVert \textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0 \rVert_{C^2(\overline\Omega)},\Omega\),
and are independent of \(f,\mu\), and \(u\). Finally, by the definition of \(r_*\), for every
\(x_0\in\partial\Omega\) and every \(x\in B_{\rho_0}(x_0)\),
the fundamental theorem of calculus gives
\eqn{ww.24}
$$
\lvert \textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0(x)-\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0(x_0)\rvert
\leq
(1-\sigma_0/2)\lvert x-x_0\rvert.
$$ Fix now \(x_0\in\partial\Omega\); we shall often abbreviate 
\(\mathcal U_0:=\mathcal U_{x_0}\).
We first establish a quantitative spacelike inequality for the
boundary values of \(u\) on \(\partial\mathcal U_0\). Fix
\(x\in\partial\mathcal U_0\cap\Omega\), and define
\(
\gamma(t):=x_0+t(x-x_0)
\)
for \(t\in[0,1]\). Since \(\gamma(1)=x\in\Omega\), let \((t_x,1]\), with
$t_x\in[0,1)$, be the connected component containing \(1\) of
$
\{t\in(0,1]:\gamma(t)\in\Omega\}.
$
Then
$
\gamma(t_x)\in\partial\Omega,
$
where possibly \(t_x=0\), in which case
\(\gamma(t_x)=x_0\).
Notice that, by \eqref{ww.21},
$\rho_0/2\le\lvert x-x_0\rvert\le \rho_0.$
Since $\Omega$ has finite measure, the embedding
$L^\infty(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L(n,1)(\Omega)$ gives a constant
$c_{\Omega}=c(n,\Omega)$ such that
$$
[f+\mu]_{n,1;\Omega}
\leq
c_{\Omega}\lVert f \rVert_{L^\infty(\Omega)}
+
[\mu]_{n,1;\Omega}
\leq
c_{\Omega}F_0+\Lambda_0
=:
\Lambda_{\rm tot}.
$$
Apply Lemma \ref{ww.lem} once, at the already fixed scale
\(\textnormal{\texttt{l}}=\rho_0\), with
$M=1+\lVert \textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0 \rVert_{C^2(\overline\Omega)}$
and global charge bound $\Lambda_{\rm tot}$. Denote its constant by
$
\sigma_{\rm ch}
=
\sigma_{\rm ch}
(
n,\rho_0,\Lambda_{\rm tot},\sigma_0,
\lVert \textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0 \rVert_{C^2(\overline\Omega)},\Omega
)
\in(0,1).
$
If \(t_x\le1/2\), then
\(
\rho_0/4
\le
\lvert x-\gamma(t_x)\rvert
\le
\rho_0,
\)
and the estimate supplied by Lemma \ref{ww.lem} on
\(\overline{x\,\gamma(t_x)}\) gives
\begin{flalign*}
\lvert u(x)-u(x_0)\rvert
&\leq
\lvert u(x)-u(\gamma(t_x))\rvert
+
\lvert u(\gamma(t_x))-u(x_0)\rvert
\\
&\leq
(1-\sigma_{\rm ch})
\lvert x-\gamma(t_x)\rvert
+
\lvert \textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0(\gamma(t_x))-\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0(x_0)\rvert
\\
&\leq 
(1-\sigma_{\rm ch})
\lvert x-\gamma(t_x)\rvert
+
(
1- \sigma_0/2
)
\lvert \gamma(t_x)-x_0\rvert
\\
&\leq
(
1-\min\{\sigma_{\rm ch}, \sigma_0/2\}
)
\lvert x-x_0\rvert.
\end{flalign*}
Here we used the fact that
\(x\), \(\gamma(t_x)\), and \(x_0\) are collinear and \eqref{ww.24} in the third line. If \(t_x>1/2\), the weak spacelike inequality on
\(\overline{x\,\gamma(t_x)}\), together with
\eqref{ww.24}, gives
\begin{flalign*}
\lvert u(x)-u(x_0)\rvert&\le
\lvert u(x)-u(\gamma(t_x))\rvert
+
\lvert \textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0(\gamma(t_x))-\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0(x_0)\rvert
\\
&\le
\lvert x-\gamma(t_x)\rvert
+
\lvert \textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0(\gamma(t_x))-\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0(x_0)\rvert
\\
&\le
(1-t_x)\lvert x-x_0\rvert
+
(
1-\sigma_0/2
)
t_x\lvert x-x_0\rvert
\le
(
1- \sigma_0/4
)
\lvert x-x_0\rvert.
\end{flalign*}
In any case we conclude with 
\eqn{ww.23}
$$
\lvert u(x)-u(x_0)\rvert
\le
(1-\widetilde\sigma)\lvert x-x_0\rvert
\qquad
\mbox{for every }
x\in\partial\mathcal U_0\cap\Omega,
$$
where
\(
\widetilde\sigma
:=
\min\{\sigma_{\rm ch},\sigma_0\}/4.
\) We now construct a suitable extension of the boundary datum
\(\left.u\right|_{\partial\mathcal U_0}\). Define
\(
R_0
:=
\overline{\mathcal U}_0
\cap
\{\rho_0/4\le\lvert x-x_0\rvert\le \rho_0\}
\)
and note that
\(
\partial R_0
:=
(\partial\mathcal U_0\cap R_0)
\cup
(\overline{\mathcal U}_0\cap
\partial B_{\rho_0/4}(x_0)).
\)
On \(\partial R_0\), set
$$
\tau(x)
:=
\begin{cases}
\displaystyle
\frac{u(x)-\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0(x_0)}{\lvert x-x_0\rvert}
&
x\in\partial\mathcal U_0\cap R_0,
\\[3mm]
\displaystyle
\frac{
4(\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0(x)-\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0(x_0))
}{\rho_0}
&
x\in
\overline{\mathcal U}_0
\cap
\partial B_{\rho_0/4}(x_0).
\end{cases}
$$
The two definitions agree on their common domain. Indeed,
$$
\partial\mathcal U_0
\cap
\partial B_{\rho_0/4}(x_0)
\subset
\partial\Omega
\cap
B_{\rho_0/2}(x_0),
$$
and hence
$ u=\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0$
there. Thus
$\tau\in C^{0}(\partial R_0).$
Moreover, \eqref{ww.23} applies on the artificial part of
\(\partial\mathcal U_0\), while \eqref{ww.24} applies on its physical
part. Hence
\(
\lVert \tau \rVert_{C^0(\partial R_0)}
\le1-\widetilde\sigma.
\)
By the Tietze extension theorem, there exists
\(\tau_0\in C^0(\overline{R_0})\) such that
\eqn{ww.26}
$$
\tau_0=\tau
\quad\mbox{on }\partial R_0,
\qquad
\lVert \tau_0 \rVert_{C^0(\overline{R_0})}
\le
1-\widetilde\sigma.
$$
Define, for \(x\in\overline{\mathcal U}_0\),
$$
\mathfrak{u}_0(x)
:=
\begin{cases}
\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0(x)
&\lvert x-x_0\rvert\le \rho_0/4
\\[1pt]
\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0(x_0)+\tau_0(x)\lvert x-x_0\rvert&
\lvert x-x_0\rvert\ge \rho_0/4.
\end{cases}
$$
By the definition of \(\tau_0\), the two expressions agree on
\(\overline{\mathcal U}_0\cap \partial B_{\rho_0/4}(x_0)\), and hence
\(
\mathfrak{u}_0
\in C^{0}(\overline{\mathcal U}_0).
\)
Furthermore,
$
\mathfrak{u}_0=u
$
on $\partial\mathcal U_0.$
Indeed, this follows from
\(\mathfrak{u}_0=\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0=u\) on
\(\partial\mathcal U_0\cap B_{\rho_0/4}(x_0)\), and from
\(\tau_0=\tau\) on the remaining part of
\(\partial\mathcal U_0\). By \eqref{ww.24} and \eqref{ww.26},
$$
\lvert 
\mathfrak{u}_0(x)
-
\mathfrak{u}_0(x_0)
\rvert
\le
(1-\widetilde\sigma)\lvert x-x_0\rvert
\qquad
\mbox{for every }
x\in\overline{\mathcal U}_0.
$$
Moreover,
\(\mathfrak{u}_0=\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0\) in
\(\overline{\mathcal U}_0\cap B_{\rho_0/4}(x_0)\), and therefore
$$
\mathfrak u_0=\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0
\quad\mbox{on }
\overline{\mathcal U}_0\cap B_{\rho_0/4}(x_0),
\qquad
\lVert 
D^2\mathfrak{u}_0
 \rVert_{
C^0(
\overline{\mathcal U}_0
\cap
B_{\rho_0/8}(x_0)
)}
\le
M_2.
$$
We now apply the quantitative barrier construction in
\cite[proof of Proposition~3.1]{bs82}, with curvature bound
\(A=F_0\), boundary datum
\(\left.u\right|_{\partial\mathcal U_0}\), extension
\(\mathfrak u_0\), cone gap \(\widetilde\sigma\), and local
\(C^2\)-bound \(M_2\). The construction gives upper and lower
barriers
\(\mathcal w_{f,x_0}^{\pm}
\in C^2(\overline{\mathcal U}_0)\) that solve
\eqn{solvef}
$$
-\mathcal M\mathcal w_{f,x_0}^{+}=:\mathfrak f_{x_0}^{+}, \qquad -\mathcal M\mathcal w_{f,x_0}^{-}=:\mathfrak f_{x_0}^{-}
$$
and that satisfy
\eqn{ww.bs.f.sources}
$$
\mathfrak f_{x_0}^-
\leq
f
\leq
\mathfrak f_{x_0}^+
\qquad
\mbox{a.e. in }\mathcal U_0.
$$
Note that $\mathfrak f_{x_0}^{+}$ and $\mathfrak f_{x_0}^{-}$ are precisely defined via (\ref{solvef}). 
Since \(\rho_0,\widetilde\sigma,M_2,F_0\), and the quantitative
\(C^2\)-geometry of the caps are uniform with respect to \(x_0\),
the parameters in the same barrier construction can be chosen
uniformly. Consequently, there exist
\(\mathcal s_f\in(0,1)\) and \(K_f<\infty\), only depending on
$
n,
\rho_0,
\widetilde\sigma,
M_2,
F_0,
\Omega,
$
such that
\eqn{ww.bs.f.bounds}
$$
\lVert D\mathcal w_{f,x_0}^{\pm} \rVert_
{L^\infty(\mathcal U_0)}
\leq
1-\mathcal s_f,
\qquad
\rho_0
\lVert D^2\mathcal w_{f,x_0}^{\pm} \rVert_
{L^\infty(\mathcal U_0)}
\leq
K_f.
$$
In particular,
\(\mathfrak f_{x_0}^{\pm}\in L^\infty(\mathcal U_0)\).
Moreover,
\eqn{ww.bs.f.boundary}
$$
\begin{cases}
\mathcal w_{f,x_0}^+(x)>u(x)
&
x\in\partial\mathcal U_0\setminus\{x_0\},
\\[1mm]
\mathcal w_{f,x_0}^-(x)<u(x)
&
x\in\partial\mathcal U_0\setminus\{x_0\},
\\[1mm]
\mathcal w_{f,x_0}^\pm(x_0)=u(x_0).
\end{cases}
$$

\medskip


{\em Step 2: Correctors}.
Since \(x_0\in\partial\Omega\) was arbitrary, Step~1 produces two
families
\(\{\mathcal w_{f,x_0}^+\}_{x_0\in\partial\Omega}\) and
\(\{\mathcal w_{f,x_0}^-\}_{x_0\in\partial\Omega}\), defined on the
corresponding caps \(\mathcal U_{x_0}\), and satisfying (\ref{solvef}) and 
\eqref{ww.bs.f.bounds} with the same constants
\(\mathcal s_f\) and \(K_f\). We are now ready to check the applicability of Corollary \ref{correctors.uniform} to such a family. To this aim we also define \(\mu_+:=\max\{\mu,0\}\) and
\(\mu_-:=\max\{-\mu,0\}\) and in the setting of Corollary \ref{correctors.uniform} we choose $\mu_{x_0}\equiv \pm\mu_{\pm}\lfloor_{\mathcal U_{x_0}}$. Let \(\varepsilon_{\rm \partial}\) be the threshold supplied by
Corollary \ref{correctors.uniform}, applied with
\(r\equiv \rho_0\), \(\mathcal s\equiv \mathcal s_f\), and \(K\equiv K_f\) and this determines the number $\varepsilon_{\partial}$ used here in (\ref{ww.31}). 
Hence \eqref{ww.41} and \eqref{ww.31}  give
\eqn{ww.local.mu}
$$
[\pm\mu_{\pm}\lfloor_{\mathcal U_{x_0}}]_{n,1}=[\pm\mu_\pm]_{n,1;\mathcal U_{x_0}}
\leq [\mu]_{n,1;\mathcal U_{x_0}}
\leq [\mu]_{n,1;\Omega_{r_0}}
\leq \varepsilon_\partial
\quad
\mbox{for every }x_0\in\partial\Omega.
$$
In view of \eqref{ww.local.mu},
Corollary \ref{correctors.uniform} yields correctors 
$\mathcal b_{x_0}^{\pm}\in
W^{2;n,1}(\mathcal U_{x_0})\cap
W^{1;n,1}_0(\mathcal U_{x_0})$ such that 
\eqn{ww.upper.corrector}
$$
-\mathcal M(\mathcal w_{f,x_0}^{\pm}+\mathcal b_{x_0}^{\pm})
=
\mathfrak f^{\pm}_{x_0}\pm\mu_{\pm}\lfloor_{\mathcal U_{x_0}},
\qquad
\lVert D\mathcal b_{x_0}^{\pm} \rVert_{L^\infty(\mathcal U_{x_0})}
\leq
\frac{\mathcal s_f}{4},
\qquad
\mathcal b_{x_0}^+\geq0, \ \ 
\mathcal b_{x_0}^-\leq0. 
$$
Given a generic but fixed \(x_0\), we abbreviate
\(\mathcal w^\pm:=\mathcal w_{f,x_0}^\pm\),
\(\mathfrak f^\pm:=\mathfrak f_{x_0}^\pm\), and
\(\mathcal b^\pm:=\mathcal b_{x_0}^\pm\), and we continue to denote
the restrictions of \(\mu_\pm\) to \(\mathcal U_{x_0}\) by
\(\mu_\pm\).
Define
$
\mathcal W^\pm:=\mathcal w^\pm+\mathcal b^\pm.
$
By \eqref{ww.bs.f.bounds} and \eqref{ww.upper.corrector},
\eqn{ww.corrected.gap}
$$
\lVert D\mathcal W^\pm \rVert_{L^\infty(\mathcal U_{x_0})}
\leq
1-\mathcal s_f+\frac{\mathcal s_f}{4}=1-\frac{3\mathcal s_f}{4}<1.
$$
Since $\mathcal b^\pm\equiv0$ on $\partial\mathcal U_{x_0}$,
\eqref{ww.bs.f.boundary} remains valid with $\mathcal W^\pm$
in place of $\mathcal w^\pm$. Moreover, by \eqref{ww.bs.f.sources} and
\eqref{ww.upper.corrector}, we have 
$$
-\mathcal M\mathcal W^-=\mathfrak f^--\mu_-
\leq f+\mu \leq
\mathfrak f^++\mu_+ =
-\mathcal M\mathcal W^+.
$$
For $\delta>0$, set
$v_\delta:=(u-\mathcal W^+-\delta)_+$, so that
$\,{\rm supp }\,v_\delta\Subset\mathcal U_{x_0}$.
We may therefore test the difference of the equations with
$v_\delta$, obtaining
$$
0\leq
\int_{\mathcal U_{x_0}}
\langle \partial H(Du)-\partial H(D\mathcal W^+),
Dv_\delta \rangle\,{\rm d}x =\int_{\mathcal U_{x_0}}
(f+\mu-\mathfrak f^+-\mu_+)v_\delta\,{\rm d}x
\leq0.
$$
Strict monotonicity gives $Dv_\delta=0$ almost everywhere,
hence $v_\delta=0$.
Letting $\delta\downarrow0$, we obtain $u\leq\mathcal W^+$.
The analogous argument with
$(\mathcal W^--u-\delta)_+$
gives $\mathcal W^-\leq u$. Therefore,
$
\mathcal W^-\leq u\leq\mathcal W^+
$
in $\overline{\mathcal U}_{x_0}$. By the endpoint Sobolev--Lorentz embedding,
$\mathcal b^\pm\in C^1(\overline{\mathcal U}_{x_0})$.
At $x_0$, the tangential derivatives of
$\mathcal W^-$, $u$, and $\mathcal W^+$ coincide.
If $\mathcal{v}$ denotes the common inward unit normal to
$\partial\Omega$ and $\partial\mathcal U_{x_0}$ at $x_0$, then
$
D_\mathcal{v}\mathcal W^-(x_0) \leq D_\mathcal{v} u(x_0) \leq D_\mathcal{v}\mathcal W^+(x_0).
$
It follows from \eqref{ww.corrected.gap} that
$$
\lvert Du(x_0)\rvert \leq \max\left\{
\lvert D\mathcal W^-(x_0)\rvert,
\lvert D\mathcal W^+(x_0)\rvert
\right\} \leq
1-\frac{3\mathcal s_f}{4}.
$$
Since $x_0\in\partial\Omega$ was arbitrary,
\eqref{ww.boundary.gap} follows with
$
\sigma_*:=3\mathcal s_f/4.
$
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- remark-15: body_md -->
{\em Perturbative solvability arguments near a prescribed reference graph also appear in \cite[Theorem 1]{tsu20}, in the Euclidean setting and with \(W^{2,q}\) estimates for \(q>n\). Moreover, in the setting of uniformly elliptic problems, a related barrier perturbation argument appears in \cite[Propositions~4.1--4.2]{bmw16}, where inhomogeneous barriers for Pucci operators are compared with homogeneous ones, controlling the error for sources in $L^q$, $q>n$.}
<!-- end remark-15 -->
<!-- corollary-6: body_md -->
\label{app.cor}
Let $\beta\in(0,1)$, let
$\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a 
$C^{2,\beta}$-regular, bounded domain, and let $\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0\in C^{2,\beta}(\overline\Omega)$ be such that  \eqref{assuntidir}$_2$ is satisfied. 
Fix $\Lambda_0,F_0, r_0>0$, and let
$\varepsilon_\partial$ and $\sigma_*$ be the quantities determined in
\eqref{piccole} according to Proposition \ref{corr}. 
Assume that
$f,\mu\in C^{0,\beta}(\overline\Omega)$,
$\lVert f \rVert_{L^\infty(\Omega)}\leq F_0$,  and that
\eqn{ww.corollary.data}
$$
[\mu]_{n,1;\Omega}\leq\Lambda_0,
\qquad
[\mu]_{n,1;\Omega_{r_0}}\leq \varepsilon_\partial.
$$
Then the solution $u$ to \eqref{ww.background.perturbed.problem} 
belongs to $C^{2,\beta}(\overline\Omega)$. Moreover, there exists 
$
\varepsilon_*
\equiv 
\varepsilon_*
(\sigma_*,[Du]_{C^{0,\beta}(\overline\Omega)},\beta)\in (0,1)
$
such that
\eqn{ww.50}
$$
\lVert Du \rVert_{
L^\infty\left(
\left\{
x\in\overline\Omega:
\,{\rm dist}(x,\partial\Omega)\leq\varepsilon_*
\right\}
\right)}
\leq
1-\frac{\sigma_*}{2}.
$$
 Moreover, 
\eqn{sottosotto}
$$
\mathcal h_u
\le
\frac{\mathcal h_{\partial\Omega}}{2} \quad \mbox{on $\overline\Omega
\cap
\left\{
\,{\rm dist}(\,\cdot\,,\partial\Omega)
\le
\varepsilon_*
\right\}$},
$$
where 
\eqn{boundary.boost.threshold}
$$
2< \mathcal h_{\partial\Omega}
:=
\frac{
2}{\sqrt{1-\left(1-\frac{\sigma_*}{2}\right)^2}}
=
\frac{2}{
\sqrt{\sigma_*
\left(1-\frac{\sigma_*}{4}
\right)}}\leq \frac{4}{\sqrt{3\sigma_*}}.
$$
In particular, 
$
\,{\rm supp }\, 
\left(\mathcal h_u-\kappa\right)_+\Subset\Omega
$
for every $
\kappa\ge
\mathcal h_{\partial\Omega}/2.$ Finally, $\,{\rm supp }\, 
\left(
\mathcal h_u-\mathcal h_{\partial\Omega}
\right)_+
\Subset
\Omega.$
<!-- end corollary-6 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
Under the assumptions of the corollary,
\cite[Theorem 3.6]{bs82} gives
$u\in C^{2,\beta}(\overline\Omega)$ and
$Du\in C^{0,\beta}(\overline\Omega)$. In view of \eqref{ww.corollary.data},
Proposition \ref{corr} applies and gives
$
\lVert Du \rVert_{C^0(\partial\Omega)}
\leq
1-\sigma_*.
$
Set
$$
M_{Du}
:= [Du]_{C^{0,\beta}(\overline\Omega)},
\qquad
\varepsilon_*
:=\min\left\{1,\left(\frac{\sigma_*}{2\max\{M_{Du},1\}}\right)^{1/\beta}\right\}.
$$
If $\,{\rm dist}(x,\partial\Omega)\leq\varepsilon_*$, choose
$y\in\partial\Omega$ with
$\lvert x-y\rvert=\,{\rm dist}(x,\partial\Omega)$. Then
$
\lvert Du(x)\rvert
\leq
\lvert Du(y)\rvert
+
M_{Du}\lvert x-y\rvert^{\beta}
\leq
1-\sigma_*+\sigma_*/2
=
1- \sigma_*/2.
$
This proves \eqref{ww.50}. This implies 
$$
\mathcal h_u\le\left[1-\left(1-\frac{\sigma_*}{2}\right)^2\right]^{-1/2}=\frac{\mathcal h_{\partial\Omega}}{2}
$$
in the corresponding boundary neighbourhood. Hence every truncation
with
$
\kappa\ge\mathcal h_{\partial\Omega}/2
$
vanishes there, and its support is contained in
$
\{
x\in\overline\Omega:
\,{\rm dist}(x,\partial\Omega)\ge\varepsilon_*
\}
\Subset
\Omega.
$
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{cacclem}
Let $u\in \mathbb{X}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ be a classical solution to \eqref{bi} with $\mu \in C^{\infty}_0(\mathbb R^n)$. Then
\begin{flalign}\label{cacc}
&(m+1)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\eta^{2}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{m-2}\lvert D\mathcal{h}_{u}\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d}x+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\eta^{2}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{m+2}\lvert D^{2}u\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d}x\nonumber \\
&\qquad \quad \le c\max\left\{1,\frac{1}{1+m}\right\}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{m+2}\lvert D\eta\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d}x+c\max\left\{1,1+m\right\}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\eta^{2}\mu^{2}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{m}\,{\rm d}x
\end{flalign}
holds whenever $m> -1$, $\eta\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ is nonnegative and compactly supported,
where \(c=c_n\).
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
Since $\mu\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$, by Proposition \ref{exex} we know that $u$ is smooth. We can then differentiate \eqref{weshall2} to achieve
\eqn{0}
$$
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\langle\partial^{2}H(Du)DD_{s}u,D\varphi\rangle\,{\rm d}x=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mu D_{s}\varphi\,{\rm d}x=
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}D_{s} \mu \varphi\,{\rm d}x,
$$
for all $\varphi\in C^{1}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ and any $s\in \{1,\cdots,n\}$. Note that, by a standard density argument, \eqref{0} remains valid for every
compactly supported $\varphi\in W^{1,2}(\mathbb R^n)$. Recalling (\ref{recalla}), we shall repeatedly use the relations
\eqn{identitas}
$$
D\mathcal h_u
=
\mathcal h_u^3D^2u\,Du, \qquad \mathcal H(Du)D\mathcal h_u
=
\sum_{s=1}^n
D_su\,\partial^2H(Du)DD_su.
$$
Let $\eta\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ be a non-negative, compactly supported function, $m> -1$ be a number, test \eqref{0} against $\varphi:=\eta^{2}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{m+1}D_{s}u$ and sum over $s\in \{1,\cdots,n\}$ to get, via (\ref{identitas})
\begin{flalign*}
0&=2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\eta\mathcal{h}_{u}^{m+1}\langle\mathcal H(Du)D\mathcal h_{u},D\eta \rangle\,{\rm d}x\nonumber +(m+1) \int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\eta^{2}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{m}\langle\mathcal H(Du)D\mathcal h_{u},D\mathcal{h}_{u}\rangle\,{\rm d}x\nonumber \\
&\ \  \ +\sum_{s=1}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\eta^{2}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{m+1}\langle\partial^{2}H(Du)DD_{s}u,DD_{s}u\rangle\,{\rm d}x\nonumber\\
&\ \ \ +\sum_{s=1}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mu D_{s}(\eta^{2}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{m+1}D_{s}u)\,{\rm d}x=:\mbox{(I)}+\mbox{(II)}+\mbox{(III)}+\mbox{(IV)}.
\end{flalign*}  
We estimate via Cauchy-Schwarz and Young inequalities,
\begin{eqnarray*}
\lvert \mbox{(I)}\rvert
&\le&2\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\eta^{2}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{m}\langle\mathcal H(Du)D\mathcal{h}_{u},D\mathcal{h}_{u}\rangle\,{\rm d}x\right)^{1/2}\nonumber\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{m+2}\langle\mathcal H(Du)D\eta,D\eta\rangle\,{\rm d}x\right)^{1/2}\nonumber \\
&\stackrel{\eqref{0.1}_{2}}{\le}&\varepsilon_{1}\mbox{(II)}\nonumber  +\frac{c}{\varepsilon_{1}(m+1)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{m+2}\lvert D\eta\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d}x,
\end{eqnarray*}
for some $\varepsilon_{1}\in (0,1)$ to be fixed and $c\equiv c_{n}$. 
Finally, 
$$
\mbox{(II)} \geq (m+1)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\eta^{2}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{m-2}\lvert D\mathcal{h}_{u}\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d}x, \qquad 
\mbox{(III)}\stackrel{\eqref{0.1.1}_{1}}{\ge}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\eta^{2}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{m+2}\lvert D^{2}u\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d}x. 
$$
Let us now take care of the terms involving \(\mu\). Expanding
\(\mathrm{(IV)}\), we have
\begin{flalign*}
|\mathrm{(IV)}|
&\le
2\int_{\mathbb R^n}
\eta|\mu|\mathcal h_u^{m+1}
|D\eta||Du|\,{\rm d}x
+(m+1)\int_{\mathbb R^n}
\eta^2|\mu|\mathcal h_u^m
|\langle Du,D\mathcal h_u\rangle|\,{\rm d}x
\\
& \qquad 
+\int_{\mathbb R^n}
\eta^2|\mu|\mathcal h_u^{m+1}
|\Delta u|\,{\rm d}x.
\end{flalign*}
Using \(|Du|\le1\), the identity
\(\mathcal H(Du)Du=Du\), the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality associated
with \(\mathcal H(Du)\), and Young's inequality, we obtain
\begin{flalign*}
|\mathrm{(IV)}|
&\le
c\int_{\mathbb R^n}
\mathcal h_u^{m+2}|D\eta|^2\,{\rm d}x
+
c\int_{\mathbb R^n}
\eta^2\mu^2\mathcal h_u^m\,{\rm d}x
+\varepsilon_2\mathrm{(II)}
+
\frac{c(m+1)}{\varepsilon_2}
\int_{\mathbb R^n}
\eta^2\mu^2\mathcal h_u^m\,{\rm d}x
\\
& \quad 
+\varepsilon_3\mathrm{(III)}
+
\frac{c}{\varepsilon_3}
\int_{\mathbb R^n}
\eta^2\mu^2\mathcal h_u^m\,{\rm d}x
\\
&\le 
c\int_{\mathbb R^n}
\mathcal h_u^{m+2}|D\eta|^2\,{\rm d}x
+
c\left(
1+\frac{m+1}{\varepsilon_2}
+\frac1{\varepsilon_3}
\right)
\int_{\mathbb R^n}
\eta^2\mu^2\mathcal h_u^m\,{\rm d}x
+\varepsilon_2\mathrm{(II)}
+\varepsilon_3\mathrm{(III)},
\end{flalign*}
for $c\equiv c_{n}$, and some $\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{3}\in (0,1)$. Merging the content of all previous displays after choosing $\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{3}\in (0,1)$ sufficiently small, and recalling $\eqref{0.1}_{1}$, we obtain \eqref{cacc}.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{w22.lem}
Let $u\in \mathbb{X}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ be a classical solution to \eqref{bi} with $\mu \in C^{\infty}_0(\mathbb R^n)$. Let
\(B\subset\mathbb R^n\) be a ball. If \(n\ge3\) and \(q\in[2,\infty)\), then
\eqn{w22.high}
$$
\begin{aligned}
&\int_B
(|D\log\mathcal h_u|^2+|D\mathcal h_u^{-1}|^2+|D^2u|^2)\,{\rm d}x\\
&\qquad\le c\mathcal h_\infty(\mu)^2
|B|^{1-2/n}+c|B|^{1-2/q}
\left(1+\frac{\mathfrak{m}_\mu^2}{|B|^{2/n}}
\right)\|\mu\|_{L^q(\mathbb R^n)}^2,
\end{aligned}
$$
where \(c=c(n,q)\). If \(n=2\), then
\eqn{w22.two}
$$
\begin{aligned}
&\int_B
(|D\log\mathcal h_u|^2+|D\mathcal h_u^{-1}|^2+|D^2u|^2)\,{\rm d}x
\\
&\qquad\le
c\mathcal h_\infty(\mu)^2
+c\left[1+ |B|^{-1}\mathfrak{m}_\mu^2\log^2(e+\mathfrak{m}_\mu)\right]
\|\mu\|_{L_x^2(\mathbb R^2)}^2,
\end{aligned}
$$
where \(c>0\) is a dimensional constant.
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
Fix a ball \(B\subset\mathbb R^n\); as clarified in Section \ref{notazioni} we denote by \(2B\)  the
concentric ball with twice the radius. Choose
\(\eta\in W^{1,\infty}_0(2B)\) such that
$
0\le\eta\le1$, $
\eta=1$ in $B$, 
$|D\eta|\le c|B|^{-1/n}.$
Using that 
$
|D\mathcal h_u^{-1}|^2
\le
\mathcal h_u^2|Du|^2|D^2u|^2,
$
since
$
1+\mathcal h_u^2|Du|^2=\mathcal h_u^2,$
it follows that
$
|D\mathcal h_u^{-1}|^2+|D^2u|^2\le\mathcal h_u^2|D^2u|^2.
$
Therefore, applying \eqref{cacc} with \(m=0\), we obtain
\eqn{w22.aux}
$$
\begin{aligned}
&\int_B
(|D\log\mathcal h_u|^2+|D\mathcal h_u^{-1}|^2+|D^2u|^2)\,{\rm d}x
\\
&\qquad\le c
\mathcal h_\infty(\mu)^2
|B|^{1-2/n}
+
c|B|^{-2/n}
\int_{2B}(\mathcal h_u-\mathcal h_\infty(\mu))_+^2\,{\rm d}x
+
c\int_{2B}\mu^2\,{\rm d}x.
\end{aligned}
$$


Assume first that \(n\ge3\). By H\"older's inequality and \eqref{czcz},
\begin{flalign*}
\int_{2B}(\mathcal h_u-\mathcal h_\infty(\mu))_+^2\,{\rm d}x
&\le
c|B|^{1-2/q}
\|(\mathcal h_u-\mathcal h_\infty(\mu))_+\|_{L^q(\mathbb R^n)}^2\\
& \le
c|B|^{1-2/q}
\frac{\mathfrak{m}_\mu^2}
{(1-\mathcal h_\infty(\mu)^{-2})^2}
\|\mu\|_{L^q(\mathbb R^n)}^2\leq c
|B|^{1-2/q}
\mathfrak{m}_\mu^2 \|\mu\|_{L^q(\mathbb R^n)}^2.
\end{flalign*}
Note that we have used that   
$
\mathcal h_\infty(\mu)\ge2,
$
and hence
$
1-\mathcal h_\infty(\mu)^{-2}
\ge
3/4.
$
Similarly, 
$$
\|\mu\|_{L^2(2B)}^2
\le
c|B|^{1-2/q}
\|\mu\|_{L^q(\mathbb R^n)}^2.
$$
Inserting the last two estimates into
\eqref{w22.aux} gives \eqref{w22.high}. Similarly, when \(n=2\) by \eqref{mm.20} we have 
$$
\|(\mathcal h_u-\mathcal h_\infty(\mu))_+\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^2)}^2
\le
c
\mathfrak{m}_\mu^2
\log^2(e+\mathfrak{m}_\mu)
\|\mu\|_{L_x^2(\mathbb R^2)}^2.
$$
Moreover,
$
\log2\|\mu\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^2)}
\le
\|\mu\|_{L_x^2(\mathbb R^2)}.
$
Combining these estimates with
\eqref{w22.aux} yields \eqref{w22.two}.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label{caccim} Let $u\in \mathbb{X}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ be a classical solution to \eqref{bi} with $\mu \in C^{\infty}_0(\mathbb R^n)$. Assume that $n\geq 3$. 
Consider $x_0\in \mathbb R^n$ and the condition
\eqn{smallina}
$$\lVert \mu \rVert_{L^{n}(B_{r}(x_{0}))}\le \lVert \textnormal{\texttt{g}} \rVert_{L^{n}(B_{2r}(x_{0}))},
$$
where $\textnormal{\texttt{g}}\in L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ is a fixed function.  
For every $m>-1$ there exists a positive threshold 
\eqn{lasoglia}
$$r_{*}\equiv r_{*}(n,m,\textnormal{\texttt{g}})\in (0,1]$$ 
such that if \eqref{smallina} is satisfied for every $r \leq r_{*}$, then the Caccioppoli type inequality
 \eqn{cacc.c}
 $$
 \int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\eta^{2}\left(\mathcal{h}_{u}^{m}+\lvert D\mathcal{h}_{u}^{m/2}\rvert^{2}\right)\,{\rm d}x+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\eta^{2}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{m+2}\lvert D^{2}u\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d}x\le c\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{m+2}\lvert D\eta\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d}x
 $$
 holds true for all non-negative $\eta\in W^{1,\infty}_{0}(B_{5r/6}(x_{0}))$ such that $r\leq r_{*}$, where $c\equiv c(n,m)$. In particular,  the choice $\textnormal{\texttt{g}}\equiv \mu$ is admissible. Consequently \eqn{ccc}
$$
\lVert \mathcal{h}_{u}^{m/2} \rVert_{W^{1,2}(B_{3r/4}(x_{0}))}\le c(n,m)r^{-1}\lVert \mathcal{h}_{u}^{m/2+1} \rVert_{L^{2}(B_{5r/6}(x_{0}))}. 
$$
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
With $\eta$ as in the statement, triangle inequality and \eqref{cacc} yield
\begin{flalign}\label{caccr.3}
&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\lvert D(\eta\mathcal{h}_{u}^{m/2})\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d}x+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\eta^{2}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{m+2}\lvert D^{2}u\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d}x\nonumber \\
&\qquad \qquad\quad\qquad \quad \le c\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\eta^{2}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{m-2}\lvert D\mathcal{h}_{u}\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d}x+c\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\lvert D\eta\rvert^{2}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{m}\,{\rm d}x\nonumber   +c\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\eta^{2}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{m+2}\lvert D^{2}u\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d}x\nonumber \\
&\qquad\qquad \qquad\quad \quad\le c\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{m+2}\lvert D\eta\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d}x+c\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\eta^{2}\mu^{2}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{m}\,{\rm d}x\nonumber \\
&\qquad \qquad\qquad\quad \quad \le c\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{m+2}\lvert D\eta\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d}x+c\lVert \mu \rVert_{L^{n}(\,{\rm supp } \, \eta )}^{2}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\eta^{2^{*}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{2^{*}m/2}\,{\rm d}x\right)^{2/2^{*}}\nonumber \\
&\qquad \qquad\qquad\quad \quad \le c\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{m+2}\lvert D\eta\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d}x+c\lVert \textnormal{\texttt{g}} \rVert_{L^{n}(B_{2r_{*}})}^{2}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\eta^{2^{*}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{2^{*}m/2}\,{\rm d}x\right)^{2/2^{*}},
\end{flalign}  
with $c\equiv c(n,m)$. By \eqref{cacc}, \eqref{caccr.3} and Sobolev embedding,
\begin{flalign}\label{cacc.4} 
&\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\eta^{2^{*}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{2^{*}m/2}\,{\rm d}x\right)^{2/2^{*}}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\eta^{2}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{m-2}\lvert D\mathcal{h}_{u}\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d}x+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\eta^{2}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{m+2}\lvert D^{2}u\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d}x\nonumber \\
&\qquad \qquad  \quad \le \tilde c\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{m+2}\lvert D\eta\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d}x+\tilde c\lVert \textnormal{\texttt{g}} \rVert_{L^{n}(B_{2r_{*}})}^{2}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\eta^{2^{*}}\mathcal{h}_{u}^{2^{*}m/2}\,{\rm d}x\right)^{2/2^{*}},
\end{flalign}
where $\tilde{c}\equiv \tilde{c}(n,m)$. By absolute continuity of the integral, choose
\(r_*=r_*(n,m,\textnormal{\texttt{g}})\in(0,1]\) such that
\[
\widetilde c\sup_{x\in\mathbb R^n}
\|\textnormal{\texttt{g}}\|_{L^n(B_{2r_*}(x))}^{2}
\le \frac12.
\]
We can then absorb the last term on the right-hand side
of \eqref{cacc.4} and obtain \eqref{cacc.c}
by standard manipulations. As \eqref{cacc.c} holds for all non-negative $\eta\in W^{1,\infty}_{0}(B_{5r/6}(x_{0}))$, we just select one such that $\mathds{1}_{B_{3r/4}(x_{0})}\le \eta\le \mathds{1}_{B_{5r/6}(x_{0})}$ and $\lvert D\eta\rvert\lesssim r^{-1}$, and (\ref{ccc}) follows directly from \eqref{cacc.c}.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{bslem}
Let $n\ge3$, let $0<\tau_1<\tau_2<\infty$, and define
\eqn{gamma}
$$
\vartheta:=
\begin{cases}
\dfrac{n-3}{n-1} & n\ge4\\[2mm]
\vartheta_0\in(0,1) & n=3.
\end{cases}
$$
Let $v\ge0$ satisfy
$
v,v^\vartheta\in W^{1,2}(B_{\tau_2}).
$
Then
$$
\inf_{\substack{
\eta\in C^1_0(B_{\tau_2})\\
\mathds{1}_{B_{\tau_1}}\le\eta\le\mathds{1}_{B_{\tau_2}}
}}
\lVert vD\eta \rVert_{L^2(B_{\tau_2})}^{2}
\le
\frac{c}
{\tau_2^{(n-1)(1-\vartheta)/\vartheta}
(\tau_2-\tau_1)^{(1+\vartheta)/\vartheta}}
\left(
\tau_2^{2/\vartheta}
\lVert Dv^\vartheta \rVert_{L^2(B_{\tau_2})}^{2/\vartheta}
+
\lVert v^\vartheta \rVert_{L^2(B_{\tau_2})}^{2/\vartheta}
\right),
$$
where $c=c(n)$ if $n\ge4$, and $c=c(\vartheta_0)$ if $n=3$.
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proposition-10: body_md -->
\label{p4.4} Within the same setting as Lemma \ref{caccim}, for every $p>1$ there exists a positive threshold 
\eqn{lasoglia2}
$$r_{p}\equiv r_{p}(n,p,\textnormal{\texttt{g}})\in (0,1]$$  such that if \eqref{smallina} is satisfied for every $r\leq r_p$, then   
\eqn{hiiigh}
$$
\lVert \mathcal{h}_{u}^{p/2} \rVert_{W^{1,2}(B_{r/8}(x_0))}\le cr^{-\gamma_p}\left(\mathcal{h}_{\infty}(\mu)+\mathfrak{m}_\mu\lVert \mu \rVert_{L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\right)^{\textnormal{\texttt{b}}_{p}}
$$
holds whenever $r \leq r_p$, where $c,\textnormal{\texttt{b}}_{p}, \gamma_p\equiv c,\textnormal{\texttt{b}}_{p},\gamma_p(n,p)\geq 1$.
<!-- end proposition-10 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
Fix \(p\in[1,\infty)\). Let \(\vartheta\in(0,1)\) be defined in
\eqref{gamma}, choosing \(\vartheta_0=1/4\) when \(n=3\), and set
$
m_0:=n-2, 
$
so that 
$
m_0> 2\vartheta/(1-\vartheta).
$
Define recursively
\eqn{4.1}
$$
m_{i+1}:=\frac{m_i}{\vartheta}-2,
\qquad
i\in\mathbb N\cup\{0\}.
$$
A direct induction gives
\eqn{4}
$$
m_i=\frac{1}{\vartheta^{i}}
\left(m_0-\frac{2\vartheta}{1-\vartheta}\right)
+\frac{2\vartheta}{1-\vartheta},\qquad
i\in\mathbb N\cup\{0\},
$$
and hence
$
m_i\to\infty.
$
Choose
$$
i_p:=\min\{i\ge1:m_i\ge p\},
\qquad \mathfrak{m}_{p}:=m_{i_p},
\qquad
(0,1]\ni r_p:=\min_{0\le j\le i_p}r_*(n,m_j,\textnormal{\texttt{g}}).
$$
Here $r_*(n,m_j,\textnormal{\texttt{g}})$ is the threshold in
\eqref{lasoglia} corresponding to $m=m_j$.
This determines the number $r_p$ appearing in
\eqref{lasoglia2}.
By the proof of Lemma \ref{caccim}, estimate
\eqref{cacc.c} holds with \(m\equiv m_i\), \(0\le i\le i_p\),
for every \(0<r\le r_p\), at the fixed center \(x_0\). Fix such a ball \(B_r(x_0)\), and choose
$
 r/8\le\tau_1<\tau_2\le 3r/4.
$ In the following all the balls will be centered at $x_0$ and we shall omit to denote the center $B_r\equiv B_r(x_0)$. 
For \(i\in\mathbb N\cup\{0\}\), define
$
\varrho_i
:=
\tau_1+(\tau_2-\tau_1)/2^{i}$, 
$
B_i:=B_{\varrho_i}.
$
Then
$
\varrho_0=\tau_2$, $
\varrho_i\searrow\tau_1$, $
\varrho_i-\varrho_{i+1} = (\tau_2-\tau_1)/2^{i+1}.
$ 
 For \(i\in\{0,\ldots,i_p-1\}\), apply Lemma \ref{bslem}
with \(v:=\mathcal h_u^{(m_{i+1}+2)/2}\).
Since $\vartheta(m_{i+1}+2)=m_i$ by \eqref{4.1}
and $r/8\le\varrho_i\le r\le1$, we obtain
$$
\inf_{\substack{
\eta\in C^1_0(B_i)\\ 
\mathds{1}_{B_{i+1}}\le\eta\le\mathds{1}_{B_i}}}
\lVert \mathcal h_u^{m_{i+1}/2+1}D\eta \rVert_{L^2(B_i)}^2
\le \frac{c}{(\varrho_i-\varrho_{i+1})^{(1+\vartheta)/\vartheta}r^{\kappa}}
\lVert \mathcal h_u^{m_i/2} \rVert_{W^{1,2}(B_i)}^{2/\vartheta},
$$
where we are denoting 
$
\kappa:=(n-1)(1-\vartheta)/\vartheta
$. 
Applying \eqref{cacc.c} with \(m=m_{i+1}\) and taking
the infimum over the admissible cutoff functions yields
\eqn{caccr.2}
$$
\lVert \mathcal h_u^{m_{i+1}/2} \rVert_{W^{1,2}(B_{i+1})}
\le
\frac{c(n,m_{i+1})}
{(\varrho_i-\varrho_{i+1})^{(1+\vartheta)/(2\vartheta)}r^{\kappa/2}}
\lVert \mathcal h_u^{m_i/2} \rVert_{W^{1,2}(B_i)}^{1/\vartheta}.
$$
Set
$$
\textnormal{\texttt{H}}_i:=
\lVert \mathcal h_u^{m_i/2} \rVert_{W^{1,2}(B_i)}^{2/m_i}.
$$
Then \eqref{caccr.2} gives
$$
\textnormal{\texttt{H}}_{i+1}
\le
\frac{c}
{(\varrho_i-\varrho_{i+1})^{(1+\vartheta)/
(\vartheta m_{i+1})}r^{\kappa/m_{i+1}}}
\textnormal{\texttt{H}}_i^{m_i/(\vartheta m_{i+1})}.
$$
Iterating this inequality, we obtain, for \(1\le i\le i_p\),
\eqn{3}
$$
\textnormal{\texttt{H}}_i
\le
\frac{c\,r^{-\alpha_i}}
{(\tau_2-\tau_1)^{\widetilde\beta_i}}
\textnormal{\texttt{H}}_0^{m_0/(\vartheta^i m_i)},
\qquad
\alpha_i:=
\frac{(n-1)(\vartheta^{-i}-1)}{m_i},
\qquad
\widetilde\beta_i:=
\frac{1+\vartheta}{m_i}
\sum_{j=1}^{i}\frac 1{\vartheta^{j}},
$$
where \(c=c(n,i)\). We now choose
$
\tau_1=r/8,
$ and $
\tau_2=3r/4.
$
By \eqref{ccc},
$$
\lVert \mathcal h_u^{m_0/2} \rVert_{W^{1,2}(B_{3r/4})}\le
cr^{-1}\lVert \mathcal h_u^{m_0/2+1} \rVert_{L^2(B_{5r/6})}.
$$
Since \(m_0+2=n\), \eqref{3} gives
$$
\lVert \mathcal h_u^{m_i/2} \rVert_{W^{1,2}(B_{r/8})}
\le
Cr^{-\gamma_i}
\lVert \mathcal h_u \rVert_{L^n(B_{5r/6})}^{\frac{n}{2\vartheta^i}},
$$
for some \(\gamma_i=\gamma_i(n,i)>0\). By \eqref{4}, the index $i_p$ is finite and depends
only on $n$ and $p$.
We apply the last estimate with $i=i_p$. Since \(\mathfrak{m}_{p}\ge p\) and
\(\mathcal h_u\ge1\), we have
$$
\lvert D\mathcal h_u^{p/2}\rvert
=
\frac{p}{\mathfrak{m}_{p}}
\mathcal h_u^{(p-\mathfrak{m}_{p})/2}
\lvert D\mathcal h_u^{\mathfrak{m}_{p}/2}\rvert
\le \frac{p}{\mathfrak{m}_{p}}
\lvert D\mathcal h_u^{\mathfrak{m}_{p}/2} 
\rvert.
$$
Moreover, by H\"older's inequality,
$$
\begin{aligned}
\lVert \mathcal h_u^{p/2} \rVert_{L^2(B_{r/8})}
&\le
|B_{r/8}|^{\frac{\mathfrak{m}_{p}-p}{2\mathfrak{m}_{p}}}
\lVert \mathcal h_u^{\mathfrak{m}_{p}/2} \rVert_{L^2(B_{r/8})}^{p/\mathfrak{m}_{p}}\le
c\lVert \mathcal h_u^{\mathfrak{m}_{p}/2} \rVert_{W^{1,2}(B_{r/8})}+c.
\end{aligned}
$$
Consequently,
\eqn{3.0.1}
$$
\lVert \mathcal h_u^{p/2} \rVert_{W^{1,2}(B_{r/8})}
\le
cr^{-\gamma_p}\left(1+\lVert \mathcal h_u \rVert_{L^n(B_{5r/6})}^{\textnormal{\texttt{b}}_p}\right),
$$
where
$
\textnormal{\texttt{b}}_p:=
n/(2\vartheta^{i_p})$ and $
\gamma_p=\gamma_p(n,p)>0$. Finally, by \eqref{czcz} with \(q=n\), recalling that
\(\mathcal h_\infty(\mu)\ge2\) and \(r\le r_p\le1\), we obtain
$$
\begin{aligned}
\|\mathcal h_u\|_{L^n(B_{5r/6})}
&\le |B_{5r/6}|^{1/n}\mathcal h_\infty(\mu)
+\|(\mathcal h_u-\mathcal h_\infty(\mu))_+\|_{L^n(\mathbb R^n)}
\\
&\le c_n\left[
\mathcal h_\infty(\mu)+\mathfrak m_\mu\|\mu\|_{L^n(\mathbb R^n)}\right].
\end{aligned}
$$
Inserting this bound into \eqref{3.0.1} and absorbing the additive
constant by using \(\mathcal h_\infty(\mu)\ge2\) yields
\eqref{hiiigh}, completing the proof.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\label{abstract.iteration}
Let $B\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a bounded domain, let
$
v:B\to[0,\infty)
$
be a measurable function, and let
$
\kappa_0\ge0.
$
For every $\kappa\ge\kappa_0$, set
$$
E_\kappa:=\{x\in B:v(x)>\kappa\}.
$$
Assume that
\eqn{abstract.zero.boundary}
$$
(v-\kappa)_+
\in
W^{1,2}_0(B)
\qquad
\mbox{for every }\kappa\ge\kappa_0,
$$
and define
$
T(\kappa)
:=
\lVert D(v-\kappa)_+ \rVert_{L^2(B)}
$
and assume that
$
S_0:=\lvert E_{\kappa_0}\rvert>0.
$
Let
$
\omega:[0,S_0]\to[0,\infty)
$
be a nondecreasing, absolutely continuous function such that
$
\omega(0)=0,
$
and assume there exists $\mathfrak{c}_0\ge1$ such that
\eqn{abstract.energy}
$$
T(\kappa)
\le
\mathfrak{c}_0\omega(\lvert E_\kappa\rvert)
\qquad
