\mbox{for every }\kappa\ge\kappa_0.
$$
It follows that
\begin{itemize}
\item
If there exist $\chi>1$ and $\mathfrak{c}_{1}\ge1$ such that
\eqn{abstract.power.level}
$$
\lvert E_{\kappa_2}\rvert
\le
\mathfrak{c}_{1}
\left(
\frac{T(\kappa_1)}
{\kappa_2-\kappa_1}
\right)^\chi
$$
whenever
$
\kappa_0\le\kappa_1<\kappa_2,
$
then
\eqn{abstract.power.bound}
$$
\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{B}v
\le
\kappa_0
+
c(\chi)\mathfrak{c}_0\mathfrak{c}_{1}^{1/\chi}
\int_0^{S_0}
\frac{\omega'(s)}{s^{1/\chi}}
\,{\rm d}s.
$$

\item
If there exist constants
$
\mathfrak{c}_{2}\ge1
$
and
$
\mathfrak{c}_{3}>0
$
such that
\eqn{abstract.exp.level}
$$
\lvert E_{\kappa_2}\rvert
\le
\mathfrak{c}_{2}
\exp\left\{
-\frac{
\mathfrak{c}_{3}(\kappa_2-\kappa_1)^2
}{
T(\kappa_1)^2
}
\right\}
\lvert E_{\kappa_1}\rvert
$$
whenever
$
\kappa_0\le\kappa_1<\kappa_2
$
and
$
T(\kappa_1)>0,
$
then
\eqn{abstract.exp.bound}
$$
\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{B}v
\le
\kappa_0
+
\frac{c\mathfrak{c}_0}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{c}_{3}}}
\int_0^{S_0}
\omega'(s)
\sqrt{\log}\left(
\frac{e\mathfrak{c}_{2}S_0}{s}
\right)
\,{\rm d}s.
$$
\end{itemize}
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
Note that $S_0>0$ implies 
$
T(\kappa_0)>0,
$
since otherwise (\ref{abstract.zero.boundary}) and Poincar\'e's
inequality would give
$
(v-\kappa_0)_+=0
$
a.e. in $B$, contradicting $S_0>0$. Note also that we can always assume that $\omega$ is strictly increasing. Otherwise, for $\varepsilon>0$, we replace it  by 
$
s \mapsto \omega_\varepsilon(s):=\omega(s)+\varepsilon s.
$
Estimate (\ref{abstract.energy}) remains valid with
$
\omega_\varepsilon
$
in place of $\omega$, and moreover (according to the case under consideration)
$$
\varepsilon
\int_0^{S_0}s^{-1/\chi}\,{\rm d}s
\to0, 
\qquad 
\varepsilon
\int_0^{S_0}
\sqrt{\log}\left(
\frac{e\mathfrak{c}_{2}S_0}{s}
\right)
\,{\rm d}s
\to0
\qquad
\mbox{as }\varepsilon\to0
$$
since $\chi>1$. Applying the lemma to $\omega_{\varepsilon}$ and eventually letting $\varepsilon\to0$ in the resulting
estimates, we therefore assume that the inverse $
\omega^{-1}
$ exists. 
\medskip

\noindent
{\em Proof of \eqref{abstract.power.bound}.}
We can assume that the right-hand side of
(\ref{abstract.power.bound}) is finite. Set
$
T_0:=T(\kappa_0)
$
and, for every $i\ge1$, define
\eqn{abstract.ti}
$$
t_i
:=
\frac{T_0}{\mathfrak{c}_0 2^{i}},
\qquad
\textnormal{\texttt{b}}_i
:=
\omega^{-1}(t_i)>0.
$$
Notice that
$t_1=T_0/(2\mathfrak{c}_0)\le\omega(S_0)/2,$
by \eqref{abstract.energy}, and hence each $\textnormal{\texttt{b}}_i$ is well-defined.
Define recursively
\eqn{abstract.di.power}
$$
d_i
:=
\frac{
\mathfrak{c}_{1}^{1/\chi}T(\kappa_{i-1})
}{
\textnormal{\texttt{b}}_i^{1/\chi}
},
\qquad
\kappa_i
:=
\kappa_{i-1}+d_i,
\qquad
i\ge1.
$$
This definition makes sense for every $i\ge1$. Thus, if
$
T(\kappa_j)=0
$
for some $j\ge0$, then
$
\kappa_i=\kappa_j
$
for every $i\ge j$, while
$
d_i=0
$
for every $i\ge j+1$. We claim that
\eqn{abstract.decay.power}
$$
T(\kappa_i)
\le
\frac{T_0}{2^{i}}
\qquad
\mbox{for every }i\ge0.
$$
The assertion is trivial for $i=0$. Fix $i\ge1$. If
$
T(\kappa_{i-1})=0,
$
then
$
d_i=0,
$
$
\kappa_i=\kappa_{i-1},
$
and therefore
$
T(\kappa_i)=0,
$
so the assertion follows. Suppose instead that
$
T(\kappa_{i-1})>0.
$
Then $d_i>0$ and
$
\kappa_{i-1}<\kappa_i.
$
By \eqref{abstract.power.level} and \eqref{abstract.di.power},
$$
\lvert E_{\kappa_i}\rvert
\le
\mathfrak{c}_{1}
\left[
\frac{T(\kappa_{i-1})}{d_i}
\right]^\chi
=
\textnormal{\texttt{b}}_i.
$$
Therefore, by \eqref{abstract.energy},
\eqn{ray0}
$$
T(\kappa_i)
\le
\mathfrak{c}_0\omega(\lvert E_{\kappa_i}\rvert)
\le
\mathfrak{c}_0\omega(\textnormal{\texttt{b}}_i)
=
\mathfrak{c}_0t_i
=
\frac{T_0}{2^{i}}.
$$
This proves \eqref{abstract.decay.power}. We next estimate the sum of the increments. From
\eqref{abstract.decay.power},
$$
d_i
\le
\mathfrak{c}_{1}^{1/\chi}
\frac{2^{-i+1}T_0}{\textnormal{\texttt{b}}_i^{1/\chi}}
=
2\mathfrak{c}_0\mathfrak{c}_{1}^{1/\chi}
\frac{t_i}{\omega^{-1}(t_i)^{1/\chi}}
$$
for every $i\ge1$. If
$
t\in[t_i/2,t_i],
$
then
$
\omega^{-1}(t)
\le
\omega^{-1}(t_i),
$
and hence
$$
\int_{t_i/2}^{t_i}
\frac{\,{\rm d}t}{\omega^{-1}(t)^{1/\chi}}
\ge
\frac{t_i}{2\omega^{-1}(t_i)^{1/\chi}}.
$$
Since
$
t_i/2=t_{i+1},
$
the intervals
$
(t_i/2,t_i]
$
are pairwise disjoint and cover $(0,t_1]$. Consequently,
\eqn{walmart}
$$
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}d_i
\leq
4\mathfrak{c}_0\mathfrak{c}_{1}^{1/\chi}
\int_{0}^{t_1}
\frac{\,{\rm d}t}{\omega^{-1}(t)^{1/\chi}}
=
4\mathfrak{c}_0\mathfrak{c}_{1}^{1/\chi}
\int_0^{\textnormal{\texttt{b}}_1}
\frac{\omega'(s)}{s^{1/\chi}}
\,{\rm d}s
\leq
4\mathfrak{c}_0\mathfrak{c}_{1}^{1/\chi}
\int_0^{S_0}
\frac{\omega'(s)}{s^{1/\chi}}
\,{\rm d}s.
$$
In the equality above we used the change of variables
$t=\omega(s)$. Set
\eqn{kkkk}
$$
\kappa_\infty
:=\kappa_0+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}d_i
\stackrel{\eqref{walmart}}{\leq}
\kappa_0+
4\mathfrak{c}_0\mathfrak{c}_{1}^{1/\chi}
\int_0^{S_0}
\frac{\omega'(s)}{s^{1/\chi}}
\,{\rm d}s.
$$
Hence $\kappa_\infty<\infty$. Moreover, by
\eqref{abstract.decay.power},
$
T(\kappa_i)\to0.
$
Since
$
(v-\kappa_i)_+\in W^{1,2}_0(B),
$
Poincar\'e's inequality gives
$
\lVert (v-\kappa_i)_+ \rVert_{L^2(B)}
\le
c(B)T(\kappa_i)
\to0.
$
Moreover,
$
\kappa_i\uparrow\kappa_\infty
$
and
$
(v-\kappa_i)_+ \to (v-\kappa_\infty)_+
$
pointwise a.e. in $B$. Hence, by Fatou's lemma,
$$
\lVert (v-\kappa_\infty)_+ \rVert_{L^2(B)}^2
\le
\liminf_{i\to\infty}
\lVert (v-\kappa_i)_+ \rVert_{L^2(B)}^2
=
0.
$$
We conclude that
$
(v-\kappa_\infty)_+=0
$
a.e. in $B$. Estimate \eqref{abstract.power.bound} now follows from
\eqref{kkkk}. 

\medskip

\noindent
{\em Proof of \eqref{abstract.exp.bound}.}
As before, we assume that the right-hand side of (\ref{abstract.exp.bound})
is finite; the proof is a variant of the power case,
and we shall partially use the same notation. 
Again set
$
T_0:=T(\kappa_0)
$
and define $t_i$ and $\textnormal{\texttt{b}}_i$ as in (\ref{abstract.ti}). We use the
convention
$$
[\log r]_+
:=
\begin{cases}
0
&\mbox{if $0\le r\le1$}
\\ \log r&\mbox{if $r>1$}.
\end{cases}
$$
For every $i\ge1$, define recursively
\eqn{abstract.di.exp}
$$
d_i
:=
\frac{T(\kappa_{i-1})}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{c}_{3}}}
\left[
\log\left(
\frac{
\mathfrak{c}_{2}\lvert E_{\kappa_{i-1}}\rvert
}{
\textnormal{\texttt{b}}_{i}
}
\right)
\right]_+^{1/2},
\qquad
\kappa_i
:=
\kappa_{i-1}+d_i.
$$
This is well-defined also when
$
T(\kappa_{i-1})=0,
$
because in that case
$
\lvert E_{\kappa_{i-1}}\rvert=0
$
by (\ref{abstract.zero.boundary}) and Poincar\'e's inequality, and our
convention gives $d_i=0$. We again claim
(\ref{abstract.decay.power}). 
The assertion is trivial for $i=0$. Fix $i\ge1$. If
$
T(\kappa_{i-1})=0,
$
then
$
d_i=0,
$
$
\kappa_i=\kappa_{i-1},
$
and
$
T(\kappa_i)=0.
$
Suppose therefore that
$
T(\kappa_{i-1})>0.
$
If
$
\mathfrak{c}_{2}\lvert E_{\kappa_{i-1}}\rvert
\le
\textnormal{\texttt{b}}_{i},
$
then $d_i=0$, so that $\kappa_i=\kappa_{i-1}$, and
$
\lvert E_{\kappa_i}\rvert
=
\lvert E_{\kappa_{i-1}}\rvert
\le
\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{-1}\textnormal{\texttt{b}}_{i}
\le
\textnormal{\texttt{b}}_{i}.
$
Otherwise, $d_i>0$ and
$
\kappa_{i-1}<\kappa_i.
$
Hence \eqref{abstract.exp.level} and \eqref{abstract.di.exp} give
$$
\lvert E_{\kappa_i}\rvert
\le
\mathfrak{c}_{2}
\exp\left\{-\log\left(\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{2}\lvert E_{\kappa_{i-1}}\rvert}{\textnormal{\texttt{b}}_{i}}\right)\right\}\lvert E_{\kappa_{i-1}}\rvert
=\textnormal{\texttt{b}}_{i}.
$$
Thus, in every case,
$
\lvert E_{\kappa_i}\rvert\le\textnormal{\texttt{b}}_{i}.
$
Using \eqref{abstract.energy}, we obtain (\ref{ray0}), which 
proves \eqref{abstract.decay.power}. Since
$
\lvert E_{\kappa_{i-1}}\rvert\le S_0,
$
estimate \eqref{abstract.decay.power} gives
$$
d_i
\leq 
\frac{2^{-i+1}T_0}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{c}_{3}}}
\sqrt{\log}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{2}S_0}{\omega^{-1}(t_i)}
\right)
=
\frac{2\mathfrak{c}_0}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{c}_{3}}}
t_i
\sqrt{\log}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{2}S_0}{\omega^{-1}(t_i)}\right).
$$
The function
$$
t
\longmapsto
\sqrt{\log}\left(
\frac{
\mathfrak{c}_{2}S_0
}{
\omega^{-1}(t)
}
\right)
$$
is nonincreasing on $(0,t_1]$. Arguing as in the previous case,
we obtain
\begin{flalign*}
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}d_i
&\leq
\frac{4\mathfrak{c}_0}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{c}_{3}}}
\int_0^{t_1}
\sqrt{\log}\left(
\frac{
\mathfrak{c}_{2}S_0
}{
\omega^{-1}(t)
}
\right)
\,{\rm d}t
\\
&=
\frac{4\mathfrak{c}_0}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{c}_{3}}}
\int_0^{\textnormal{\texttt{b}}_1}
\omega'(s)
\sqrt{\log}\left(
\frac{
\mathfrak{c}_{2}S_0
}{
s
}
\right)
\,{\rm d}s\leq \frac{4\mathfrak{c}_0}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{c}_{3}}}
\int_0^{S_0}\omega'(s)
\sqrt{\log}\left(\frac{e\mathfrak{c}_{2}S_0}{s}\right)
\,{\rm d}s.
\end{flalign*}
Upon setting this time
$$
\kappa_\infty
:=
\kappa_0+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}d_i\le
\kappa_0
+
\frac{c\mathfrak{c}_0}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{c}_{3}}}
\int_0^{S_0}
\omega'(s)
\sqrt{\log}\left(
\frac{
e\mathfrak{c}_{2}S_0
}{
s
}
\right)
\,{\rm d}s,
$$
the rest of the proof follows as in the proof of \eqref{abstract.power.bound}.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- proposition-11: body_md -->
\label{boostdopo}
Let
$u\in\mathbb X(\mathbb R^n)\cap C^\infty(\mathbb R^n)$
be the minimizer of \eqref{bi.en} with charge
$\mu\in C^\infty_0(\mathbb R^n)$.
If $n=2$, assume in addition that $\mu\in L^1_0(\mathbb R^2)$. 
Let $\mathcal h_\infty(\mu)$ be as in
Proposition \ref{com.sup}.
\begin{itemize}
\item If $n\ge3$, then
\eqn{boost.bound}
$$
\lVert \mathcal h_u \rVert_{L^\infty(\mathbb R^n)}
\le
\exp\left\{c_n[\mu]_{n,1}\right\}.
$$
\item If $n=2$, then, for every $\alpha>2$,
\eqn{boost.2d}
$$
\lVert \mathcal h_u \rVert_{L^\infty(\mathbb R^2)}
\le\mathcal h_\infty(\mu)\exp\left\{c_\alpha\lVert \mu \rVert_{L^2(\log L)^\alpha(\mathbb R^2)}\right\}.
$$
\end{itemize}
Here $c_n$ depends only on $n$, and $c_\alpha$
only on $\alpha$.
<!-- end proposition-11 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
By Proposition \ref{exex}, $u$ is a classical solution
to \eqref{bi}. Therefore, we can use all the results already proved for these ones. The proof splits in two steps. 

{\em Step 1: Level sets estimate for $\log \mathcal h_u$}. If $n\ge3$, fix an arbitrary $\tau>1$.
If $n=2$, set $\tau:=\mathcal h_\infty(\mu)$.
By \eqref{boost.at.infinity}, there exists a ball
$B$ centered at the origin such that
$
\,{\rm supp }\,(\mathcal h_u-\tau)_+\Subset B.
$
When $n=2$, we take
$B:=B_{\textnormal{\texttt{r}}_\mu}(0)$ as in Proposition \ref{com.sup}.
For $\kappa\ge\tau$, set
$
\textnormal{\texttt{A}}_\kappa:=\{x\in B:\mathcal h_u(x)>\kappa\}.
$
Then
\eqn{supporto}
$$
\,{\rm supp }\,(\mathcal h_u-\kappa)_+\Subset B
\quad
\mbox{for every $\kappa\ge\tau$}.
$$We test \eqref{0} against $\varphi:=(\mathcal{h}_{u}-\kappa)_{+}D_{s}u$ and sum over $s\in \{1,\cdots,n\}$ to get
\begin{flalign*}
0&=\sum_{s=1}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\mathcal{h}_{u}-\kappa)_{+}\langle\partial^{2}H(Du)DD_{s}u,DD_{s}u\rangle\,{\rm d}x\nonumber  +\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\langle\mathcal H(Du)D\mathcal h_u,D(\mathcal{h}_{u}-\kappa)_{+}\rangle\,{\rm d}x\nonumber \\
&\quad +\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mu(\mathcal{h}_{u}-\kappa)_{+}\Delta u\,{\rm d}x+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mu\langle D(\mathcal{h}_{u}-\kappa)_{+}, Du\rangle\,{\rm d}x=:\sum_{i=1}^{4}\mbox{(I)}_{i}.
\end{flalign*}
Here we used (\ref{identitas}) and the definition of $\mathcal H$ in
\eqref{recalla}.
Via \eqref{0.1.1}-\eqref{0.1}  we bound from below as follows
$$
\mbox{(I)}_{1}+\mbox{(I)}_{2}\ge \int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\mathcal{h}_{u}-\kappa)_{+}\mathcal{h}_{u}\lvert D^{2}u\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d}x\nonumber +\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\langle\mathcal{H}(Du)D(\mathcal{h}_{u}-\kappa)_{+},D(\mathcal{h}_{u}-\kappa)_{+}\rangle\,{\rm d}x.
$$
Next, recalling (\ref{supporto}), via Young's inequality we find
\begin{flalign*}
\lvert \mbox{(I)}_3\rvert
&\le
c_n
\int_{\mathbb R^n}
\lvert \mu\rvert\lvert D^2u\rvert
(\mathcal h_u-\kappa)_+
\,{\rm d}x
\\
&\le
\frac14
\int_{\mathbb R^n}
(\mathcal h_u-\kappa)_+
\mathcal h_u\lvert D^2u\rvert^2
\,{\rm d}x
+
c_n
\int_{\mathbb R^n}
\frac{(\mathcal h_u-\kappa)_+}{\mathcal h_u}
\mu^2\,{\rm d}x
\\
&\le
\frac14
\int_{\mathbb R^n}
(\mathcal h_u-\kappa)_+
\mathcal h_u\lvert D^2u\rvert^2
\,{\rm d}x
+
c_n
\int_{\textnormal{\texttt{A}}_\kappa}\mu^2\,{\rm d}x.
\end{flalign*}
Similarly, this time by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality relative to $\mathcal H(Du)$ and using 
$
\mathcal H(Du)Du=Du
$, we obtain
\begin{flalign*}
\lvert \mbox{(I)}_{4}\rvert&=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mu\langle \mathcal H(Du) D(\mathcal{h}_{u}-\kappa)_{+}, Du\rangle\,{\rm d}x\right|
\\
&\le
\frac14
\int_{\mathbb R^n}
\left\langle
\mathcal H(Du)D(\mathcal h_u-\kappa)_+,
D(\mathcal h_u-\kappa)_+
\right\rangle\,{\rm d}x+
4\int_{\textnormal{\texttt{A}}_\kappa}\mu^2\lvert Du\rvert^2\,{\rm d}x
\\
&\le
\frac14\int_{\mathbb R^n}\left\langle
\mathcal H(Du)D(\mathcal h_u-\kappa)_+,
D(\mathcal h_u-\kappa)_+
\right\rangle\,{\rm d}x+4\int_{\textnormal{\texttt{A}}_\kappa}\mu^2\,{\rm d}x.
\end{flalign*}
Merging the content of all previous displays and reabsorbing terms, we obtain
\eqn{matrix}
$$
\int_{\textnormal{\texttt{A}}_\kappa}\left\langle\mathcal H(Du)D\mathcal h_u,D\mathcal h_u\right\rangle\,{\rm d}x=\int_B
\left\langle
\mathcal H(Du)D(\mathcal h_u-\kappa)_+,
D(\mathcal h_u-\kappa)_+\right\rangle\,{\rm d}x
\le c_{n} \int_{\textnormal{\texttt{A}}_\kappa}\mu^2\,{\rm d}x. 
$$
Recalling that
$
\left\langle
\mathcal H(Du)\xi,\xi
\right\rangle
\ge
\mathcal h_u^{-2}\lvert \xi\rvert^2,
$
\eqref{matrix} implies
\begin{flalign}
\nonumber  \lVert 
D(\log\mathcal h_u-\log\kappa)_+
 \rVert_{L^2(B)}^2
 & =
\int_{\textnormal{\texttt{A}}_\kappa}
\mathcal h_u^{-2}\lvert D\mathcal h_u\rvert^2\,{\rm d}x\\
& \le
\int_{\textnormal{\texttt{A}}_\kappa}
\left\langle
\mathcal H(Du)D\mathcal h_u,
D\mathcal h_u
\right\rangle\,{\rm d}x
\le
c
\int_{\textnormal{\texttt{A}}_\kappa}\mu^2\,{\rm d}x \label{whit}. 
\end{flalign}
Define
\eqn{ray10}
$$
\omega_\mu(s):=
\left(
\int_0^s
\left(
(\lvert \mu\rvert\mathds1_B)^*(r)
\right)^2
\,{\rm d}r
\right)^{1/2} , \qquad  0 \leq s \leq |B|. 
$$
By the classical Hardy--Littlewood rearrangement inequality,
$$
\int_{\textnormal{\texttt{A}}_\kappa}\mu^2\,{\rm d}x
=
\int_{\textnormal{\texttt{A}}_\kappa}
(\lvert \mu\rvert\mathds1_B)^2\,{\rm d}x
\le
\int_0^{\lvert \textnormal{\texttt{A}}_\kappa\rvert}\left(
(\lvert \mu\rvert\mathds1_B)^*(r)
\right)^2\,{\rm d}r
=
\omega_\mu(\lvert \textnormal{\texttt{A}}_\kappa\rvert)^2,
$$
so that (\ref{whit}) finally becomes
\eqn{og.cacc}
$$
\lVert D(\log\mathcal h_u-\log\kappa)_+ \rVert_{L^2(B)}
\le c_{n}\omega_\mu(\lvert \textnormal{\texttt{A}}_\kappa\rvert).
$$
The constant $c_{n}$ appearing in the display is independent of $\tau$, $\kappa\geq \tau$, and $B$.
In particular, the preceding estimates use only
$0\le(\mathcal h_u-\kappa)_+/\mathcal h_u\le1$
and are valid independently of any lower bound on $\tau-1$.
Note that the function $\omega_\mu$ is nondecreasing and absolutely continuous
on $[0,\lvert B\rvert]$, and
$
\omega_\mu(0)=0.
$
Before approaching the next step we record an estimate for $\omega_\mu'$. Since $s \mapsto (\lvert \mu\rvert\mathds 1_B)^*(s)$ is
nonincreasing, we have  
$
\omega_\mu(s)^2
\ge
s(\lvert \mu\rvert\mathds 1_B)^*(s)^2.
$
It follows that, for a.e. $s\in(0,|B|)$ such that
$
\omega_\mu(s)>0,
$
\eqn{boost.omega.derivative}
$$
\omega_\mu'(s)
=
\frac{(\lvert \mu\rvert\mathds 1_B)^*(s)^2}{2\omega_\mu(s)}
\leq 
\frac{(\lvert \mu\rvert\mathds 1_B)^*(s)}{2\sqrt{s}}.
$$
\indent
{\em Step 2: Iterations according to Lemma \ref{abstract.iteration}.}
The occurrence of (\ref{og.cacc}) leads us to define the function $v$
$$
v:=\left(
\log\frac{\mathcal h_u}{\tau}
\right)_+
\Longrightarrow
(v-\kappa)_+
=
\left(
\log\frac{\mathcal h_u}{\tau e^\kappa}
\right)_+,
\quad \kappa\ge0,
$$
to which we apply Lemma \ref{abstract.iteration} with initial level
$
\kappa_0:=0.
$
Note that $v$ is non-negative, continuous and belongs to
$W^{1,2}_0(B)$.
For every $\kappa\ge0$, set
$$
E_\kappa
:=
\{x\in B:v(x)>\kappa\}
=
\left\{
x\in B:
\mathcal h_u(x)>\tau e^\kappa
\right\}.
$$
In particular,
$
\tau e^\kappa\ge\tau
$
and
$
(v-\kappa)_+\in W^{1,2}_0(B).
$
Following the notation of Lemma \ref{abstract.iteration}, define
$
T(\kappa)
:=
\lVert D(v-\kappa)_+ \rVert_{L^2(B)}
$
and set
\eqn{ss00}
$$
S_0:=\lvert E_{\kappa_0}\rvert
=\lvert E_0\rvert
=
\lvert \left\{x\in B:\mathcal h_u(x)>\tau\right\}\rvert.
$$
If $S_0=0$, then $v=0$ in $B$ and the estimates for $v$
obtained below are immediate.
To derive these estimates, we may therefore assume that
$
S_0>0.
$
Moreover,
$
E_\kappa\subset E_0
$
for every $\kappa\ge0$, and hence
$
\lvert E_\kappa\rvert\le S_0.
$
Replacing $\kappa$ by $\tau e^\kappa$ in (\ref{og.cacc}), we obtain
$
T(\kappa)
\le
c_n\omega_\mu(\lvert E_\kappa\rvert)
$
for every $\kappa\ge0$.
Thus condition \eqref{abstract.energy} holds with
$\kappa_0=0$, $\mathfrak c_0=c_n$, and
$\omega=\omega_\mu$ on $[0,S_0]$,
where $\omega_\mu$ is defined in \eqref{ray10}.

\medskip

\medskip

\noindent
{\em Case $n\ge3$.} We verify (\ref{abstract.power.level}). 
Take the usual Sobolev embedding exponent 
$
2^*
:=
2n/(n-2)
$
and fix
$
0\le\kappa_1<\kappa_2.
$
We have 
$
(v-\kappa_1)_+\in W^{1,2}_0(B),
$
while
$
(v-\kappa_1)_+
\ge
\kappa_2-\kappa_1$
a.e. on $E_{\kappa_2}$. This last fact, Chebyshev and Sobolev inequalities give 
$$
\lvert E_{\kappa_2}\rvert
\leq \frac{\lVert (v-\kappa_1)_+ \rVert_{L^{2^*}(B)}^{2^*}}{(\kappa_2-\kappa_1)^{2^*}}
\leq 
c_{n}
\frac{
\lVert D(v-\kappa_1)_+ \rVert_{L^2(B)}^{2^*}}{(\kappa_2-\kappa_1)^{2^*}}
=
c_{n}
\frac{T(\kappa_1)^{2^*}}{(\kappa_2-\kappa_1)^{2^*}}.
$$
Thus \eqref{abstract.power.level} holds with
$
\chi=2^*$ and 
$\mathfrak c_1=c_{n}\ge1.
$
All the assumptions in the first part of Lemma
\ref{abstract.iteration} are therefore satisfied. Since the initial
level for $v$ is
$
\kappa_0=0,
$
estimate \eqref{abstract.power.bound} gives
\eqn{boost.abstract.high}
$$
\lVert v \rVert_{L^\infty(B)}
\le
c_{n}
\int_0^{S_0}
\frac{
\omega_\mu'(s)
}{
s^{1/2^*}
}
\,{\rm d}s.
$$
Using \eqref{boost.omega.derivative} and the identity
$
1/2+1/2^*=1-1/n$
we find
$$
\int_0^{S_0}
\frac{
\omega_\mu'(s)
}{
s^{1/2^*}
}
\,{\rm d}s
\leq 
\frac12
\int_0^{S_0}
s^{1/n}(\lvert \mu\rvert\mathds 1_B)^*(s)\frac{\,{\rm d}s}{s}
\leq 
c[\mu]_{n,1;B}
\leq 
c[\mu]_{n,1}.
$$
Combining this estimate with \eqref{boost.abstract.high}, we obtain
$$
\lVert v \rVert_{L^\infty(B)}
\le
c_{n}[\mu]_{n,1}.
$$
The constant $c_n$ in the above display is independent of $\tau$ and $B$. 
By the definition of $v$, we obtain
$
\mathcal h_u
\le
\tau\exp\left\{c_n[\mu]_{n,1}\right\}
$
in $B$.
Moreover, the choice of $B$ gives
$
\mathcal h_u\le\tau
$
in $\mathbb R^n\setminus B$.
Therefore,
$$
\lVert \mathcal h_u \rVert_{L^\infty(\mathbb R^n)}
\le
\tau\exp\left\{c_n[\mu]_{n,1}\right\}.
$$
Since $\tau>1$ is arbitrary, letting $\tau\downarrow1$
proves (\ref{boost.bound}).

\medskip

\noindent
{\em Case $n=2$.} Again, we have to check (\ref{abstract.exp.level}). 
Fix
$
0\le\kappa_1<\kappa_2
$
such that 
$
T(\kappa_1)>0.
$
Since $\mathcal h_u$ is smooth, $v$ is continuous and
$
E_{\kappa_1}
$
is open, so that 
$
(v-\kappa_1)_+
\in
W^{1,2}_0(E_{\kappa_1}).
$
Lemma \ref{trudinger.lemma} yields absolute constants
$
\mathfrak c_2\ge1
$
and
$
\mathfrak c_3>0
$
such that
$$
\int_{E_{\kappa_1}}
\exp\left\{
\frac{\mathfrak c_3(v-\kappa_1)_+^2}{T(\kappa_1)^2}
\right\}\,{\rm d}x
\le
\mathfrak c_2
\lvert E_{\kappa_1}\rvert.
$$
On $E_{\kappa_2}$ we have
$
(v-\kappa_1)_+
\ge
\kappa_2-\kappa_1.
$
It follows that
\begin{flalign*}
\lvert E_{\kappa_2}\rvert
\exp\left\{
\frac{
\mathfrak c_3(\kappa_2-\kappa_1)^2
}{
T(\kappa_1)^2
}
\right\}
&\le
\int_{E_{\kappa_1}}
\exp\left\{
\frac{
\mathfrak c_3(v-\kappa_1)_+^2
}{
T(\kappa_1)^2
}
\right\}
\,{\rm d}x
\leq 
\mathfrak c_2
\lvert E_{\kappa_1}\rvert.
\end{flalign*}
Therefore we obtain the following inequality, which shows that \eqref{abstract.exp.level} is also satisfied
$$
\lvert E_{\kappa_2}\rvert
\le\mathfrak c_2\exp\left\{-\frac{
\mathfrak c_3(\kappa_2-\kappa_1)^2}{T(\kappa_1)^2}\right\}
\lvert E_{\kappa_1}\rvert.
$$
All the assumptions in
the second part of Lemma \ref{abstract.iteration} hold with
$
\kappa_0=0,
$
and \eqref{abstract.exp.bound} gives
\eqn{boost.abstract.two}
$$
\lVert v \rVert_{L^\infty(B)}
\le
c
\int_0^{S_0}
\omega_\mu'(s)
\sqrt{\log}\left(
\frac{e\mathfrak c_2S_0
}{s}\right)
\,{\rm d}s.
$$ 
We now estimate the right-hand side of the above inequality as follows (recall $S_0$ in \eqref{ss00})
\begin{flalign*}
&\int_0^{S_0}
\omega_\mu'(s)
\sqrt{\log}\left(
\frac{e\mathfrak c_2S_0}{s}
\right)
\,{\rm d}s
\\
&\qquad\stackrel{(\ref{boost.omega.derivative})}{ \leq}
c
\int_0^{S_0}
(\lvert \mu\rvert\mathds 1_B)^*(s)
\sqrt{\log}\left(
e+
\frac{e\mathfrak c_2S_0}{s}
\right)
\frac{\,{\rm d}s}{\sqrt{s}}
\\
&\qquad\stackrel{(\ref{stimamis})}{ \leq}
c \int_0^{\mathfrak{s}_n}
(\lvert \mu\rvert\mathds 1_B)^*(s)
\sqrt{\log}\left(e+\frac1s\right)
\frac{\,{\rm d}s}{\sqrt{s}}
\\
&\qquad\ \ \le
c
\left(
\int_0^{\mathfrak{s}_n}
(\lvert \mu\rvert\mathds 1_B)^*(s)^2
\log^\alpha\left(
e+\frac1s
\right)\,{\rm d}s\right)^{1/2}
\left(\int_0^{\mathfrak{s}_n} \log^{1-\alpha}\left(e+\frac1s \right)\frac{\,{\rm d}s}{s}\right)^{1/2}
\\
&\qquad \stackrel{(\ref{orlicz.rearr})}{ \leq}
c
\lVert \mu \rVert_{L^2(\log L)^\alpha(\mathbb R^n)}
\left( \int_0^{\mathfrak{s}_n}
\log^{1-\alpha}\left( e+\frac1s\right) \frac{\,{\rm d}s}{s} \right)^{1/2}
 \le
c \lVert \mu \rVert_{L^2(\log L)^\alpha(\mathbb R^n)},
\end{flalign*}
where $c\equiv c(n,\alpha)$. We used the convention that
$
(\lvert \mu\rvert\mathds1_B)^*
$
is extended by zero outside $[0,\lvert B\rvert]$. Moreover,
 note that the last integral appearing in the latest display is finite precisely when $\alpha >2$. 
Combining this last estimate with (\ref{boost.abstract.two}), we obtain
$
\lVert v \rVert_{L^\infty(B)}\le c(n,\alpha)\lVert \mu \rVert_{L^2(\log L)^\alpha}
$
and, since this time $\tau=\mathcal h_\infty(\mu)$,
the definition of $v$ and the bound $\mathcal h_u\le\tau$
outside $B$ yield \eqref{boost.2d}.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- remark-16: body_md -->
\label{controre}
\emph{ When $n=2$ the factor in \eqref{boost.2d} cannot be dropped. For this, we largely use the material in Proposition \ref{connessione}.
Indeed, let $\eta_T$ be the cut-off functions
defined in \eqref{logatau}.
For $a\in(0,1)$ and $T$ sufficiently large, set 
$
u_{a,T}(x):=a x_1\eta_T(x)$ and $
\mu_{a,T}:=
-\,{\rm div}(\mathcal h_{u_{a,T}}Du_{a,T}).
$ 
Direct computations give 
$
\lVert Du_{a,T} \rVert_{L^\infty(\mathbb R^2)}
\le
a\left(1+C/\log T\right)
$, so that, choosing $T\equiv T(a)>1$ large enough, we have 
$
\lVert Du_{a,T} \rVert_{L^\infty(\mathbb R^2)}< 1$, i.e., 
$u_{a,T}$ is strictly spacelike and belongs to $\mathbb X(\mathbb R^2)$.
The function $\mu_{a,T}$ is smooth and compactly supported. Moreover, $\mu_{a,T} \in L^1_0(\mathbb R^2)$
by Proposition \ref{connessione} and 
$u_{a,T}$ is the associated global minimizer, again by Proposition \ref{connessione}.
Using computations similar to those in Proposition \ref{connessione}, and the definition of 
Luxemburg norm given in (\ref{luxi}), we find that for every fixed $\alpha>0$,
\eqn{contra}
$$
\lVert \mu_{a,T} \rVert_{L^2(\log L)^\alpha(\mathbb R^2)}
\lesssim_{a, \alpha}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\log T}}.
$$
On the other hand, $\eta_T=1$ near the origin, so
\eqn{contra2}
$$
\mathcal h_{u_{a,T}}(0)=\frac1{\sqrt{1-a^2}}.
$$
Note that this identity is independent of $T$. 
Now assume that 
$\lVert \mathcal h_u \rVert_{L^\infty(\mathbb R^2)}
\le \exp\{c_\alpha\lVert \mu \rVert_{L^2(\log L)^\alpha(\mathbb R^2)}\}$ holds for some constant $c_{\alpha}$ and for all admissible $\mu$. 
For every integer $m\geq 2$, by (\ref{contra})-(\ref{contra2}), we can find $a<1$ and, consequently, $T\equiv T(a)>1$, such that $
\lVert Du_{a,T} \rVert_{L^\infty(\mathbb R^2)}< 1$ and  $$\mathcal h_{u_{a,T}}(0) > m > 1+1/m > \exp\left\{c_\alpha\lVert \mu_{a,T} \rVert_{L^2(\log L)^\alpha(\mathbb R^2)}\right\},$$ which is a contradiction. 
}
<!-- end remark-16 -->
<!-- remark-17: body_md -->
\label{prefactor}
\emph{The factor $\mathcal h_{\partial\Omega}$ in
\eqref{boost.bound.dir} cannot be dropped.
To see this, we go back to Remark
\ref{controre}. 
For $n\ge3$, define $\eta_T$ on $\mathbb R^n$
by the same formula as in \eqref{logatau}, and set
$
u_{a,T}(x):=a x_1\eta_T(x),
$
where $a\in(0,1)$ and $T$ is sufficiently large. On the fixed domain $\Omega=B_1(0)$, we have
$
u_{a,T}(x)=a x_1.
$
Therefore, the corresponding charge vanishes in $\Omega$, and $u_{a,T}|_\Omega$ is the Dirichlet minimizer with
boundary datum $a x_1$, by convexity of $H$. All the assumptions of Theorem \ref{dir.borderline}
are satisfied with $\textnormal{\texttt{u}}_0(x)=a x_1$,
$\sigma_0=1-a$, and $f=\mu_*=0$.
However,
$$
\lVert \mathcal h_{u_{a,T}} \rVert_{L^\infty(\Omega)}=\frac1{\sqrt{1-a^2}}>1
=
\exp\left\{c_n[\mu]_{n,1;\Omega}\right\}.
$$}
<!-- end remark-17 -->
<!-- remark-18: body_md -->
\label{standre}{\em
The one above is a typical example of replication of singularities. Once identified the basic, one point phenomenon, one can replicate it using standard tools from Real Analysis, distributing the singularities on suitable bad sets and their accumulation points. See for instance \cite[Section~6]{jms89},
\cite[Section~3]{fmm04}, and \cite{gwz17} for various types of constructions, also involving fractals. In this situation the extension is rather straightforward as the initial construction is already based on the replication and the scaling of the same profile on balls with disjoint supports.  
}
<!-- end remark-18 -->
<!-- proposition-12: body_md -->
\label{exex}
Let $\mu\in \mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)^*\cap L^\infty_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n)$ and, if $n=2$, assume in addition that 
\eqn{addition}
$$
\int_{\mathbb R^2}\mu\,{\rm d}x=0.
$$
There exists a unique minimizer $u\in\mathbb X(\mathbb R^n)$ of the functional
$\mathcal E_\mu$ such that
\eqn{l1l1}
$$
\int_{\mathbb R^n}
\frac{|Du|^2}{\sqrt{1-|Du|^2}}\,{\rm d}x
\le
\langle\mu,u\rangle.
$$
Moreover,
$
u\in C^1(\mathbb R^n)\cap W^{2,2}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n)
$
and $u$ is a strictly spacelike strong solution to
\eqref{bi}. In particular,
\eqn{mm.9}
$$
\int_{\mathbb R^n}
\langle\partial H(Du),Dw\rangle\,{\rm d}x
=
\int_{\mathbb R^n}\mu w\,{\rm d}x
$$
for every compactly supported
$w\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb R^n)$.
If $\mu\neq0$ in $\mathcal D^{1,2}(\mathbb R^n)^*$,
then $u\not\equiv0$.
If, in addition, $\mu\in C^k(\mathbb R^n)$
for an integer $k\ge1$, then
$u\in C^{k+1}(\mathbb R^n)$ and \eqref{bi}
holds classically.
<!-- end proposition-12 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
Using (\ref{elema}), for every \(w\in\mathbb X(\mathbb R^n)\),
\begin{align}
\mathcal E_\mu(w)
&=
\int_{\mathbb R^n}H(Dw)\,{\rm d}x-\langle\mu,w\rangle
\ge
\frac12\|Dw\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^n)}^2
-
\|\mu\|_{\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)^*}\|w\|_{\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)}
\nonumber\\
&\ge
\frac14\|w\|_{\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)}^2
-
\|\mu\|_{\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)^*}^2.
\label{mm.1}
\end{align}
Since \(0\in\mathbb X(\mathbb R^n)\) and
\(\mathcal E_\mu(0)=0\), it follows that
$
-\infty
<
m_\mu
:=
\inf_{w\in\mathbb X(\mathbb R^n)}
\mathcal E_\mu(w)
\le0.
$
Let
$
\{u_j\}_{j\in\mathbb N}
\subset\mathbb X(\mathbb R^n)
$
be a minimizing sequence so that \eqref{mm.1} shows that
\(\{u_j\}\) is bounded in \(\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)\).
Therefore, after passing to a subsequence, there exists
\(u\in\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)\) such that
$
u_j\rightharpoonup u$
weakly in $\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n).$
The set \(\mathbb X(\mathbb R^n)\) is convex and strongly closed in
\(\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)\), and, consequently, it is also weakly closed so that 
$
u\in\mathbb X(\mathbb R^n).
$
Since \(H\) is convex and lower semicontinuous, the integral functional is weakly lower semicontinuous, while the linear term is weakly continuous. Therefore 
$
\mathcal E_\mu(u)
\le
\liminf_{j\to\infty}\mathcal E_\mu(u_j)
=
m_\mu,
$
and hence \(u\) is a minimizer. As for uniqueness, let $v \in \mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)$ be another minimizer.  Then \((u+v)/2\in\mathbb X(\mathbb R^n)\) and the
strict convexity of \(H\) implies 
$
Du=Dv$ a.e. in $\mathbb R^n$. Since \(w\mapsto Dw\) is injective on \(\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)\), we conclude
that \(u=v\). Notice that, in dimension two, this last conclusion
follows from the condition 
$(u-v)_{B_1(0)}=0. 
$
The validity of \eqref{l1l1} for $n=2$ can be retrieved as in \cite[Proposition 2.7]{bdp16}, and we briefly report the argument for completeness. For every \(t\in[0,1]\), one has
$
tu\in\mathbb X(\mathbb R^n).
$
If 
$
\psi(t):=\mathcal E_\mu(tu)
$, then the minimality of $u$ implies 
$
\psi(1)\le\psi(t)
$
for every $t\in[0,1].$ 
The function \(\psi\) is convex and, for \(t\in(0,1)\),
\[
\psi'(t)
=
\int_{\mathbb R^n}
\frac{t|Du|^2}{\sqrt{1-t^2|Du|^2}}\,{\rm d}x
-
\langle\mu,u\rangle.
\]
By convexity,
\[
\psi'(t)
\le
\frac{\psi(1)-\psi(t)}{1-t}
\le0 \Longrightarrow 
\int_{\mathbb R^n}
\frac{t|Du|^2}{\sqrt{1-t^2|Du|^2}}\,{\rm d}x
\le
\langle\mu,u\rangle.
\]
Letting \(t\uparrow1\) and using the monotone convergence theorem, we
obtain (\ref{l1l1}). 
Fix $R>1$ and set $B_R:=B_R(0)$.
By Remark \ref{globale.locale}, $u$ minimizes the
corresponding Dirichlet functional in $B_R$, namely
$
\mathcal E_\mu(u;B_R)\le\mathcal E_\mu(w;B_R)
$
 for every $w\in\mathfrak X(u;B_R)$.
Denote 
$$
\mathcal S_{u,\partial}(B_R)
:=
\bigcup
\left\{\overline{xy}:
x,y\in\partial B_R,\quad
x\neq y,\quad
|u(x)-u(y)|=|x-y|
\right\}.
$$
It is then easy to see that \(\mathcal S_{u,\partial}(B_R)\) is relatively closed in \(B_R\). Since \(u|_{\partial B_R}\) is bounded and admits a weakly spacelike
extension to \(B_R\), namely \(u\) itself, we can apply
\cite[Corollary 4.2]{bs82}. It follows that \(u\) is strictly
spacelike and solves \eqref{bi} in
$
B_R\setminus\mathcal S_{u,\partial}(B_R).
$ 
Actually, we do have that 
\eqn{vuotino}
$$
\mathcal S_{u,\partial}(B_R)=\varnothing
\qquad
\text{for every }R>1.
$$
Assume by contradiction that this is not the case. Then there exist
\(x,y\in\partial B_R\), \(x\neq y\), such that
$
|u(x)-u(y)|=|x-y|
$
(as usual we may assume that
\(u(x)>u(y)\)). Since \(u\) is \(1\)-Lipschitz we have
\eqn{mm.4}
$$
u(y+t(x-y))
=
u(y)+t|x-y|
\qquad
\text{for every }t\in[0,1].
$$
Now fix \(R_{1}>R\). Since \(u\) minimizes the corresponding
Dirichlet problem in \(B_{R_{1}}\), and the segment
\(\overline{xy}\Subset B_{R_{1}}\) satisfies \eqref{mm.4},
Theorem \ref{al.t} implies that
$
u(y+t(x-y))
=
u(y)+t|x-y|
$
for every \(t\in\mathbb R\) such that
$
y+t(x-y)\in B_{R_{1}}.
$ Here we recall that we are assuming that $\mu$ is locally bounded. 
Since \(R_1>R\) is arbitrary, we conclude that
$
u(y+t(x-y))
=
u(y)+t|x-y|$
for every $t\ge0. 
$
This means that $u$ has linear growth at infinity which obviously contradicts the fact that $u(x)\to 0$ at infinity when $n\geq 3$ and \eqref{weightsmall} when $n=2$. This establishes (\ref{vuotino}) and therefore we infer that \(u\) is strictly spacelike in
\(\mathbb R^n\). By \cite[Corollary 4.2]{bs82} we have that 
$
u\in
C^1_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n)
\cap
W^{2,2}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n)
$
and \(u\) solves \eqref{bi} in every ball. Consequently,
(\ref{mm.9})
holds for every smooth $w$ with compact support. 
We finally extend the validity of (\ref{mm.9}) to compactly supported
\(W^{1,\infty}\)-functions $w$. Choose \(R_w>1\) such that
$
\,{\rm supp }\,   w \Subset B_{R_w}.
$
By standard mollification, there exists
\(\{\varphi_j\}\subset C_{0}^\infty(\mathbb R^n)\) such that
$
\,{\rm supp }\,   \varphi_j\Subset B_{2R_w}$ and 
$\varphi_j\to w$ uniformly, 
and
$
D\varphi_j\rightharpoonup^* Dw$ 
in $
L^\infty(B_{2R_w};\mathbb R^n)$. Obviously 
\eqn{mm.99}
$$
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\langle\partial H(Du),D\varphi_j\rangle\,{\rm d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mu \varphi_j\,{\rm d}x\,.
$$
Since \(u\in C^1_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n)\) is strictly spacelike,
compactness gives
$
\|Du\|_{L^\infty(B_{2R_w})}\le\theta_w<1.
$
Hence
$\partial H(Du)=Du/\sqrt{1-|Du|^2}\in L^\infty(B_{2R_w};\mathbb R^n).$
We can therefore pass to the limit in \eqref{mm.99} and obtain
(\ref{mm.9}). If $u\equiv0$, then \eqref{mm.9} yields
$\int_{\mathbb R^n}\mu\varphi\,dx=0$
for every $\varphi\in C^\infty_0(\mathbb R^n)$.
By Remark \ref{concreto} and density, this implies
$\mu=0$ in $\mathcal D^{1,2}_{\#}(\mathbb R^n)^*$, proving the nontriviality assertion. Moreover, since $
u\in W^{2,2}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n)
$
and \(u\) is strictly spacelike, we conclude that 
$
\partial H(Du)\in W^{1,2}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb R^n;\mathbb R^n),
$
so \eqref{bi} holds almost everywhere. Thus \(u\) is a so-called strong
solution. Finally, if \(\mu\in C^k(\mathbb R^n)\), the equation is uniformly
elliptic on every compact subset of \(\mathbb R^n\). Standard
elliptic bootstrap therefore gives
$
u\in C^{k+1}(\mathbb R^n);
$
see also \cite[Remark, pag.~147]{bs82}. Hence, if $k\ge1$, $u$ is a classical solution
of \eqref{bi}.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- proposition-13: body_md -->
\label{cz.p}
Let $n\ge2$, let $\mu\in C^\infty_0(\mathbb R^n)$
satisfy \eqref{addition} when $n=2$, and let
$u\in\mathbb X(\mathbb R^n)$ be the minimizer
of $\mathcal E_\mu$.
Assume that there exists $\mathcal h_\infty>1$
such that $\,{\rm supp }\, (\mathcal h_u-\mathcal h_\infty)_+$
is compact. If $n\ge3$, then, for every $2\le q<\infty$,
\eqn{czcz}
$$
\int_{\mathbb R^n}
(\mathcal h_u-\mathcal h_\infty)_+^q\,{\rm d}x
\le
c\,\mathbb H(\mu,\mathcal h_\infty,q)
\int_{\mathbb R^n}|\mu|^q\,{\rm d}x,
$$
where $c=c(n,q)$.
If $n=2$, then
\eqn{mm.20}
$$
\int_{\mathbb R^2}
(\mathcal h_u-\mathcal h_\infty)_+^2\,{\rm d}x
\le
c\,\mathbb H(\mu,\mathcal h_\infty,2)
\int_{\mathbb R^2}
\mu^2\log^2(e+|x|)\,{\rm d}x,
$$
where $c>0$ is a dimensional constant.
The quantity $\mathbb H(\cdot)$ is defined in \eqref{haccone}. Finally, $u$ is smooth, strictly
spacelike, and solves \eqref{bi} classically. In fact, the same estimates hold for any smooth, strictly
spacelike classical solution $u\in\mathbb X(\mathbb R^n)$
to \eqref{bi}, with the same assumptions on $\mu$
and on the support of
$(\mathcal h_u-\mathcal h_\infty)_+$.
<!-- end proposition-13 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
The proof closely follows those in \cite[Theorem 3.5]{bs82},
\cite[Theorem 3.6]{haa20} and \cite[Proposition 4.2]{bi23},
where such statements are available, in a slightly different
form, in the case $n\geq3$. Here we report the necessary
modifications and the proof for the case $n=2$. By Proposition \ref{exex}, $u$ is smooth, strictly
spacelike, and solves \eqref{bi} classically. Conversely, for a smooth strictly spacelike solution
satisfying the stated hypotheses, the support condition
gives $\mathcal h_u\in L^\infty(\mathbb R^n)$, hence
$\partial H(Du)\in L^2(\mathbb R^n;\mathbb R^n)$. 
By density and convexity, such a solution minimizes
$\mathcal E_\mu$ as well. The proof is based on the structural fact that the boost
function $\mathcal h_u$ satisfies an inhomogeneous subsolution
inequality for a linear elliptic operator with coefficient
matrix $\mathcal H(Du)$ - see (\ref{cz.aux}) below.
When $\mu\equiv0$, this reduces to a genuine subsolution
property. Throughout the proof, we set
$
\eta:=(\mathcal h_u-\mathcal{h}_{\infty})_+
$
and
$
\delta:=1-\mathcal{h}_{\infty}^{-2}\in(0,1).
$
When $q=2$, every occurrence of $\eta^{q-2}$
is understood as $\mathds{1}_{\{\eta>0\}}$, consistently
with $D\eta=\mathds{1}_{\{\eta>0\}}D\mathcal h_u$
almost everywhere.
We use the notation in Section \ref{ner}. Testing (\ref{0}) with $ \varphi D_su$ instead of $\varphi$, where
$\varphi\ge0$ is compactly supported, summing over
$s\in\{1,\ldots,n\}$, recalling (\ref{identitas}),
and dropping the nonnegative term containing
$\partial^2H(Du)D_sDu$, we obtain
\eqn{cz.aux}
$$
\int_{\mathbb R^n}
\langle\mathcal H(Du)D\mathcal h_u,D\varphi\rangle\,{\rm d}x
\le
\int_{\mathbb R^n}
\varphi\langle Du,D\mu\rangle\,{\rm d}x.
$$
\indent
{\em Case \(n\ge3\)}. 
When $n\geq 3$ we essentially reproduce the arguments of \cite[Theorem 3.6]{haa20} and \cite[Proposition 4.2]{bi23}. 
Let \(q\in[2,\infty)\). Since \(\eta\) has compact support, we may
use
$
\varphi
=
u\mathcal h_u^{-1}\eta^q
$
as a test function in \eqref{mm.9} to get (recall (\ref{recalla}))
$$
\begin{aligned}
\int_{\mathbb R^n}\eta^q|Du|^2\,{\rm d}x
={}&
\int_{\mathbb R^n}
u\mathcal h_u^{-1}\eta^q
\langle Du,D\mathcal h_u\rangle\,{\rm d}x
-
q\int_{\mathbb R^n}
u\eta^{q-1}
\langle Du,D\mathcal h_u\rangle\,{\rm d}x
+
\int_{\mathbb R^n}
u\mathcal h_u^{-1}\eta^q\mu\,{\rm d}x.
\end{aligned}
$$
Since
$
0\le \eta/\mathcal h_u\le1
$
and, using \(\mathcal H(Du)Du=Du\) with the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality 
\eqn{cscs}
$$
|\langle Du,D\mathcal h_u\rangle|
=|\langle
\mathcal H(Du)Du,D\mathcal h_u
\rangle|\le
|Du|\langle\mathcal H(Du)D\mathcal h_u,D\mathcal h_u\rangle^{1/2},
$$
we infer that
\eqn{cz.111}
$$
\int_{\mathbb R^n}\eta^q|Du|^2\,{\rm d}x
\le
(q+1)
\int_{\mathbb R^n}
|u|\eta^{q-1}|Du|
\langle
\mathcal H(Du)D\mathcal h_u,D\mathcal h_u
\rangle^{1/2}\,{\rm d}x
+
\int_{\mathbb R^n}
|u|\eta^{q-1}|\mu|\,{\rm d}x.
$$
On the support of \(\eta\), we have
\(\mathcal h_u\ge\mathcal{h}_{\infty}\), and hence
\eqn{cz.delta}
$$
|Du|^2=1-\mathcal h_u^{-2}\ge1-\mathcal{h}_{\infty}^{-2}=\delta.
$$
By Young's inequality, \eqref{cz.111} yields
\eqn{cz.2}
$$
\begin{aligned}
\int_{\mathbb R^n}\eta^q|Du|^2\,{\rm d}x
\le{}&
c\|u\|_{L^\infty(\mathbb R^n)}^2
\int_{\mathbb R^n}
\eta^{q-2}\langle
\mathcal H(Du)D\mathcal h_u,D\mathcal h_u
\rangle\,{\rm d}x+\frac{c}{\delta}\|u\|_{L^\infty(\mathbb R^n)}^2\int_{\mathbb R^n}\eta^{q-2}\mu^2\,{\rm d}x.
\end{aligned}
$$
We next control the first integral on the right-hand side.
Taking
$
\varphi=\eta^{q-1}
$
in \eqref{cz.aux}, we obtain
$$
(q-1)
\int_{\mathbb R^n}\eta^{q-2}
\langle\mathcal H(Du)D\mathcal h_u,D\mathcal h_u\rangle\,{\rm d}x
\le\int_{\mathbb R^n}
\eta^{q-1}\langle Du,D\mu\rangle\,{\rm d}x.
$$
Integrating by parts and using
$
-\,{\rm div}\, (\mathcal h_uDu)=\mu,
$
we find
$$
\int_{\mathbb R^n}
\eta^{q-1}\langle Du,D\mu\rangle\,{\rm d}x
=
\int_{\mathbb R^n}
\mathcal h_u^{-1}\eta^{q-1}\mu^2\,{\rm d}x
+
\int_{\mathbb R^n}
\mu\eta^{q-2}
\left(
\mathcal h_u^{-1}\eta-(q-1)
\right)
\langle Du,D\mathcal h_u\rangle\,{\rm d}x.
$$
Again using (\ref{cscs}) and 
Young's inequality we obtain
\eqn{cz.3}
$$
\int_{\mathbb R^n}
\eta^{q-2}
\langle
\mathcal H(Du)D\mathcal h_u,D\mathcal h_u
\rangle\,{\rm d}x
\le
c
\int_{\mathbb R^n}
\eta^{q-2}\mu^2\,{\rm d}x.
$$
Combining \eqref{cz.2} and \eqref{cz.3}, and recalling that
\(\delta<1\), gives
$$
\int_{\mathbb R^n}
\eta^q|Du|^2\,{\rm d}x
\le
\frac{c}{\delta}
\|u\|_{L^\infty(\mathbb R^n)}^2
\int_{\mathbb R^n}\eta^{q-2}\mu^2\,{\rm d}x
$$
with $c\equiv c(q)$. 
By \eqref{cz.delta},
$$
\int_{\mathbb R^n}\eta^q\,{\rm d}x
\le
\frac{c}{\delta^2}
\|u\|_{L^\infty(\mathbb R^n)}^2
\int_{\mathbb R^n}\eta^{q-2}\mu^2\,{\rm d}x.
$$
H\"older's inequality yields (not needed when $q=2$)
$$
\int_{\mathbb R^n}\eta^{q-2}\mu^2\,{\rm d}x
\le
\left(
\int_{\mathbb R^n}\eta^q\,{\rm d}x
\right)^{\frac{q-2}{q}}
\left(
\int_{\mathbb R^n}|\mu|^q\,{\rm d}x
\right)^{\frac2q}.
$$
Consequently,
$$
\int_{\mathbb R^n}\eta^q\,{\rm d}x
\le
\frac{c(n,q)}
{\delta^q}
\|u\|_{L^\infty(\mathbb R^n)}^q
\int_{\mathbb R^n}|\mu|^q\,{\rm d}x
$$
for $c\equiv c(n,q)$. 
By Proposition \ref{boun.p},
$
\|u\|_{L^\infty(\mathbb R^n)}
\le
c\,\mathfrak{m}_\mu,
$
and hence \eqref{czcz} follows. 

{\em Case \(n=2\)}. 
Here we deal with the two dimensional case, which is not available in the literature; we have to compensate for the logarithmic growth of
\(u\). In the following we abbreviate 
$
L_A(x):=\log(A+|x|)$, where 
$A\ge e 
$
will be fixed below. 
We first derive a weighted estimate for \(D\mathcal h_u\). Taking 
$
\varphi=L_A^2\eta
$
in \eqref{cz.aux} we obtain
\eqn{cz.4}
$$
\begin{aligned}
I
:=
\int_{\mathbb R^2}
L_A^2
\langle
\mathcal H(Du)D\mathcal h_u,D\eta
\rangle\,{\rm d}x
& \le
-2
\int_{\mathbb R^2}
\eta\frac{L_A}{A+|x|}
\left\langle
\mathcal H(Du)D\mathcal h_u,
\frac{x}{|x|}
\right\rangle\,{\rm d}x\\
& \qquad 
+
\int_{\mathbb R^2}
L_A^2\eta
\langle Du,D\mu\rangle\,{\rm d}x.
\end{aligned}
$$
Note that $D\eta=\mathds{1}_{\eta>0}D\mathcal h_u$, thus $I\geq 0$. 
By the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality associated with
\(\mathcal H(Du)\) - recall also that $\langle
\mathcal H(Du)\xi, \xi \rangle \leq |\xi|^2$ - 
the first term on the right-hand side can be estimated by
$$
\begin{aligned}
2\left|
\int_{\mathbb R^2}
\eta\frac{L_A}{A+|x|}
\left\langle
\mathcal H(Du)D\mathcal h_u,
\frac{x}{|x|}
\right\rangle\,{\rm d}x
\right|
\le\frac18 I
+\frac{c}{A^2}
\int_{\mathbb R^2}\eta^2\,{\rm d}x.
\end{aligned}
$$
Set
$
g:=L_A^2\eta.
$
Using
$$
g\langle D\mu,Du\rangle
=
\left\langle
D\left(\frac{\mu g}{\mathcal h_u}\right),
\mathcal h_uDu
\right\rangle
-\mu\langle Dg,Du\rangle+
\frac{\mu g}{\mathcal h_u}
\langle D\mathcal h_u,Du\rangle,
$$
and integrating by parts, we obtain
$$
\begin{aligned}
\int_{\mathbb R^2}
g\langle Du,D\mu\rangle\,{\rm d}x
={}&
\int_{\mathbb R^2}
\frac{\mu^2g}{\mathcal h_u}\,{\rm d}x-
\int_{\mathbb R^2}
\mu L_A^2\langle Du,D\eta\rangle\,{\rm d}x
\\
&-
2\int_{\mathbb R^2}
\mu\eta\frac{L_A}{A+|x|}
\left\langle
Du,\frac{x}{|x|}
\right\rangle\,{\rm d}x
+
\int_{\mathbb R^2}
\frac{\mu g}{\mathcal h_u}
\langle D\mathcal h_u,Du\rangle\,{\rm d}x.
\end{aligned}
$$
Since \(D\eta=D\mathcal h_u\) a.e. on \(\{\eta>0\}\) and
$
\eta=\mathcal h_u-\mathcal{h}_{\infty}
$
there, the second and fourth terms combine into
$$
-\int_{\{\eta>0\}}
\mu L_A^2\frac{\mathcal{h}_{\infty}}{\mathcal h_u}
\langle D\mathcal h_u,Du\rangle\,{\rm d}x.
$$
Moreover,
$
\eta/\mathcal h_u\le1,
$
and
$
|\langle D\mathcal h_u,Du\rangle|
\le
|Du|
\langle
\mathcal H(Du)D\mathcal h_u,D\mathcal h_u
\rangle^{1/2}.
$
Therefore Young's inequality gives
$$
\int_{\mathbb R^2}
L_A^2\eta
\langle Du,D\mu\rangle\,{\rm d}x
\le
\frac18 I
+
\frac{c}{A^2}
\int_{\mathbb R^2}\eta^2\,{\rm d}x
+
c\int_{\mathbb R^2}\mu^2L_A^2\,{\rm d}x.
$$
Inserting this estimate into \eqref{cz.4} and absorbing the fractions
of \(I\), we obtain the weighted Caccioppoli type estimate 
\eqn{cz.5}
$$
I
=
\int_{\mathbb R^2}
L_A^2
\langle
\mathcal H(Du)D\mathcal h_u,D\eta
\rangle\,{\rm d}x
\le
\frac{c}{A^2}
\int_{\mathbb R^2}\eta^2\,{\rm d}x
+
c\int_{\mathbb R^2}\mu^2L_A^2\,{\rm d}x.
$$
We now test \eqref{mm.9} with
$
\varphi
=
u\mathcal h_u^{-1}\eta^2.
$
Arguing exactly as in \eqref{cz.111}, with \(q=2\), we obtain
$$
\int_{\mathbb R^2}|Du|^2\eta^2\,{\rm d}x
\le 3\int_{\mathbb R^2}
|u|\eta|Du|
\langle
\mathcal H(Du)D\mathcal h_u,D\mathcal h_u
\rangle^{1/2}\,{\rm d}x+
\int_{\mathbb R^2}|u|\eta|\mu|\,{\rm d}x.
$$
Using \eqref{linf}$_2$ and Young's inequality gives, for every
\(\sigma\in(0,1)\),
$$
\int_{\mathbb R^2}|Du|^2\eta^2\,{\rm d}x\leq 
\frac18
\int_{\mathbb R^2}|Du|^2\eta^2\,{\rm d}x
+c\mathfrak{m}_\mu^2 I+\sigma\int_{\mathbb R^2}\eta^2\,{\rm d}x+
\frac{c\mathfrak{m}_\mu^2}{\sigma}
\int_{\mathbb R^2}\mu^2L_A^2\,{\rm d}x.
$$
Using \eqref{cz.5}, we therefore find
\eqn{cz.6}
$$
\int_{\mathbb R^2}|Du|^2\eta^2\,{\rm d}x\le \frac18
\int_{\mathbb R^2}|Du|^2\eta^2\,{\rm d}x+
c\left(\frac{\mathfrak{m}_\mu^2}{A^2}+\sigma\right)\int_{\mathbb R^2}\eta^2\,{\rm d}x
+c\mathfrak{m}_\mu^2\left(1+\frac1{\sigma}\right)\int_{\mathbb R^2}\mu^2L_A^2\,{\rm d}x.
$$
By \eqref{cz.delta},
$$
\int_{\mathbb R^2}\eta^2\,{\rm d}x
\le\frac1{\delta}
\int_{\mathbb R^2}|Du|^2\eta^2\,{\rm d}x.
$$
Choose a sufficiently large dimensional constant \(c_0\ge1\), and set
$$
\sigma
:=\frac{\delta}{16c_0},
\qquad
A:=e+\frac{4\sqrt{c_0}\,\mathfrak{m}_\mu}{\sqrt{\delta}}.
$$
With \(c_0\) sufficiently large, the terms containing
\(\int|Du|^2\eta^2\) on the right-hand side of \eqref{cz.6} can be
absorbed into the left-hand side. We conclude that
$$
\int_{\mathbb R^2}|Du|^2\eta^2\,{\rm d}x
\le
c\,
\frac{\mathfrak{m}_\mu^2}{\delta}
\int_{\mathbb R^2}\mu^2L_A^2\,{\rm d}x.
$$
Using once more \eqref{cz.delta}, we obtain
$$
\int_{\mathbb R^2}\eta^2\,{\rm d}x
\le
c\,
\frac{\mathfrak{m}_\mu^2}{\delta^2}
\int_{\mathbb R^2}\mu^2L_A^2\,{\rm d}x.
$$
Finally, since \(A\ge e\),
$
L_A(x)=\log(A+|x|)\le\log A\log(e+|x|),
$
and our choice of \(A\) gives
$$
\log A
\le
c\log\left(
e+
\frac{\mathfrak{m}_\mu}{\sqrt{\delta}}
\right).
$$
Therefore
$$
\begin{aligned}
\int_{\mathbb R^2}
(\mathcal h_u-\mathcal{h}_{\infty})_+^2\,{\rm d}x
\le{}&
c\,\frac{\mathfrak{m}_\mu^2}{\delta^2}\log^2\left(e+\frac{\mathfrak{m}_\mu}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)
\int_{\mathbb R^2}
\mu^2\log^2(e+|x|)\,{\rm d}x.
\end{aligned}
$$
Recalling that
$
\delta=1-\mathcal{h}_{\infty}^{-2},
$
this is precisely \eqref{mm.20}. The proof is complete.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
